#220. Apple Creator Studio is getting its biggest update yet — how to preorder

Apple had more than a set of new Macs to show off on Tuesday. The same morning it introduced the 14-inch MacBook Pro with M6 and a new iMac, the company also announced a sizable update to Apple Creator Studio, the $12.99-a-month subscription that bundles Final Cut Pro, Logic Pro, Pixelmator Pro, Motion, Compressor, and MainStage with a slew of additional premium features in Keynote, Pages, and Numbers.

Apple calls it the biggest update since Creator Studio launched in January. In addition to adding the MotionVFX plug-in library directly into Final Cut Pro, it also brings additional stock video to Apple’s Content Hub, the Generate Fill tool to Pixelmator Pro, and puts the spotlight on AI assistants built on Apple models and OpenAI inside Logic Pro, Keynote, Pages, and Numbers.

The biggest addition to Apple Creator Studio that's worth upgrading for is MotionVFX. This third-party library of titles, transitions, graphics, and effects now comes built into Final Cut Pro and Motion. Final Cut Pro is also getting the Content Hub, with Apple’s stock video, photos, and backgrounds you can use to drop straight into a timeline.

Pixelmator Pro can also add in Generate Fill, which lets you circle part of an image, describe what you want changed, and have the app fill it in while matching textures, colors, and backgrounds around it.

Logic Pro is also getting a significant Liquid Glass redesign as well as a new assistant in beta that can start a song or set up a vocal track from a single prompt. There's a redesigned browser addition that will also keep loops, instruments, effects, and a Splice library together in one place, too.

Finally, Keynote, Pages, and Numbers both get an assistant that can "understand" the documents you’re working on, along with stock video in the Content Hub and new templates and themes to choose from.

You can save with an educator's discount on Apple Creator Studio. It's just $2.99 per month or $29.99 per year for students or teachers, but verification is required.

There's another way to try out all the new additions as well. If you buy a new Mac or a qualifying iPad from Apple or an authorized reseller, you get three months of Apple Creator Studio for free.

So if you're ready to give it a try, now's the time to prep for its November release, though there's no specific date to watch out for just yet.

Brittany is fueled by horror, rainbow-sugar-pixel-rushes, and video games. Until her dying breath she'll be wielding a BFG made entirely of killer drive and ambition. Check out her work at PfhorTheWin.com. Like a fabulous shooter once said, get psyched!

#219. What Germany thinks of carmaker Audi's $4 billion deal with Trump

The luxury carmaker is set to open its first factory in the United States — a move Trump credits to his tariffs. DW examines the German reaction amid a massive auto industry cull at home.

US President Donald Trump announced the investment on Saturday, during a campaign rally in Georgia, a key battleground state for his Republican Party in the midterm elections next week.

Audi quickly confirmed the plan, saying it would build its first US factory near Macon, Georgia, costing around $4 billion (€3.6 billion) and creating some 4,500 jobs.

The German automaker said construction would begin next year, with a planned annual production of up to 200,000 vehicles due to start in the early 2030s.

The firm plans to build premium SUVs for the US market at the plant, marking a "long-term commitment" to what it said was "one of the world's most important premium markets."

The US investment comes as German automakers face unprecedented pressure from China, rising costs and two conflicts.

Backed by huge government subsidies, Chinese rivals have stolen a march in the transition to electric vehicles (EVs), scaling up production of new models that are fast gaining market share in a crowded global auto market.

BMW, Mercedes-Benz and Audi's parent company, Volkswagen— the world's second-largest automaker — have all issued profit warnings in recent months as margins come under pressure.

They've also announced major cost-cutting. VW is preparing to axe up to 100,000 jobs by 2030, while Audi's factory in Neckarsulm, which has around 15,000 staff, is one of four plants at risk of closure.

Trump credited his 15% tariffs on European-made vehicles for Audi's investment, saying it "would not have happened without tariffs."

While the commitment does feed into Trump's plan to bring manufacturing back to the United States from abroad, Audi said it was making the huge outlay for growth and resilience.

“We have much greater ambitions for Audi in America," said the carmaker's CEO Gernot Döllner, who appeared alongside Trump at the Republican's campaign rally.

"The United States is one of the world's most important premium markets, and we have not yet realized our full potential. We are determined to change that ... This is about building a stronger Audi business in America — not only about building a factory," he said.

However, Trump's tariffs have undoubtedly added to the automaker's woes.

Audi, which currently exports to the US market from Europe and Mexico, said the additional costs amounted to about €1.2 billion ($1.3 billion) last year.

With more than 160,000 vehicles sold, Audi's US sales last year fell 16%, amid intense competition and after Trump scrapped subsidies on electric vehicles in its second-biggest market.

By building a US factory, Audi should be able to avoid or reduce Trump's tariffs, industry analysts say.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

There's been fierce criticism of the deal due to the severe pressure facing German automakers and because the new US investment could mean further job losses at home.

Much of the criticism focused on Döllner's rally appearance alongside Trump.

The center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD)'s transport policy spokesperson Isabel Cademartori described it as campaign assistance for an autocrat who is waging a trade war against Germany.

"This is a disgrace, especially considering the history of the Volkswagen Group," she stated, referring to the company's links with Adolf Hitler's Nazi party during World War II.

Christiane Benner, president of the powerful IG Metall union, labeled the rally appearance as "extremely embarrassing, politically unacceptable and not worthy of the company."

VW works council chief Daniela Cavallo went further, saying Döllner had let himself be turned "into a puppet in [Trump's] propaganda show."

Cavallo, however, noted that the US plant could make sense from a business perspective, as VW needs to grow its market share there.

An Audi spokesperson defended the US investment, saying it was "not a decision against Germany, but rather a step toward strengthening our global competitiveness."

Manfred Döss, chairman of Audi's supervisory board, insisted the automaker's future cannot be secured by cost-cutting and efficiency alone.

"We need the courage to invest, to seize new opportunities and to make decisions that will shape our company for decades to come," he added.

The announcement certainly appears timed to coincide with the US midterm elections on November 3.

Voters will decide whether the Republicans or Democrats hold the balance of power in Congress during Trump's last two years in office.

Holding the announcement in the key battleground state of Georgia is seen as a strategic move to boost the fortunes of local Republican candidates.

Since he returned to the White House last year, Trump has used tariffs and political pressure to force global automakers to relocate their factories to American soil.

Audi's investment follows moves by Stellantis and Japan's Toyota to expand US production, while Honda is in talks to build a new US plant.

US auto giants General Motors and Ford have also promised to increase domestic production.

While one investment announcement may not be enough to sway many voters, it serves one of Trump's main election promises — to revive industrial production in the US.

#218. American Airlines hikes annual fees on rewards cards for first time since 2018

American Airlines is raising the annual fee for two of its rewards credit cards for the first time since 2018 and adding new benefits like a TSA PreCheck credit and points from spending on hotels.

The fee on the Citi AAdvantage Platinum Select World Elite Mastercard will go up to $150 a year from $99. That is the first hike since 2018, when it raised the fee from $95 to $99, an airline spokeswoman said.

The Citi AAdvantage Business Mastercard will also have a fee increase and will now cost $150 a year.

Airlines and their bank partners have been steadily raising fees on rewards credit cards.

Those cards generate billions of dollars a year for airlines, which make money when miles are sold to banks, which then give them to customers. American's move, announced Tuesday, follows similar increases by rivals such as United Airlines.

Meanwhile, American Express last year raised the fee on its popular Platinum card, which comes with airport lounge access, to $895 a year from $695. JPMorgan Chase increased the annual fee on its competing card, the Sapphire Reserve, to $795 from $550.

The new perks for the American card include three times AAdvantage miles earnings on hotels booked through American's portal, a credit of up to $120 every four years for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck, and a $100 annual statement credit on in-flight purchases like food.

#216. How to Hide Your Baby From the Government

At first blush, Kristin LaHargoue’s social media resembles that of many thirtysomething moms espousing aspirational lifestyles. To her 88,000-plus followers on Instagram, LaHargoue—a crunchy blonde living in central Utah—routinely posts her children, her husband, and herself traipsing around the globe on her account @veda.revival. There they are, standing before the Sagrada Familia, striking silly poses on a busy Tokyo street, or dancing beachside in the Philippines. In an April 2026 post soundtracked to a version of Taylor Swift’s “The Fate of Ophelia,” LaHargoue wrote that she and her family have thus far traveled to over 40 countries.

Flying around the world at such a clip isn’t unusual for influencers. But LaHargoue says her children, although born in the US, travel without birth certificates or Social Security numbers. Her thinking is aligned with the sovereign citizen movement: an ever-expanding group of anti-government activists that, for almost half a century, have operated on the belief that they aren’t subject to US law. “Hot take: You don’t actually need a birth certificate to raise a thriving, traveling, fully functional child,” she writes in the same post. “Yeah … I said it. Because we are living proof of it.” By virtue of not registering her two young children’s births, she says her sons are “sovereign babies”—an oft-used hashtag, on accounts such as LaHargoue’s, to describe a child bearing alternative identification documents.

With her brand Veda Revival, LaHargoue offers a white-glove concierge service to new and expecting parents, rooted in the belief that obtaining requisite birth documents is tantamount to entering one’s children into “the jurisdiction of the US corporation.” A larger sovereign citizen movement has existed in the US for decades, with its practitioners similarly eschewing traditional government documents; acts of protest can also include tax evasion, driving without a license, and in the case of Terry Nichols—domestic terrorism. But the rise of influencers selling an alternative means of obtaining a passport, and the idea that babies can be sovereign, represents a new, digitally native outgrowth of this ideology.

For $497, LaHargoue sells a bundle that includes videos and templates for parents to obtain passports for their children based on “nationality” versus “citizenship,” with detailed instructions so they might respond confidently during “interactions with agents of the matrix.” Those seeking something more intensive can pay $997 for “hand-holding,” meaning that LaHargoue will personally guide them in navigating this divergent route. (LaHargoue declined WIRED’s request for an interview, citing the forthcoming birth of her third child.)

Five hauntings from the other side of technology.

Have a ghost story of your own? Share it in the comments below.

LaHargoue, who says she has worked with over 1,000 parents to obtain passports for their sovereign babies, is one of a growing cohort of influencers working in the unregulated industry that’s now selling this self-taught knowledge. One “family legacy architect” named Minister Dionne, known for broadcasting missives to her 125,000 Instagram followers about how registering one’s child makes them “wards of the state,” offers “Status Correction FastTrack Bundles” for securing noncitizen national passports (for as much as $555). LaHargoue also offers an affiliate program for those who want to make a commission of 15 to 20 percent by successfully selling her sovereign baby services.

It’s impossible to quantify how many sovereign babies currently live in the US, given their parents’ feelings about record-taking. (According to one oft-cited estimate, 100,000 to 300,000 people identify as sovereign citizens, although it's hard to know exactly because of the nature of the movement.) But Adam Bojak, a family law attorney in western New York who is currently on the ballot for the New York state legislature, says that he’s seen an uptick in cases involving sovereign children. In decades past, he’d have maybe one client per year who’d vacate or even try to reject formal identification documents for their children; lately, that’s increased to a half dozen a year, a phenomenon he suspects is linked to the “psychic break” that happened during Covid. “So many of these things are downstream from the aftereffects of that,” he says. “Just where people were ripped apart socially and physically from each other, emotionally. [It’s a rupture] that leads into this idea that everybody’s out for themselves.”

Remote learning during the pandemic also transformed education. Homeschooling rates surged in the early 2020s and remain high: The National Home Education Research Institute estimated earlier this year that 3.4 million children in the US are homeschooled—a number that is almost certainly underreported—compared to 1.4 million back in 2003. Tess Ulrey, executive director of the Coalition for Responsible Home Education, is unsurprised that the sovereign baby movement has risen in tandem with homeschooling rates, as homeschooling can become an inadvertent gateway into sovereign thinking. Some parents might pull their child out of school and “get tied up in this ideology of like, ‘Well, it is my right to do whatever I want. Well, why would the government be tracking that?’” she says.

Indeed, peppered between posts name-checking Rumi and denouncing circumcision, LaHargoue encourages parents to reject conventional education systems. In her July appearance on the Born Free Method podcast, LaHargoue spoke about her belief that the US school system is connected to a larger government plot that, by virtue of the birth certificate, “contracts” children as human capital. “That school system is now teaching them how to be a good worker,” she said. “Let’s shuffle them right down this path.”

A recent rise in “world schooling,” or rooting a child’s instruction in travel rather than curricula, is similarly linked with the sovereign baby movement. Jemila Moore, a doula based in South Carolina, lives nomadically with her partner and four young children. Her three youngest kids have neither birth certificates nor Social Security numbers. Moore, who swears by world schooling, wanted to ensure that her children had the ability to travel with passports. Wherever her family may be, Moore accompanies her children to local public libraries to read and do math exercises. “There's nothing greater than children being able to learn about other countries while actually witnessing those countries and their culture, and let those countries tell their history versus just reading it in a textbook,” she says. “I think that it really makes a difference on our development as people and how we view the world.”

While sovereign babies are bred on the grounds of government suspicion, sovereign-minded parents still “want to travel under the banner of the United States of America without actually being subject to the United States of America,” says Bojak. “Like George Costanza said, ‘We’re living in a society,’ and these are the barriers you have to clear to live in a society.” Rob Finch, a North Carolina–based consultant who trains law enforcement officers to engage with sovereign citizens who don’t believe they are subject to the law, says this ideology is "not fact-based," adding: “You've got a lot of opportunistic people selling these packages of ‘Keep your kid off the grid, here's how you do it.’”

Without clearing these barriers, experts worry that sovereign babies are far more likely to fall through societal cracks and live insular lives. Most will be unable to drive, find jobs, or even secure housing without the crucial documents one needs to move through the world.

Birth certificates and Social Security numbers represent a relatively new standard for identification in the US. During the colonial period, American churches primarily kept birth records. But when millions of Europeans immigrated to the States in the 19th century, reformers became concerned about rising poverty rates, overpopulation, and the spread of epidemics in densely populated urban areas. In a bid to better understand public health, the US Census Bureau developed standardized certificates and began tracking births, though not every state followed suit. The process became more nationally standardized around the 1930s, and firmly de rigueur during World War II, when some factories would only employ US citizens as part of the war effort.

To some sovereign citizens, it’s no coincidence that birth certificates became rote in the 1930s, the same decade FDR signed an executive order replacing gold as the backing standard for US currency. Proponents of the “straw man theory,” a central premise in sovereign ideology, hold that when parents take out a birth certificate and Social Security number for their child, the government then opens an account in their name and uses them, in the absence of gold, as human capital to back US debt. This bogus theory is often invoked by sovereign citizens who, say, get pulled over for driving without a license, as a way of justifying their actions: They believe that every individual has two separate personas—a legal entity that is bound by the government, and themselves—and that laws thus don’t apply to them as individuals. To date, no US court has ever sided with sovereign citizens invoking these arguments, says family law attorney Christopher C. Melcher: “There's no legal basis for what they're saying, but they say it with enough conviction and consistency that they hope they can get away with it.”

The modern sovereign citizens movement has its roots in a hate-fueled paramilitary cohort called Posse Comitatus (Latin for “power of the county”). In the 1970s, an ex-Army lieutenant colonel turned “minister” named William Potter Gale founded the group, hinged on racist and antisemetic beliefs, in Southern California. Gale believed in a “citizen’s government” that would exact justice in its own way; throughout the decade, his followers violently detained and assaulted an IRS worker and attempted to “indict” a judge. When an agricultural crisis gripped the US, his conspiracy-driven ideas spread throughout the country. Posse Comitatus’ anti-government tactics, which include not paying taxes, later spread into a larger sovereign citizens’ movement, which now has dozens of different factions and chapters in various states. One such group is the American State Nationals, who align with sovereign citizen ideology but distance themselves from the moniker because of the movement’s white supremacist origins. ASNs believe one can obtain a noncitizen national passport that, by virtue of having a certain number of stars on it, gives them limited diplomatic immunity. Some even sell bundles on social media that allow people, for several hundred dollars, to exit their “contract” with the US government through a passport based on nationality rather than citizenship.

Beyond a skepticism of the US government, the parents of sovereign babies are difficult to pin down. They typically do not align with any one political party or religious group. While some live more holistic and off-grid lifestyles, and an overlap does exist between sovereignty practitioners and ardent anti-vaxers, it wouldn’t be accurate to say that this is a strain of the Make America Healthy Again movement, either. Rather than having one centralized leader or a physical community, the sovereign baby phenomenon is more akin to a vast constellation, people connected by the belief that by applying for a birth certificate and Social Security number, they are “binding” their children with the “United States corporation,” as LaHargoue likes to put it. In a series of videos included in Veda Revival’s “Born Free Blueprint,” LaHargoue argues that sovereign babies are “ushering in a new era of freedom.” (These videos are free, but before purchasing and viewing LaHargoue’s more in-depth guides, all clients are required to upload a passport/ID and sign an agreement accepting the terms of entering her member community—which threatens legal action if one does not use her information with “integrity and benevolence.”)

The US Department of State requires evidence of citizenship before approving a passport. If one doesn’t have a primary document (such as a birth certificate), the agency accepts several forms of secondary evidence, including a delayed birth certificate and a Letter of No Record—which must be issued by the state and include public record documents such as a hospital birth certificate, baptism records, school records, or a census record. Those hewing to sovereign thinking tend to go with another option: a family Bible containing written inscriptions denoting the births and deaths of relatives. Melcher says it’s difficult, but not impossible, for those born on US soil to obtain secondary evidence that’s “sufficient to convince the State Department to issue a passport, based on them claiming that they were born in a state or territory, without a birth certificate.”

A woman living in Texas, whom I’ll call Deborah, purchased an instructional video from LaHargoue’s catalog and says she was able to obtain a passport for her infant—sans birth certificate—by submitting baptism records, a family Bible, an affidavit confirming that she had indeed given birth to him, and a formal announcement in a local newspaper announcing his birth. She notarized each of these documents and sent them certified through the mail. After an extensive back and forth, she said the office eventually approved the passport. “I opted for the passport route because that is a way of announcing, ‘Hey, I have this child,’” she says. “I'm claiming parental rights and backing them with the paperwork they'll need to make whatever choices they want in the future.”

In recent years, parents have stumbled into the sovereign baby movement through online rabbit holes, especially on Instagram, Telegram, Facebook, and Reddit. The confluence of factors that has given way to the sovereign baby movement—including the ubiquity of influencers, the isolating aftermath of Covid, skepticism of the medical establishment, and waning trust in the government—feels utterly of this time. Yet this phenomenon clashes against another tension: the Trump administration’s brutality toward immigrants and visible show of deporting people out of the US. At a moment when undocumented immigrants, green card holders, asylum seekers, and citizens are being shuttled to remote countries with little legal recourse, it’s striking that parents of sovereign children—of babies born in the US—have consciously stacked the odds against themselves and their kids.

“We shouldn't discount the fact that there's a guy in the Oval Office now, for the second time, who doesn't believe any rules pertain to him, and he's actually proven that over and over again that he doesn't have to follow laws,” Bojak, the attorney, says. “He doesn't have to follow court orders. And so if he doesn't, why the hell should any of the rest of us?”

Others arrive in other ways entirely: Deborah learned about sovereign citizen ideology—and later declined to get her toddler a birth certificate—after she’d accrued over $2,000 in speeding tickets. (She rejects the term "sovereign citizen” and claims it’s a slur; instead, she prefers to say she is "maximizing potential freedom” for her child.) “I ended up doing this deep dive from just having, honestly, a totally delusional idea of, ‘I'm not gonna pay these,’” she says. Some, like Zoe Durant, got here through in-person conversations with a doula, a midwife, or another professional birth attendant who usually helps new parents complete standard paperwork.

In 2016, while studying engineering in college, Durant was working as a bottle server and promotional model in Nevada. Several months after giving birth to her first child that same year, Durant and her partner traded the neon gleam of casinos for an offgrid solar farm mere miles away from Kilauea, one of the world’s most active volcanoes. She’s a “first-generation farmer” of grapefruits, bananas, lilikoi, and lychee crops in the remote Hawaiian jungle, posting dispatches on Instagram under the name @junglefairyfarm. (They have since moved to a less harsh location).

Early into building their Big Island home, in 2021, Durant found out she was pregnant with her second child. When she learned that her partner couldn’t accompany her to requisite appointments because of Covid, Durant eschewed a hospital birth altogether. Instead, she found a midwife who helped deliver her daughter in a birthing pool at home. When it was time to consider her newborn’s birth certificate and Social Security number application, Durant consulted her midwife. “She was like, ‘Well, there's families that I work with who don't do that,’” Durant says. “And I was like, ‘Oh, tell me more.’”

The midwife, Durant explained, told her that she’d helped several parents who identified as sovereign. Intrigued, and recalling how she’d felt pushed to sign these papers after her first child’s hospital birth, Durant chewed over the alternative the midwife had told her about. “It seems like something that [my daughter] could do later on if she wanted to consent to that, and it also helps protect her identity,” Durant reasoned. “In the future, she's not going to worry about someone stealing her Social Security number. And honestly, the way the economy is, I don't even know if Social Security is going to be around when she's old enough.”

But Durant worried that without documents like a birth certificate and a Social Security card, her child might run into hurdles if she wished to live a normal life. Durant instead applied for a passport for her daughter, submitting various secondary documents. At one point, Durant says, a passport agent told her that the office couldn’t grant her the document because she didn’t have hard proof of her child’s birth. Durant then sent the official a photo of herself sitting in the shallow birthing pool with her newborn, telling them, “This is the only evidence I have of the birth—because it was a sovereign birth.” These are “my beliefs,” she added, “sorry if you don’t like it.” She says the birth photo worked, and the passport office eventually sent the document along; all told, it took about six months.

Last year, Durant wrote and self-published a book, Opting Out: A Parent's Guide to a Barcode-Free Birth and Keeping Your Child Off the Grid: How to Protect Your Child's Identity and Secure Their Future Outside the State’s System. At a little under $20 per copy, it’s not the main source of the content creator/model’s income—she’s sold a few hundred copies thus far, she estimates—but Durant says that’s secondary to how “passionate” she feels about the subject. “I think a lot of parents are just trying to take back power because the government is getting a little bit overzealous with their control,” she says. One chapter of Durant’s book is dedicated solely to dealing with Child Protective Services, should the agency inquire about why one’s child does not have a birth certificate. In it, she writes: “Take a deep breath. Stand your ground. You have done your research. They were not taking your child. In that moment you realized something. The birth certificate was not just paperwork. It is permission, permission for the government to track you. To control you and intervene in your family's life whenever they see fit.”

Durant’s beliefs diverge from the mainstream sovereign baby movement, which is intertwined with free birthing—a separate, largely influencer-led movement that advocates for giving birth without medical intervention. In 2024, 1.5 percent of births in the US were free births, a number that tripled from 2004. (Durant had assistance during her home birth and believes in seeking out traditional medicine for “life-saving care”; she has been undergoing chemotherapy for a stage IV colon cancer diagnosis.) Free birthers can also opt out of prenatal care altogether, in what’s known as a “wild pregnancy.” LaHargoue, a vocal proponent of the Free Birth movement, has posted about her appearance on the Free Birth Society Podcast alongside Reels about traveling without these documents.

Marisse Vista, 34, first learned about sovereignty during the pandemic, when she was newly single and pregnant. She felt alone, save for an online “village of women” whom Vista credits with helping her “unearth a lot of things that didn't align with what I had known to be true.” After one woman told her that she believed “the birth certificate was a [government] bond,” Vista began questioning other “mainstream things,” like vaccinating her children. When Vista became pregnant with her second child, she decided to free-birth at an Airbnb after “binging” the FBS podcasts. After she decided not to obtain traditional birth documents for her infant, she started poking around online to find alternate routes to getting a passport.

By that point, Vista, a first-generation Filipina who grew up in Spring, Texas, had launched a Facebook community for like-minded sovereign mothers. When Vista mentioned in the group that she’d been trying to get her head around the alternative passport process, someone brought up Veda’s name. Vista bought LaHargoue’s step-by-step course to ensure that she had the requisite documents before going to the passport office in Waco. LaHargoue became her “echo chamber to make sure that I was doing it like fully right, because I had done all this research alone,” Vista says. “It was just a confirmation that, like, all my research was correct.”

When she became pregnant with her third child, Vista had “a pain-free, orgasmic, Frank breech free birth,” meaning that the baby emerges from the birth canal buttocks-first. The experience led Vista to help people conceive their “spirit babies” and birth them as sovereign parents. Under the banner of her brand La Souveraine Mere, her services range from a digital guide to “understanding how spirit babies communicate, what they need energetically before they can land, and how to align your womb space with their frequency” ($44) to a “Sovereign Conception Program” that includes “Spinal Energetics, frequency work, ancestral clearing, and womb activation woven into a container that treats your body as the oracle it already is” ($5,555).

Once these expectant parents get into Vista’s “world,” as she describes, “then I start to talk about, like, ‘Once you have the baby, what are you going to do? You're going to steward them. You're going to make sure that … their custodian isn't the government,’ and so we then talk about birth certificates and Social Security numbers.”

A bone-deep distrust of the medical establishment—and, in some cases, traumatic experiences—is another common gateway into free-birthing and, then, the sovereign baby movement. Moore, the former nursing assistant who studied public health in college, says she wasn’t expecting to feel “very pressured” to get an induction until she went into labor at a hospital. “When I was like, ‘Hey, since I'm already contracting, can we not do the induction? Can I just let my body go naturally?’” she says. “They're like, ‘Well, the doctor put the order in. We're just gonna do it.’” In hindsight, Moore wishes she had "advocated for herself more” in that moment. “My body was probably already doing what was necessary for me, and the induction just made my labor process a little bit more challenging than what it is actually with the natural flow.” Moore says it caused her to reflect on the disparities that women of color face when delivering children, and later informed her decision to free-birth her other kids.

There is an indisputable maternal health crisis in this country, and especially among women of color. But experts warn that free-birthing carries a heightened risk of complications, and the practice has come under intense scrutiny after infants and parents alike have died from the complications of unassisted birth. Sovereign baby influencers may risk further sowing distrust by advocating for risky, radical procedures without any prenatal care or medical professionals—particularly to those who might already be more vulnerable—by alleging that physicians are cogs in a bigger birth-certificate conspiracy. “They’ve tricked us into hiring licensed providers,” LaHargoue says in a video titled “Who’s Child Is It Really? The Truth About Birth Certificates & Legal Fiction.” “They’ve tricked us into believing that birth is even a medical event.”

“I’ve been saving this cigarette,” Sarah Ashley Townsend tells me over Zoom one June afternoon. She’s sitting in her home in Maryland, near the Susquehanna River. The windows are open. “I think that’s the hardest part to talk about,” the 43-year-old says, lighting the end of the smoke. Townsend is referring to the moment she became aware that she’d been raised differently.

Shortly before her birth, Townsend’s mother tumbled down the sovereign-ideology-aligned rabbit hole via a neighbor, who convinced her to not give birth at a hospital and to refuse paperwork. “I was not given a Social Security number, because she was going to do this all outside the boundaries of society now,” Townsend says. Vaccines were verboten, and she and her siblings lived consciously isolated from others in their community.

When her father’s family intervened and lobbied to send Townsend to school, the 5-year-old walked into kindergarten “thinking that I started school with everyone else, and I did not,” she says, her voice breaking. “I thought it was the first day of school, and apparently it was the last month of school. They made fun of me because I couldn't tie my shoes … and they all asked if I was from another planet.” Townsend’s mother would pack her inappropriate lunches or fight over signing papers that would have allowed her child to buy a meal: “She didn’t want me to have their tainted food,” she says. “She would blame us for subscribing to their system, because she didn't think we should have to be there anyway.”

Townsend considers herself fortunate that her father, while distant, advocated for her. One day he showed up at Townsend’s elementary school, and the pair walked to a medical clinic; he quietly ensured that she received vaccinations without her mother knowing. After her parents divorced, Townsend’s mother moved her and her sister around constantly, choosing off-grid places where they sometimes lived without power for months. Townsend’s father would ship her boxes with plushies and gift cards hidden inside, which she would use to buy clothes. Her dad would tuck novels into these packages with words underlined, a secret code telling her she was loved, and that she needed to stay strong.

When Townsend was 16, she tried to apply for a driver’s license and was repeatedly denied on account of her inconsistent birth and medical history. She approached her music and theater teachers for help: “How do I be an adult … how do I go do things I'm supposed to do?” she remembers asking them.

She believed she had her documents straightened out by the time she turned 18. But Townsend kept running into hurdles when she tried to join her husband’s health insurance shortly afterward. Giving birth a year later became its own kind of hell. “Do you know what happened when I gave birth to my son? All of his paperwork got kicked back,” she says, because the office said it couldn’t find evidence of her own birth. “My son's birth certificate had to be amended twice. I'm a week postpartum, 22 years old, running all over town with my husband getting notarized documents,” she says. “All I want is for my kid to have … of all the things in the world, a Social Security number and a birth certificate from the get-go.”

More than two decades on, Townsend is still haunted because she lacked a birth certificate and a Social Security number earlier in life. “As an adult, you can't have a fucking normal existence, ever, because you always have to go through a routine that will remind you that no one cared, and that you were some pawn in a weird fucking game that will affect you for the rest of your life,” she says. “And like, yes, I'm a valid citizen. I live here. I am a person. I exist.”

#214. Kyiv hopes for ‘new momentum’ in peace talks after Finnish and Norwegian leaders’ call with Putin – as it happened

Alexander Stubb and Jonas Gahr Støre held ‘substantive’ talk with Russian president on peace negotiations, but faced sceptical reactions from some European leaders

Ukraine’s foreign minister Andrii Sybiha has confirmed the Finnish and Norwegian call with Putin was coordinated with Ukraine, saying it was “the right approach” to any engagement with Putin.

Speaking alongside Finnish and Norwegian foreign ministers in Helsinki, Sybiha said:

“We welcome such active support for our diplomatic efforts and peace process. This call was, as it was mentioned, coordinated with us, and this is the right approach. … I will say this once again, Ukraine wants to end this war. We have doable proposals and solutions. We are ready for partial ceasefires and a full end to hostilities. Of course, everything needs to be fair. We will not take any one-sided steps.”

Sybiha said that Ukraine was looking for “a new momentum” in peace talks with Russia, and to “define new role for Europe in our peace efforts.”

“So president Zelenskyy shared with his vision which signals our friends should convey directly to the Putin, and these signals were conveyed. Ceasefire … and another signal meeting with Putin. We are ready to meet with him. … Probably the only way to get …. new quality in our talks is a meeting at the level of president Zelenskyy and Putin.”

He later said that the negotiators can only discuss things up to a certain level, but key decisions can only be made by the heads of states.

He also added that Kyiv was “ready for the trilateral meeting” in the UAE next month, and “we will use every opportunity to bring peace closer.”

He also welcomed the limited ceasefire in Oleshky, saying it could “pave the way for other steps in the peace process as a confidence-building measure.”

But conceded that “the reality on the ground in Ukraine remains very tough” as a result of relentless Russian strikes.

And, on that note, we will be closing the blog for today.

Finland’s influential president Alexander Stubb and Norway’s prime minister Jonas Gahr Støre held a 90-minute phone call with Russia’s Vladimir Putin as they continue attempts to break the deadlock in peace talks between Russia and Ukraine (10:41).

Finland’s Stubb welcomed the “frank and substantive” phone call, insisting that “ending the war requires direct discussions” with Russia.

Støre insisted “all our partners and allies were informed in advance” (13:43), despite scepticism or outright criticism from the Danish, Estonian, Polish and Swedish prime ministers (11:26, 14:03, 15:01, 15:53).

But he conceded that “there remain significant differences in our respective positions.”

Ukraine’s Volodymyr Zelenskyy called for “real de-escalatory steps” in the conflict after his debrief with the two Nordic leaders (11:55).

Ukraine’s foreign minister Andrii Sybiha confirmed the call was “coordinated” with Ukraine and said he hoped for “a new momentum” on peace talks with Russia (16:27).

But the Kremlin readout revealed that Russia’s Vladimir Putin blamed the EU and Nato for “confrontational policies” and demanded “full and unconditional recognition of the legitimate interests of the Russian Federation” (11:53).

The phone call took place just hours after a localised ceasefire kicked in the Ukrainian city of Oreshky after intense calls for humanitarian aid to be made available to the city’s residents (9:55, 17:08).

Wes Streeting, the UK defence secretary, has warned that Russia’s Vladimir Putin is “testing us and testing Nato’s resolve” as he condemned hybrid threats and “state-sponsored terrorism” from Russia (12:05).

Romania has summoned the Russian ambassador today, blaming Moscow for a recent fire at the state-owned Carfil defence plant in Brașov (16:10).

The Netherlands has dropped (again) from the 2027 Eurovision song contest amid “sensitivities” over Israel’s participation in the competition (12:35).

I am also on Bluesky at @jakubkrupa.bsky.social and on X at @jakubkrupa.

The US first lady, Melania Trump, has issued a brief statement confirming the humanitarian ceasefire in Oleshky, Ukraine, which she was involved in organising.

In a statement, she said “timing was accelerated two days ahead of schedule to protect the safety of civilians and to facilitate the delivery of critical humanitarian aid.”

She also added that “the ceasefire’s geographic area has expanded beyond Oleshky, within the Kherson Region, to provide safe passage for the humanitarian convoys.”

Ukraine’s Sybiha has also pushed back against suggestions that it’s not a good moment for having a call with Putin.

“If you are at war, every decision, every step which support peace efforts is always timely. So that’s why we welcome such type of steps, efforts to speed up our negotiations.”

He also added that the European channel should not be seen as “the alternative” for the US-led talks, but “a supplementary track.”

Ukraine’s foreign minister Andrii Sybiha has confirmed the Finnish and Norwegian call with Putin was coordinated with Ukraine, saying it was “the right approach” to any engagement with Putin.

Speaking alongside Finnish and Norwegian foreign ministers in Helsinki, Sybiha said:

“We welcome such active support for our diplomatic efforts and peace process. This call was, as it was mentioned, coordinated with us, and this is the right approach. … I will say this once again, Ukraine wants to end this war. We have doable proposals and solutions. We are ready for partial ceasefires and a full end to hostilities. Of course, everything needs to be fair. We will not take any one-sided steps.”

Sybiha said that Ukraine was looking for “a new momentum” in peace talks with Russia, and to “define new role for Europe in our peace efforts.”

“So president Zelenskyy shared with his vision which signals our friends should convey directly to the Putin, and these signals were conveyed. Ceasefire … and another signal meeting with Putin. We are ready to meet with him. … Probably the only way to get …. new quality in our talks is a meeting at the level of president Zelenskyy and Putin.”

He later said that the negotiators can only discuss things up to a certain level, but key decisions can only be made by the heads of states.

He also added that Kyiv was “ready for the trilateral meeting” in the UAE next month, and “we will use every opportunity to bring peace closer.”

He also welcomed the limited ceasefire in Oleshky, saying it could “pave the way for other steps in the peace process as a confidence-building measure.”

But conceded that “the reality on the ground in Ukraine remains very tough” as a result of relentless Russian strikes.

Romania has summoned the Russian ambassador today, blaming Moscow for a fire at the state-owned Carfil defence plant in Brașov.

The fire damaged the premises of several commercial entities. Three people are being investigated for their involvement in the incident.

The Romanian security intelligence SRI confirmed yesterday that it had “identified a coordinated sabotage network from the Russian Federation” to be behind the arson attack, with “other attempts on targets in Romania. including international military facilities or those used for training the Ukrainian armed forces.”

Foreign minister Dan Neculăescu said in a post on X:

“MFA Romania summoned the Russian Ambassador today to convey a firm protest over the September 14th sabotage in Brașov. Evidence proves Russia’s responsibility for this unacceptable hybrid attack.

We also condemned Russia’s systematic strikes on commercial vessels in the Black Sea, a deliberate escalation violating international law and freedom of navigation, with global consequences.”

Earlier today, Romania’s president, Nicușor Dan, condemned the incident, calling it “a grave violation” of Romania’s sovereignty.

“Let it be clear: these are not isolated, unrelated incidents; rather, they are part of a premeditated and coordinated campaign aimed at sowing division within our societies and undermining the commitment of European states to their own security and to collective defence.

We must not delude ourselves regarding the nature of this threat. This hybrid war has been ongoing for years, manifesting in various forms, all with the same objective: to weaken European states from within and erode the solidarity among them.”

Estonia’s foreign minister, Margus Tsahkna, does not seem to be convinced by the idea of talking to Putin either.

“Only increased pressure on Russia will make it take peace negotiations seriously.

Every contact with Russia must convey Europe’s unity and determination to support Ukraine and maintain pressure on Russia, while making clear that Europe will not negotiate away the fundamental principles underpinning its security.

Ukraine wants peace, and Estonia supports all efforts to achieve it. But there is no indication that Russia has changed its strategic objectives or is genuinely interested in negotiations.

Russia’s goal is not merely to seize parts of Ukraine, but to subjugate the entire country and undermine Europe’s security order, founded on NATO and the EU.

A just and lasting peace requires increased pressure on Russia, tighter sanctions, action against sanctions evasion, Russia’s international isolation and unwavering support for Ukraine. We must show Russia through concrete action that, as long as its war continues, pressure will remain strong and Ukraine’s supporters will not abandon it.”

Sweden’s prime minister, Ulf Kristersson, has insisted that any peace “must be achieved on Ukraine’s terms,” joining a growing chorus of sceptical voices after an earlier call between Finland and Norway’s leaders and Russia’s Vladimir Putin.

Talking to reporters, Kristersson said that any peace settlement “must be brought about through strength, and not through appeasement towards Russia.”

“If talks lead to that, then talks are good. But my experience so far – based on the reports we received from German and French attempts at dialogue [with Moscow] – is that the results have been very, very modest.”

Kristersson added that given Russia’s continued aggression against Ukraine, “I see nothing to suggest that they are seeking peace.”

“But we will surely receive an update on those discussions later today, and I am interested in that,” he said.

German chancellor Friedrich Merz said on Tuesday that attempts by ⁠the US and Russia to end the war in Ukraine “can never find solutions without Ukraine” ⁠and said any ⁠talks ​must also include European countries.

Merz was speaking at the Yusof Ishak Institute in Singapore as ⁠he described diplomatic efforts to build a negotiating format with Moscow.

He said the end of ⁠the war depended “only on one person” and framed continued backing ​for Kyiv as a ‌defence of Europe’s political ‌order.

He said “as long as he [Putin] does not want ‌to end the war, we will support Ukraine” and that Europe was not just defending Ukraine’s territorial integrity but also “the entire political order of the European continent”.

Merz added that Russia, Ukraine, the United States and European countries ‌should be at the negotiating table and warned against a scenario where “Russia, perhaps China and the ​United States of America” end the war without Ukraine.

Poland’s prime minister Donald Tusk has also responded to Stubb and Støre’s engagement with Putin, giving a detailed account on the European consultations ahead of and after the call.

Speaking before the cabinet meeting in Warsaw, Tusk said the call followed “consultations with me, among others, but primarily with president Zelenskyy and the US side … that it would be good for European politicians to have a working contact given the planned ceasefire talks that may take place in Abu Dhabi.”

He said the UAE talks between Russia, Ukraine, and the US are provisionally planned for 4 November.

Tusk confirmed he had been briefed on plans for the call beforehand, with Norway’s Støre assuring him that he and Stubb were ready to take political responsibility for this rare engagement with Putin, and would stick to the agreed position of the coalition of the willing.

He said Finland’s Stubb then briefed him after the call, saying “both sides maintained their positions,” and that “the European side’s impression during the call was one of very restrained optimism” about the chances of making progress in Abu Dhabi.

Tusk said he was “very cautions” about engaging with Russia and urged Stubb and Store to be “vigilant” in their talks with Putin, as “Russia’s agenda and interests are quite precisely defined, and that one should harbour any illusions about getting an agreement.”

He said Stubb and Støre also told Putin that the security of Nato’s northern and eastern flanks were “non-negotiable”.

Poland’s PM also stressed that despite the resumed dialogue, “nothing changes” in Europe’s “full support for Ukraine,” or collective views on “the [broader] security architecture and security guarantees for Ukraine.”

“We should not have any illussions; it is clear what the Russian side is playing at, but I will remain in constant contact with all those who possess the good will and the belief that an agreement is possible – or at the very least, a ceasefire before winter.”

Without specifying the source of his claim, Tusk said that “three, four nights ago,” “Russia lost 2,500 men” during one night. “This gives us an idea of ​​just how dire the further consequences of this war could be.”

Norway’s Jonas Gahr Støre has offered a bit more colour on his and Stubb’s call with Russia’s Putin in a huddle with journalists in Helsinki just now.

He and Stubb had been initially given only 20 minutes to talk to Putin, but ended up speaking for 90 minutes, in a conversation he described as “substantive, focused on the issues at hand, and professional.”

“Much of what he said was already known – he [Putin] recalled his views on how the conflict evolved since 2014 – and we stated our positions. These are well-known views, which we were able to acknowledge and respond to,” he said.

The talks touched on what can be done to tackle most pressing issues, including attacks on energy infrastructure and in the Black Sea.

But Støre conceded “there remain significant differences in our respective positions,” reiterating Norway’s continued support for Ukraine.

“We stand with Ukraine, defend its right to self-defence, and emphasise the need for a diplomatic path to end this war in a lasting and just manner,” he said.

Støre also stressed that he and Finland’s Stubb spoke on behalf of their two countries only, “and no one else.”

Responding to criticism of their call from Denmark’s Frederiksen (11:26), he said that “all our partners and allies were informed in advance and received a detailed account afterwards.”

“If one assumes that the current situation – where communication from the American administration to Moscow serves as the sole channel for discussing Ukraine outside of Ukraine – is the only way, then one is entitled to that view. I believe it is important that other voices are heard as well.”

Støre also stressed that the importance of the meeting in Abu Dhabi, first agreed as a result of the Oslo consultations that he and Stubb hosted.

In a brief section of the briefing in English, Støre again focused on pushing back against Frederiksen’s criticism:

“I believe that talking, having the right messages, is not wrong. Today these messages [are] coming out of the American administration; that is the only thing reaching Moscow. So [now] there are [also] voices from two neighbouring states, speaking on behalf of Finland and Norway, not speaking on behalf of others, but … in full compliance with the coalition of the willing who share view on the nature of this war and how it has to end.”

Russia is exploring the possibility ⁠of ⁠cooperation with ​partners from the ⁠United States in the resource-rich ⁠Arctic, president ​Vladimir ‌Putin ‌told a conference ‌of gas-exporting countries in Moscow.

Putin did not specify ‌whether the joint projects under consideration ​would involve extracting natural gas ⁠or other resources.

Three tankers carrying Russian diesel are en route to the United States, with a fourth expected to depart Wednesday, Moscow said, part of a deal that triggered a fierce Ukrainian and European backlash.

US president Donald Trump this month struck an agreement with Russia’s president Vladimir Putin to ease sanctions in exchange for Moscow releasing diesel onto global markets.

The measure was seen as an attempt to lower fuel prices for American consumers ahead of the US midterm legislative elections on 3 November and drew sharp criticism from Ukraine and its allies.

“At present, three tankers” with Russian fuel are on their way to the United States, Russian deputy prime minister Alexander Novak told reporters on Tuesday. A fourth was expected to set off on Wednesday, he added.

The first US-bound tanker with Russian diesel departed last week. According to data from the maritime tracking firm Kpler, it loaded nearly 300,000 barrels of diesel at the Primorsk terminal and was headed to New York.

In other news, the Netherlands has dropped (again) from the 2027 Eurovision song contest as public broadcaster NPO reversed its plans to pick up the baton after another broadcaster pulled out in August.

The withdrawal is linked with “sensitivities” over Israel’s participation in the competition, NPO’s media director Jurre Bosman said, acknowledging the issue continues to “elicit various reactions in society and causes discussion and division.”

“Therefore, after consultation with the Board of Broadcasters, the NPO has decided to forgo participation in 2027,” he added.

This is the second time a Dutch broadcaster has dropped out of the competition: in August, the Avrotros, which usually selects the Dutch entry and broadcasts the show, had pulled out, paving the way for NPO to step in.

Ireland and Spain have also declared they will not take part in the 2027 competiton because of Israel’s involvement, and the Czech Republic pulled out over financial constraints. Iceland and Slovenia also didn’t take part.

The 2027 Eurovision song contest will take place in Bulgaria’s Black Sea city of Burgas, after singer Darina Yotova, performing as Dara, won this year’s contest in Austria with “Bangaranga”.

#213. Opposition leader María Machado returns from exile to a Venezuela reshaped by Trump

Watch: Crowds welcome Venezuela's MarÃ­a Corina Machado after months in exile

Driving down roads lined with jubilant supporters from the western state of Lara to the capital Caracas, MarÃ­a Corina Machado's return to Venezuela could hardly have been more different to her exit 10 months earlier.

Colourful, loud, open and defiant, it stood in stark contrast to the way the Venezuelan opposition leader slipped out of the country in the dead of night in December last year to receive the Nobel Peace Prize in Oslo.

She had been unable to make it back to her homeland until now, thwarted on several occasions by the Venezuelan government - and by the Trump administration, which insisted the "time wasn't right" for her return.

"MarÃ­a Corina is the indisputable leader of Venezuela," says 20-year-old Gregory Noriega, wearing a sweatshirt emblazoned with the logo of the opposition movement Vente Venezuela. "Her return represents hope."

"This is a step forward in the electoral process which we've been waiting for since last year," echoes 18-year-old Hilary, who turned out onto the streets of the capital hoping to catch a glimpse of the opposition leader on her victory lap.

"She's returned after the double earthquakes when Venezuela needs her," adds her friend, Manuel. "The whole country is behind her to show that now Venezuela isn't scared."

Much has changed since Machado left Venezuelan soil to receive the Nobel Peace Prize.

Then-President NicolÃ¡s Maduro is no longer at the top of the Venezuelan government.

Maduro was forcibly removed from power by US troops on 3 January, and whisked to New York where he faces drugs, weapons and torture charges â to which he's pleaded not guilty.

He was replaced by his former vice-president, Delcy RodrÃ­guez, who has turned the page with Washington in ways few could have imagined.

The bilateral relationship is today unrecognisable from the years of mutual animosity during the reigns of President Hugo ChÃ¡vez and then his handpicked successor, Maduro.

The new circumstances throw up some interesting conundrums for both sides.

When Machado left Venezuela, she had long been considered Washington's favoured alternative to Maduro. She returns to find the Trump administration perfectly happy with the one they've got â at least for the time being.

The Trump administration recently signed a major oil deal with Venezuela which gave the US a 100-year concession over a staggering 65bn barrels of Venezuelan crude oil.

The deal was reached in conjunction with a Venezuelan private company called North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP) run by a businessman with a chequered past, Alejandro Betancourt.

Machado has openly criticised the deal which the RodrÃ­guez government reached with the US, albeit careful to direct her criticism at Caracas and at Betancourt rather than President Trump, to whom she gave her peace prize and whose trust and support she craves to move forward.

President Trump has repeatedly said how satisfied he is with Delcy RodrÃ­guez, calling her "wonderful" and "a terrific person". This was the vice-president of an administration he previously described as made up of "narco-communists".

But the outpouring of jubilation for Machado's caravan into the capital shows she can still count on significant support on the streets, says Venezuelan political analyst Beningno AlarcÃ³n.

"The return of MarÃ­a Corina Machado, in my personal view, is the most significant [political] event since 3rd January," says AlarcÃ³n. "It means the most important political leader in Venezuela is back in the country."

"The reception for her return shows that her ability to mobilise her support remains intact, which means the opposition in Venezuela now has something very solid: a leadership which is present on the ground as opposed to solely in exile," he says.

AlarcÃ³n provides some evidence that the opposition â and the public at large â feel more emboldened since 3 January, a situation he believes will become only more pronounced with Machado's reappearance.

For instance, a joint study by the political think tanks Polis and Nexo suggests that Venezuela saw three times as many street protests in the first nine months of this year as in all of 2025, with demonstrations spanning a range of issues, from politics and working conditions to water supply.

Furthermore, there's evidence that the repression of such protests has reduced by more than half.

In such an environment, the return of a figurehead like Machado can galvanise an electorate that has spent years fearful of the consequences of speaking out.

Her return also needs to be seen within the context of the wider talks brokered by Washington between the Venezuelan government and the opposition towards a negotiated political solution.

Certainly, the US Secretary of State, Marco Rubio, was quick to say her "safe return" to Venezuela was "the direct result of the US-supported reconciliation talks".

The Nobel Peace Prize winner still faces charges in Venezuela ranging from treason to conspiracy of insurrection. For years, she and her supporters have dismissed the charges as politically motivated and baseless.

Young supporters of MarÃ­a Corina Machado

Having made at least eight attempts to return to Venezuela by air and by sea before the Trump administration gave her the green light, Machado now knows she has Trump's blessing to be back on home soil â a position Delcy RodrÃ­guez is both unwilling and unlikely to challenge.

She called Machado's return a "show" and the entire cross-country event, which was livestreamed by her supporters over the weekend, was studiously ignored by state television outlets.

But RodrÃ­guez, too, needs a harmonious relationship with Washington if she is to avoid a fate similar to that of her predecessor and indicated she might be prepared to hold talks with Machado.

For as much as Trump has complimented RodrÃ­guez, he has also made it clear that Washington expects nothing less than her full submission and malleability on key issues like energy.

Some reports have even suggested all major decision-making in the oil-rich nation is being largely dictated by Secretary Rubio via regular WhatsApp calls to the interim president.

The young supporters gathered in droves in Caracas to cheer MarÃ­a Corina Machado home say their fears of criticising their government or demanding change are vanishing.

Her return marked an irreversible political transition in Venezuela, they insist, one which they could barely fathom when she left for Oslo just a year ago.

#212. The 11 best portable Bluetooth speakers that we use and love

A Bluetooth speaker is one of those devices that, once you find one you like, you won't be able to imagine how you lived without it. And since Bluetooth and speakers aren't exactly new technology, you'll find that this is one of the larger product categories in the tech world (admittedly, this feels like the case for almost anything you buy these days).

Enter: the Mashable tech and shopping teams. We stay on top of the latest Bluetooth speaker releases from brands like Sony, JBL, Bose, Sonos, and Marshall, while also applying our audio and product testing expertise to determine when old favorites are the better choice. We spend time testing the speakers in our own homes, evaluating the sound, portability, and, of course, price.

For 2026, our top picks include bass-thumping party speakers from Sony, the near-indestructible JBL Charge 6, and the charmingly retro Marshall Emberton III.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

The JBL Charge 6 is big enough to produce sound that will fill an outdoor party, yet still small enough to live on your bookshelf. Plus, it can charge other devices with a USB-C connection. On balance, we think it's the best Bluetooth speaker for most people right now.

The Charge 6 is one of the latest speakers from JBL, the brand behind the popular Flip Bluetooth speakers. Though the Charge 6 is bigger and $50 more expensive, we prefer it for the versatility and superior sound it offers.

We have to start with the namesake of this speaker — it's ability to charge devices. That makes it an exceptionally good choice for beach days or camping trips. JBL ditched the USB-A port on the Charge 5, opting instead for a USB-C to USB-C cable that allowed us to charge phones, earbuds, and even other speakers. Cool as it was, this feature alone isn't what made this speaker stand out.

Even at the lowest volumes, the Charge 6's sound easily filled a living room, but never sacrificed quality for loudness. When listening to Japanese Breakfast's For Melancholy Brunettes (& sad women), the sound balance came through strong and clear. I could hear both the bass notes and Michelle Zauner's voice ringing out with impressive clarity, and without losing the sound of the guitar. The companion app also comes with four different equalizer presets and a custom equalizer, so you can adjust the sound to be just right for your ears.

The Charge 6 also offers a couple of ways to amplify your sound even further. Thanks to the included Auracast, you can pair it with other JBL speakers with Auracast to double up on the sound or create a studio mode. When we tested it out with the Flip 6, it worked without a hitch. If you want a louder sound and more battery life, you can toggle on the speaker's PlaytimeBoost mode, which offers an extra four hours of battery life, though the quality of the sound sometimes struggles in this mode.

Whether you're taking it with you on the go or using it at home, the Charge 6 gives you options. For instance, the flat base allows it to sit on a table without any danger of rolling off, but if you prefer it to stand instead, it's also reliably balanced, thanks to the silicone ends. Though this speaker is larger than most of our other picks, it does come with an adjustable handle strap, which you can loop, use as a handle, or remove entirely. Ultimately, though, if you're looking for something that can easily fit into most bags, we recommend the smaller Flip 7 instead.

The Ultimate Ears Wonderboom 4 is not a must-have upgrade for anyone who owns the Wonderboom 3, but like the former version, it's a powerhouse for its size and price. This speaker's small (and rugged enough) to take along for a hike, while traveling, and for tossing next to the pool. Plus, it's so easy to find on sale that it's often available at a cheaper price than the older Wonderboom 3.

The Wonderboom 4 is basically the same exact speaker as the Wonderboom 3, visually and internally. Like the Wonderboom 3, you can drop it from heights of up to five feet without worrying about destroying it. It’s also waterproof with an IP67 rating. If you drop it in water, it can float for up to 30 minutes and come out the other side working completely fine. It also features a healthy 14 hours of battery life, which is more than other speakers of a similar size.

So what's different? On the sound front, the Wonderboom 4 has less muddiness than its predecessor, especially at higher volumes. In addition to the Outdoor Boost mode, you'll also get a Podcast Mode. We will say that we're not necessarily fans of the new addition — hosts' voices came through compressed, while background static felt more pronounced. Generally, this mode shone much better on the brand's Everboom speaker.

However, the standard audio is good enough for listening to podcasts and music, so ignoring the new EQ feels like a fine concession. As with the Wonderboom 3, we found that overall, the sound profile is well-rounded and can get loud. We do wish that this speaker had been updated with a companion app for more customization.

On the bright side, it's very easy to plug, play, and now charge, thanks to the addition of a USB-C charging port. While it's not all that different from the Wonderboom 3, anyone who wants a no-frills speaker that sounds good will be happy with this one. The Wonderboom 4 is easy to find in stock and on sale, making it our updated pick for the best speaker under $100.

There are portable speakers, and then there are ultra-portable speakers truly designed to move with you. JBL’s line of Clip speakers falls under the latter. The company’s latest creation, the Clip 5, is a moderate upgrade to the highly recommended Clip 4 (which we previously included). Carrying it around is easy-peasy, thanks to the signature clip that attaches to anything, from backpacks to belt loops. Its blend of affordability, fine sound, and resilient build quality earns the Clip 5 mass appeal among budget-conscious shoppers seeking a high-quality everyday speaker.

Honestly, the clip-on design. JBL’s built-in carabiner is a staple in the portable speaker category that is best executed on the Clip 5.

While providing maximum portability and protection (IP67 dust- and waterproofing), this fifth-gen speaker has a fun factor that only a handful of other compact speakers can match. Its IP certification makes the Clip 5 a stellar shower companion, producing adequate clarity and enough oomph to wake you up during early bathing sessions. The customizable EQ and multiple presets are crucial for enhancing sound quality; adjusting the frequency levels helps balance output. Pairing two Clip 5 speakers together will open the door to stereo sound.

The Clip 5 has impressively loud volume that also diminishes mids and highs when raised all the way up. You can get better sound from other portable speakers, such as the UE Wonderboom 4 that costs about $30 more, but is well worth the pickup if audio is your priority. Otherwise, the Clip 5 is just as small, sturdy, and serviceable at a cheaper price.

We recommend the Bose SoundLink Plus for beachgoers or campers who want loud music in a portable design. It's waterproof and dustproof, meaning it can withstand ocean water and sand or dirt. And it comes in lovely colors that scream summer.

Bose's SoundLink line includes an array of excellent speakers, and we've found the Plus to be in that sweet Goldilocks spot between the SoundLink Flex and SoundLink Max. It's incredibly loud with crisp, clear audio. And its size makes it portable and not too heavy (though it does have some heft to it), while still producing room-filling sound. Whether you're at the beach, hosting a pool party, camping, or just hanging out at home, the Bose SoundLink Plus is a stellar Bluetooth speaker. It can also easily pair with other Bose SoundLink speakers for extended sound.

Large-party hosts and performers. It can serve as a karaoke machine or live sound system, while also functioning as a wireless speaker that immerses the environment with surface-rattling bass response. The PartyBox Stage 320 isn’t just one of the best JBL speakers and party speakers — it’s arguably the best all-in-one option for the price.

Two 6.5-inch woofers and two 25mm tweeters create 240 watts of sound that can loudly fill up an entire room. JBL tuned this speaker for bass heads, which is evident when blasting contemporary tracks. Lows are energetic and punchy. Toying around with the manual EQ helps balance frequency range for more mids and highs (read our guide on how to customize your EQ settings). The multiple audio modes and presets enhance select content; Movie Mode is highly recommended for watching films on large screens.

JBL’s festive features are the biggest selling point. Traveling with the PartyBox Stage 320 isn’t much of a hassle either, thanks to the retractable handle and sturdy wheels to easily roll it around.

Listening at high volume causes distortion, so be mindful of this when hosting parties. It’s also wise to keep the speaker away from excessively wet environments, like the pool (water-resistant does not mean waterproof).

Not really, but it doesn’t hurt to have this protection. You’ll want to own a waterproof speaker if you plan on playing music at the beach, pool, or in your shower. It's not out of the question that your speaker could be a target for spills in the house. So, if you have the option to go waterproof (and you do), do it. It’s also worth educating yourself on ingress protection (IP) ratings to know the difference between each. Here is a breakdown below.

IPX1 – Survives vertical drip of 1 millimeter of water

IPX2 – Survives drip of 3 millimeters of water

IPX4 – Survives omnidirectional water splashing (water resistant)

IPX5 – Survives water jet splashing up to 12.5 liters for 1 min

IPX6 – Survives water jet splashing up to 100 liters for 3 mins

IPX7 – Complete submersion up to 1 meter for 30 mins

IPX8 – Complete submersion up to 3 meters for 30 minutes

When you're shopping for a new speaker, you're going to see the word "wattage" a lot. While that might be meaningless to you when you see it on a product page, it's actually a pretty simple concept. Basically, wattage refers to how loud your speaker can get without distorting the sound. Unless you enjoy really blasting your tunes or you regularly host parties, 50 watts is going to be more than enough for the average listener. But if you do like to crank it up to 11, the more watts, the merrier. While smaller portable Bluetooth speakers tend to have lower wattage, there are certainly some exceptions, including some of the products in this guide.

Our motto is quality over quantity. Having one amazing speaker is going to get you better sound than having multiple speakers that are just OK. If you’re fine with listening to music in a single room or plan on entertaining small crowds, then one speaker will suffice for mono presentation. Just make sure the environment has good acoustics. If you’re hosting larger crowds in larger settings or want a more immersive listening experience, then the way to go is pairing two of the same speakers (or speakers from the same brand) for stereo sound. Some models even let you pair up to 100 speakers at once to create full omnidirectional sound.

No. Some Bluetooth speakers are WiFi speakers. Not all WiFi speakers are Bluetooth speakers. Basically, a Bluetooth speaker connects to devices through (what else) Bluetooth, which is the most common wireless technology for the product category. WiFi speakers connect to devices through a reliable internet connection, making it a more appealing option for those with an integrated smart home ecosystem (e.g., Apple, Google, Sonos).

Essentially, yes. It’s all a matter of what audio codecs your speaker supports. AAC and SBC are the two most common codecs you’ll find on current portable Bluetooth speakers. There are some speakers that come with stronger codecs like LDAC to stream music at high-res 24bit/96kHz. All that means is you’ll hear more detail and nuance from recordings at a higher transmission. Something else to look for is LE Audio-ready speakers, which improve audio streaming at lower latency. Your speaker should run Bluetooth 5.2 or higher. Anything less than that could affect what you hear. The higher the Bluetooth version, the stronger the connection and streaming quality.

We created this guide by sifting through our past Bluetooth speaker reviews from Mashable staff and contributors, as well as testing out new portable Bluetooth speakers specifically for this article. All of the audio products in this guide have been thoroughly tested by the Mashable team, and we tested plenty more that didn't make the final cut.

We make sure to test every speaker with a variety of audio types (from audiobooks to videos to podcasts) and music genres. Where available, we look at equalizer options and see how they impact the sound of a speaker. Of course, we also test out the full volume range and evaluate whether it impacts the balance of the sound at any point. Other notable sound features we thoroughly test are adaptive audio modes, Bluetooth codecs, digital assistance (Google Assistant, Siri), mono and stereo, and speakerphone capability.

A speaker's portability (and usability) suffers if it can't stay alive for a least a few hours. Typically, we test speakers out for at least a week with regular use to see how often someone might have to charge them. It's not a perfect science, as we play around with special features and the volume settings. Our goal is to get the most accurate read of playtime for the average user and see if the speaker falls within the battery life claimed by brands. Something else we inspect is whether the speaker supports quick charging.

For a Bluetooth speaker's design, we look at a few different factors, including any water or dust proof rating, its size and options for taking it on the go, and even whether or not it would like nice (or distractingly bad) on a bookshelf in someone's home.

Finally, we look at how all the above contribute to the speaker's price point. We expect a $200 speaker to have markedly better sound quality and more features than a $80 speaker. As we expand our testing pool, we take into consideration how different speakers match up to their competitors.
