#191. England and Bayern Munich striker Harry Kane wins Ballon d'Or

The England captain ends his country's 25-year wait for the prestigious award after scoring 73 goals for club and country last season, beating competition from Lamine Yamal and Kylian Mbappe.

England captain Harry Kane won the men's Ballon d'Or for the first time on Monday, becoming the first English player to receive the prestigious football award since 2001.

The 33-year-old Bayern Munich striker was honored at a ceremony at the London Palladium after scoring 73 goals in 65 appearances for club and country last season.

The Ballon d'Or, awarded by French magazine France Football, is one of football's most prestigious individual honors, recognizing the world's best players.

The last English player to win the award was former Liverpool striker Michael Owen, who claimed the prize 25 years ago.

Kane's remarkable goalscoring record helped Bayern Munich secure a German league and cup double last season.

The former Tottenham Hotspur forward also led England to the World Cup semifinals, the country's best finish since winning the tournament in 1966.

Kane has finished as the Bundesliga's leading scorer for three consecutive seasons. He was also Europe's top club goalscorer during the 2025-26 campaign.

At Monday's ceremony, Kane thanked his Bayern Munich teammates for their support.

"Without you guys (his Bayern Munich team-mates) and the rest of the team it wouldn't be possible, the way you guys push me, the way we compete. So thank you very much," he said.

Kane also paid tribute to his former club Tottenham Hotspur, which he joined at the age of 11, as well as his England teammates and family.

"To my wife Kate, my best friend. We've been through ups and downs. You are my biggest support and I felt like you wanted this more than me," he said.

Reflecting on his career, Kane encouraged young footballers to pursue their ambitions despite setbacks.

"I hope my journey, it wasn't straightforward, I didn't go straight to the top, I had to experience a lot of loan moves and fight my way to get to where I am now. I hope that can give you inspiration."

Kane faced strong competition from Barcelona's Spanish teenager Lamine Yamal and Real Madrid's French forward Kylian Mbappe.

He joins Stanley Matthews, Bobby Charlton, Kevin Keegan and Owen among the English players to have won the award.

Spain midfielder Alexia Putellas claimed the women's Ballon d'Or for the third time in her career, having previously won the award in 2021 and 2022.

The 32-year-old was recognized after a successful season with Barcelona, winning the treble and scoring 21 goals across all competitions.

Putellas also played a key role in Barcelona's Champions League triumph, contributing seven goals and seven assists during the campaign.

She joined English club London City Lionesses in July after spending 14 years at Barcelona.

Her victory ended a run of three consecutive Ballon d'Or wins by fellow Spaniard Aitana Bonmatí.

Barcelona's Lamine Yamal won the Kopa Trophy for the best young male player for the third consecutive year, while his teammate Vicky Lopez claimed the women's award for the second year running.

Barcelona coach Pere Romeu was named women's coach of the year, while Spain manager Luis de la Fuente won the men's coaching award.

Spain goalkeeper Unai Simon received the men's Yashin Trophy, with Cata Coll winning the women's equivalent.

Barcelona was named women's club of the year, while Paris Saint-Germain took the men's award.

#186. ‘Are you waiting for me to be attacked?’ Fatima Payman slams PM over staffing

Albanese promises ‘swift response’ on MP safety as he mulls panic buttons for politicians

Follow our Australian politics live blog for latest updates

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Anthony Albanese has promised a “swift government response” to safety threats to MPs and floated the provision of duress alarms for electorate offices, saying politicians and their staff have put up with threats for too long.

The prime minister’s pledge to the Labor party room came hours after Senator Fatima Payman again slammed her former government colleagues over the lack of staffing given to her office, controversially asking: “Is the prime minister waiting for something, like what happened to Dr Helen Haines, to occur to my office?”

MPs across the political divide have spoken forcefully of the need for more protection for their offices after the alleged attack on the Indi MP last week, raising alarm about threats and violence directed to their staff. Politicians are also revealing the scale of abuse, online and offline, directed toward them, including death and rape threats, with Australia’s eSafety commissioner, Julie Inman Grant, telling Senate estimates she and her family had also been subject to online abuse and threats.

In the first Labor party-room meeting since the alleged attack, Albanese gave a long address, saying this week “must be a turning point” for civil discourse and how threats are responded to. He also conceded there needed to be more investment in security and safety for politicians.

Albanese said MPs have had to “put up with the threat environment for too long”, that the government “need to do more” and “more can and should be done”.

“You’ve all dedicated so much to the safety of others in the workplace. I’m determined to make sure we do everything for your safety and the safety of your staff as well,” he said.

Albanese did not give specifics, but promised a “swift government response” on safety, and noted numerous MPs had raised the prospect of receiving duress alarms, or panic buttons, to alert authorities to a safety incident. This was among a “series of options” under consideration.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

The home affairs minister, Tony Burke, also told the meeting that the government was looking at safety at polling booths around election time.

Government sources said numerous changes were being considered, with a full response and proposal likely to be revealed in coming days. MPs have raised a wide range of concerns, including greater security and hardening of their electoral offices, better communication with federal police, and resources for security at their private homes such as CCTV and lighting – all options understood to be under consideration.

An ongoing review into commonwealth workplaces will also be expedited by Labor, with recommendations specifically about safety and security to be delivered soon.

The opposition leader, Angus Taylor, told his MPs in a Coalition party-room meeting of ongoing conversations with the government about personal safety and the importance of getting it right, but did not raise any specific proposals.

Payman, who quit Labor in 2024 to sit on the crossbench, on Monday night lashed out at the prime minister for not responding to a request for additional staff in line with other independent political offices.

The prime minister has discretion over the allocation of personal staff, which are typically more senior advisers. All politicians receive electoral office staff, but Payman has zero advisers while 16 other crossbench MPs and senators shared 30 advisers, according to an internal document tabled in the hearing.

In a Senate estimates hearing, Payman questioned the minister Don Farrell whether the staff allocation shortfall was due to Albanese having a personal gripe with her, claiming her staff were severely overworked.

“If [Albanese] cares so much about the welfare and wellbeing of people who work in this building, then I implore you [Farrell] and the prime minister to please look into the fact that my staff are burning out,” she said.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“Is the prime minister waiting for something, like what happened to Dr Helen Haines, to occur to my office – to me – for him to take our request seriously?”

Farrell and other Labor senators in the hearing strongly rejected the insinuation, but Payman refused to withdraw the comments when asked until her staffing request was dealt with.

Payman told Guardian Australia last week there had been a massive increase in the number of death threats and hate comments since 2024 when she defected from Labor. Payman said her office manager had opened mail last year containing human faeces, resulting in a biohazard team having to respond.

“We had all these people in hazmat suits come through [the office],” she said. “The trauma that that inflicts on my staff members is horrible. No office or no worker should ever have to deal with that kind of crap.”

Inman Grant said in her final appearance before Senate estimates on Tuesday evening that it was a “sensitive and pivotal moment” for parliament in the wake of the alleged attack, and the environment was “dangerously febrile for women in senior government roles”.

“When public officials are used for political point scoring, a target goes on our backs and we are subjected to an onslaught of online abuse and threats,” she said.

“As many of you know, this has been the normal state of affairs for me and my family for years, yet somehow this is meant to be accepted as part of public service.”

Inman Grant did not name any people who have targeted her personally, but her work in the role has seen her labelled as a “censorship commissar” by the world’s richest person, Elon Musk. The US Republican congressman Jim Jordan labelled Inman Grant as a “zealot for global takedowns”.

Inman Grant said more needs to be done for the next generation of leaders to feel safe in online spaces or public-facing roles.

“Social media is no longer the once envisioned digital town square – it has become more like a gladiatorial arena,” she said. “Meanwhile, platforms continue to roll back abuse and violent speech policies that let some of the most egregious offenders out of digital jail.”

#185. NASA’s Oldest Martian Spacecraft Have Been Running for Decades. Here’s How

NASA’s Mars Odyssey spacecraft has been operating for so long, it can rent a car without any fees. The spacecraft arrived at the Red Planet on October 24, 2001. It, along with its 20-year-old sibling, the Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter (MRO), have operated longer than anyone watching their launches could have dreamed possible.

Having such a long record of observations from above the Martian surface has rewritten the story of water on the planet, past and present alike. Keeping the wizened orbiters going for so long has been possible only thanks to great engineering and constant problem solving—and a little luck. But scientists wonder whether, despite their storied legacies, Odyssey and MRO will be the last of their kind, at least for the Red Planet.

“Both Odyssey and MRO are some of the most productive spacecraft that have ever existed,” says Briony Horgan, a planetary scientist at Purdue University who isn’t affiliated with either mission, calling them “critical” to NASA’s overall Mars research program. Odyssey’s observations of the planet’s ice, for example, and MRO’s ability to see surface features change over time have each given scientists unprecedented insight into Mars as a vibrant planet rather than a lump of rock. Odyssey and MRO also connect humans to every NASA rover, which can’t communicate with Earth directly. It’s an impressive legacy for a pair of orbiters that were only meant to last just a few years.

Space is a notoriously harsh environment that takes luck and resilience to survive for decades. Particularly tricky is the barrage of high-energy particles hitting machinery; a powerful enough strike to a delicate enough apparatus can be fatal. And whereas engineers can keep constant tabs on satellites orbiting Earth, bandwidth to Mars is more limited—science spacecraft might only check in once or twice a week, says Rob Lillis, a planetary and space physicist at the University of California, Berkeley, who has worked on a range of more recent Mars orbiters.

“A Mars craft has to be able to manage by itself,” he says.

A concept image of the Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter.

Losing an instrument is bad, but losing the power or computing systems can brick the whole craft. NASA often doubles up on vital parts of a spacecraft to ensure that if something important fails, the mission can run off the spare until engineers can troubleshoot a problem.

In the MRO’s case, one challenge it’s faced is ironically due to its staying power. The orbiter determines its precise location and orientation by comparing what its cameras see with a stellar atlas onboard—with just one hiccup. “MRO is actually old enough that some of those stars have moved across the night sky—very slightly, but they have moved,” says Paul Fieseler, an engineer at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory and chief engineer for MRO. The tiny misalignments confused the spacecraft, and last year, engineers had to send up an updated map to keep MRO oriented into the future.

Odyssey’s greatest risk right now is simply running out of propellant it uses to make tiny adjustments to maintain its orientation in space. The spacecraft doesn’t have a fuel gauge–without gravity to push the fuel down, there’s no volume to measure–so NASA can’t be sure how much juice it still has. Engineers have improvised new ways to fully operate the spacecraft on just a few drops at a time in a bid to extend its life.

Experts say both spacecraft benefited from failures during NASA’s infamous “faster, better, cheaper” era. In 1999, NASA lost two missions arriving at Mars: First the Mars Climate Orbiter burned up in the atmosphere, and then, just months later, the Mars Polar Lander botched its arrival and slammed into the surface. Each catastrophe, investigations determined, was caused by what might in more pedestrian realms be dubbed careless mistakes. The first was due to miscommunication between a ground system relying on imperial units and the flight system using metric, while the second was due to a software error not caught during testing.

“Those failures were very much on the mind of the team pulling together the Odyssey spacecraft,” says Glen Havens, an engineer at JPL and project and mission manager of Mars Odyssey. With those losses stinging, NASA invested more in perfecting the Odyssey and MRO spacecraft before launch, designing each to be as resilient as possible and testing every tiny component and every system configuration.

Topographic imagery from Mars captured by the MRO.

One key principle is what NASA calls “margin”—the spacecraft equivalent of carrying a snack, just in case.

“Unexpected things might happen, and you want to make sure the spacecraft will survive,” says Nilton Renno, a spaceflight engineer at the University of Michigan who hasn’t worked on either mission. MRO launched with a larger battery and more propellant than it strictly needed, Fieseler notes, and that margin is now keeping the mission going, with NASA hoping to operate it for another decade.

But orbiting Mars, it’s wise not to take anything for granted. Just last year, NASA lost the 11-year-old Mars Atmosphere and Volatile Evolution—its youngest Red Planet orbiter—after the spacecraft unexpectedly lost power on the far side of the planet. As Odyssey and MRO continue to age, questions linger about what Mars orbiters come next for NASA.

The agency has hired Blue Origin to build a dedicated communications satellite to beam scads of data back to Earth for at least five years—but unlike its forebears, which began as science missions before turning to communications work, this orbiter will only relay signals for other spacecraft.

NASA’s Mars science agenda includes only relatively limited projects. One mission, which Lillis leads, features two orbiters currently on their way to study how the planet responds to the constant stream of charged particles flowing off the sun. But even he calls the mission “right on the edge of being too small to do good science.” Then, there’s Skyfall, the trio of small helicopters meant to launch aboard the first nuclear-propelled spacecraft within just two years—a shockingly rapid timeline, and for a mission with minimal ability to answer meaty research questions.

To scientists, none of these initiatives quite build on the legacy of Odyssey and MRO and may even raise the specter of the infamous Mars failures that came before. “Certainly they’re pushing for faster, cheaper,” Horgan says. “It’s going to be a lot more challenging to do good science in that environment.”

I worked for a whole day using only the iPhone Duo

#182. We spent 12 months testing red light masks: Here are 6 that gave us real results (and 2 to skip)

No one is seriously questioning whether the best red light masks work anymore. The professional skincare community and dermatologists largely agree on their effectiveness. The more nuanced question is which red light therapy devices provide the best visible results. When choosing a red light mask or LED skincare device, factors like light wavelength, irradiance, and bulb placement all determine whether a mask is actually doing anything for your fine lines or inflammation.

Since consistency is so key for visible results, the best red light mask is the one that you'll actually feel like wearing on a daily basis. So, over months and months of at-home testing, Mashable reporters and editors used popular red light therapy devices from Shark, Solawave, CurrentBody, and more. After trying over 10 different products, we ultimately found six that delivered consistent results for our testers — and two we can't recommend.

These are the tech, tools, and products — from laptops to e-readers, from earbuds to robovacs, and more — that Mashable ranks best in class.

There are some ways to tell that a red light face mask won't be effective before you buy it and use it for a few months.

Wavelength is the first factor anyone will tell you to consider. You want to find products that emit light within the therapeutic window, or the range of wavelengths that can be effective when treating common skin conditions. There is now an abundance of evidence that specific wavelengths of red light can be effective for:

Reduction of fine lines, dark circles, and crow's feet

To be effective, the light coming from the mask must penetrate different layers of your skin. There are optimal wavelengths for each type of light a face mask emits, and any reputable brand will put their mask's wavelengths front and center in the listing. These carefully chosen ranges are strong enough to penetrate your skin, but gentle enough to avoid over-activating delicate face skin.

Red light does its work in the middle layer (dermis) of your skin, requiring wavelengths between 630 and 633 nm to reach past the top layer. There, red light stimulates cells to produce rejuvenating proteins like collagen and elastin, evening out surface redness, and preventing scarring or post-pimple red spots.

Most red light masks also use near-infrared red light therapy, which requires longer wavelengths to travel further than regular red light. Near-infrared light between 830 and 850 nm reaches into tissue under the dermis to further stimulate cell turnover, firm skin, and heal deep-seated spots or stubborn dark circles.

Blue light is often used to target acne, and some of the best red light masks use a combination of blue and red light therapy.

Blue light operates on the top layer (epidermis) of your skin, so its ideal wavelength is shorter than either red light standard. Blue light at 415nm has an antimicrobial effect, ideally killing the bacteria that cause acne before a breakout starts. If hellbent bacterial acne makes it past the blue light phase — or if you're also dealing with hormonal or cystic acne not caused by bacteria — red light swoops in to neutralize inflammation and speed up the healing process.

The thing is, most mainstream LED face masks hit those wavelength marks. So, how do you choose between masks if their wavelength numbers are all the same?

Irradiance is another major element that determines whether or not a red light mask actually works. While wavelength refers to the length the light can travel, irradiance refers to the amount of light energy that's actually reaching your skin per session (not just the intensity of light being generated at the bulb). If wavelength numbers are correct but irradiance is low, the mask will still be kind of a dud.

Irradiance is officially expressed as milliwatts per square centimeter. The optimal irradiance range for a red light face mask is pretty wide (anywhere between 30 and 100 mW/cm²), though many brands don't directly provide that number.

Most brands do note the length of each session, which is one way to quickly gauge irradiance without getting into calculations. Dr. Mamina Turegano sums it up best on her YouTube account: "Higher irradiance means more effective energy delivery per minute of use." Longer sessions don't automatically mean that results will be more powerful — a mask with sessions that last for less than 10 minutes has higher irradiance than a mask with sessions that last for 20 to 30 minutes. Since a mask with lower irradiance needs to sit on your face for longer, you might be less likely to keep up with wearing it.

The number of bulbs and the placement of bulbs inside the mask can also impact efficacy, especially if you're expecting results in a specific area of your face. Masks with more bulbs packed evenly across the face will provide more even coverage across every inch of your face than masks with more sporadically placed bulbs.

Some masks also provide more light sources overall by emitting both red and blue light simultaneously from every single bulb, like the Shark CryoGlow. This would be a better option for full-face acne prevention than masks with blue and red LEDs only concentrated in certain spots, like the Dr. Dennis Gross DRx SpectraLite.

Of course, you probably didn't come here for a science lesson. You want to find the best red light masks Mashable's testers used and loved.

After trying out 11 different products, we ultimately found six that we can recommend. These masks delivered visible results for our product testers.

Remember: Consistency is essential for the best results (or any results)with LED face masks. You'll need to wear your mask consistently for at least one to two months.

Read my full review of the Shark CryoGlow mask.

After testing it for several months, I believe the Shark CryoGlow mask is a universally good choice for just about anyone. It offers carefully curated settings that target signs of aging and acne, automatically switching between red and blue light depending on the skin concern you've chosen on the remote. The unique under-eye cooling pads are such a treat for anyone who hates mornings.

Frequent traveling is the only case where the CryoGlow won't be the most convenient choice — its bulky, hard-shell design isn't exactly travel-friendly.

It would be hard to argue that any mask is a better value or offers a more premium experience than the Shark CryoGlow. If it tells you anything, I always revert to the CryoGlow after finishing testing windows for other masks. At $349, the Shark mask is actually relatively budget-friendly compared to multiple competitors that don't have blue light at all: The CurrentBody Skin Series 2 and OmniLux both cost around $100 more.

The Shark mask is lined with 160 evenly spaced tri-wick bulbs. Every single bulb can emit red, near-infrared, or blue light, and each bulb can emit all three light types simultaneously for 480 light sources total. That's true full-face coverage for the duration of any setting, compared to other masks that turn red light bulbs off completely if you're on the blue light setting.

The CryoGlow's blemish mode played a major role in saving my chin, cheeks, and jawline from raging adult acne that wasn't responding to my very consistent skincare routine. Rather than each setting being tied to one specific wavelength, the LEDs automatically switch between a combination of various wavelengths depending on the skin concern. Blemish Repair starts on blue and infrared to target acne-causing bacteria, then shifts to a purple glow of blue and red to fight inflammation from any pimples that have already popped up.

The under-eye chill pads are the CryoGlow's other unique feature. The cool compress feels amazing on groggy eyes in the morning or post-shower on a summer night. Just remember that the de-puffing effect is only temporary, and the pads do take the place of bulbs that could be targeting fine lines or dark circles. But the Shark mask is also affordable enough to pair with a dedicated red light eye mask.

Read our full review of the TheraFace Mask. This red light mask review comes from Mashable Deputy Shopping Editor Miller Kern.

Therabody's TheraFace Mask is for someone who wants to focus on facial health as a whole. It combines LED light with vibrational massage to provide a well-rounded facial treatment. It's also for someone who has room in their budget for a splurge.

Alternative: If you don't need vibration, but like the sound of the mask otherwise, check out the more budget-friendly TheraFace Mask Glo for $379.99.

As the most expensive option on this list, the TheraFace Mask has to stand out. It does so by offering the most LEDs of the bunch (648) and offering Therabody's signature massage therapy directly on the mask. The combination of LED and vibration is meant to help with skin appearance and ease tension and boost circulation.

One of my favorite features of the mask is that it has built-in treatments, so you don't have to guess how long you should run different light cycles. It's also totally remote-free — there are just two buttons on the mask to control the LEDs and vibrations. One press of the LED button on the right side of the mask triggers a 9-minute treatment that cycles through red, blue, and red plus infrared lights (3 minutes each) and different massage patterns across the face and head strap. You can press both buttons to turn the massage feature on/off, stick with just one light color, or turn off the LEDs altogether for just a head massage. The mask also has a removable eye shield, which you can take out if you want to target wrinkles close to the eyes.

Even after my initial two weeks of testing the TheraFace Mask, I noticed my acne getting smaller and flatter. My skin also felt firmer and softer. I sometimes get lazy with my skincare routine and don't always feel like incorporating a nine-minute LED treatment. But as soon as I feel a new cystic pimple creeping under my skin, the TheraFace Mask is my first line of defense.

Simply put, you won't find a better budget red light mask than the Dreame Chrona. Its MSRP of $269.99 is already much easier to stomach than the $400 to $600 price tags on other popular masks — but budget shoppers should be able to find it on sale for as low as $224.99 on a regular basis.

The Chrona is also the ideal LED face mask for someone who wants to stay productive while red light therapy-ing. The lights don't cloud your vision, and not even the remote is wired to the mask, so you'll be able to complete tasks on your to-do list without interruption.

Its flexible exterior also makes it a stellar red light mask for travel, plus it comes with a soft travel pouch.

The Dreame Chrona isn't just a great mask for the price — it's a great red light mask period. (Who would have thought that the brand behind the best robot vacuum I've tested in 2026 is also the brand behind the best red light mask I've tested in 2026?)

First, the Chrona mask is such a chill red light mask to wear around the house. It's one of the few out there that doesn't have a remote attached to it, so there's no cord in the way when you're trying to do a chore or feed a pet. The lights near the eyes aren't so intense that you can't see, and the ultra-slim design doesn't mess with your peripherals.

The Chrona mask has an expansive selection of light modes to cover anti-aging from all angles. Past the red, near-infrared, and blue light settings that everyone expects, there's also green light (for neutralizing redness) and yellow light (for balancing and brightening your complexion). I think my cheeks look a little less red after two months, but I'm confident that I'll see better results if I stay more consistent with these two unique settings.

I always get nervous to pause my Shark CryoGlow usage to test a new mask (I just trust it that much). I'm happy to report that the Dreame Chrona has sustained results in my two staple categories. I haven't really gotten any major non-hormonal pimples in my initial two-month window. I've also noticed less texture and smoother makeup application on my nose and chin. You can really tell it's working when my CC cream and pressed powder aren't settling into fine lines anymore, even after several hours.

iRestore is a popular brand on Amazon that's best known for its popular red light device for treating hair loss. I put the brand's red light eye mask to the test for this guide.

I found that the iRestore Illumina Eye Mask is the premium choice for those who are more concerned about an eye area glow-up than their entire face. iRestore's eye mask costs more than most other red light eye masks due to its densely-packed LED layout, so the ideal buyer would be willing to pay a little extra for more precise anti-aging coverage around their crow's feet or 11 lines.

For a mask that only covers half of your face, there's quite a bit of power packed into the eye mask version of iRestore's Illumina. Each bulb holds two LEDs, adding up to 180 total LED sources compared to 80 LED sources in CurrentBody's $249.99 eye mask.

I had been noticing that my crow's feet look deeper than those of my similarly-aged friends when we're all smiling in a group photo. However, you wouldn't peg me as someone who smiles, laughs, or has any whimsy or energy at all if you saw how dark my under-eyes are on some days. (I've had insomnia for a decade and it shows.) Though the Shark CryoGlow mask is my all-time favorite, I'd be remiss to not mention its major blind spot: The cooling pads take the place of LED bulbs that would target your eye area, and the de-puffing effects of that cooling are very temporary.

So, I introduced the iRestore eye mask as a supplement to my routine — if I used the Shark mask in the morning that day, I used the iRestore eye mask before bed. My eye area definitely saw a sustainable glow up. CC cream under my eyes doesn't look nearly as raisin-y, even if I add powder — the lines that they used to settle into aren't as deep anymore. I say CC cream instead of concealer because my under-eyes are no longer so dark that I have to brighten them to look alive. I don't think the iRestore eye mask can completely eliminate crow's feet or match the effectiveness of Botox, but I certainly believe it can stretch the amount of time I need between appointments.

The elite Shark and eye mask combo costs $598 all together, which doesn't seem that wild in comparison to other masks, given the robust combination of red, near-infrared, and blue light that you're getting to every inch of your face.

The Illumina Eye Mask is significantly easier to pack than a full-face mask, even the bendy ones. It's super thin and barely weighs a pound. Since it lasts for at least 10 days on one charge, it'd be safe to take on vacation without worrying about the charger.

The CurrentBody Skin Series 2 and its rare deep near-infrared light would be a good choice for those with advanced signs of aging, stubborn scarring, or other intense texture concerns.

Having tried other LED face masks that do much more, I'm not convinced that the CurrentBody Skin 2 provides a premium-enough experience to cost what it does. Still, it's one of the only mainstream masks that hits deep near-infrared at 1,072 mm, offering even more extensive penetration than the 830 nm (regular near-infrared), where most masks max out.

Deep NIR reaches into the skin's deepest tissue layers to be absorbed by mitochondria and turned to cellular energy, which can help with collagen production and serious blood flow stimulation. This powerful CurrentBody speaks more to shoppers trying to combat deep-rooted wrinkles or severe loss of elasticity, especially around the eye or mouth area.

Since preventing acne and treating fine lines are my main concerns when testing a red light mask, the CurrentBody Skin 2 wasn't the best value for me. It doesn't have blue light at all, and it's pretty expensive for my mostly preventive anti-aging goals — I could pay $100 or $200 less for a mask that still reaches 830 nm NIR to treat my super-light wrinkles.

Not for nothing, I kind of dreaded putting this mask on every day. Though it's flexible and lightweight, it felt oddly constricting and sweaty. The deep NIR bulbs around the eyes are so intense that the entire room turns blue and a little blurry. For such a pricey mask, the remote is incredibly basic with no countdown time or adjustable settings to be found. Because there's no screen, the only way to find out that the battery is was low is when the mask suddenly dies.

Who it's for: Guys (or anyone, really) who aren't ready for a full Hannibal Lecter-style face mask yet, but who are tired of looking at dark circles under their eyes. It's also a super-affordable entry point into the category. As an added bonus, it's easier to pack during travel than the best red light face masks.

This red light mask review comes from Mashable Tech Editor Timothy Beck Werth.

For people who are primarily worried about the eye area, the Luminator is a smart and low-cost way to try out red light treatment. It also makes a great gift for men.

The Luminator has a sleek design and attractive price point, making it less intimidating than most $500 red light masks. In addition, you only need to wear it for three minutes each day, and it's comfortable on the face.

I didn't even need to use the included straps — it just fit perfectly on my face. I did notice improved luminosity in my under-eye and crow's feet areas, and I've seen improvement to dark circles as well. Honestly, the only thing I don't like about it is cleaning it.

One of our freelancers has also tested this particular device and gave it similarly high marks for ease of use and effectiveness. It uses multiple wavelengths of light to penetrate deep into the skin, and, according to Lumen, can help boost collagen production and improve signs of aging.

In our experience, it had a positive impact on dark circles and skin texture around the eyes.

This red light mask review comes from Lead Shopping Reporter Bethany Allard.

Of course, the brand behind the viral red-light skincare wand also has a red light mask, and though I had high hopes, the Solawave Wrinkle Retreat Pro mask didn’t quite hit the mark for me. It’s not that it didn’t work — after about a month of regular use, I noticed my skin was glowier, smoother, and generally more even in tone. Even immediately after use, my skin looked perked up. So what’s the problem? Well, at $499, it’s not exactly the cheapest option out there, and it’s missing the more robust experience — like facial massage, heat and cooling therapy, and blue light — that I’d expect at that price point.

The lack of blue light was especially disappointing. Even though the Solawave mask reduced the redness of breakouts, I know from my experience with the Shark CryoGlow that blue light can make all the difference — Shark’s mask basically made it so I never had breakouts in the first place.

That said, this mask does have great battery life and is super easy to store or pack into a suitcase. Solawave runs buy-one, get-one-free deals relatively often, so if you have another person in your life you want to gift a mask, this could be more worth it, but for the majority of people, I recommend checking out one of the other options on this list instead.

I actually had an overall positive experience with the iRestore Illumina mask. It's extremely lightweight for a mask that's not the floppy silicone material and seems to last forever on one charge. I prefer my LED masks to have countdown timers on the remotes, and the iRestore is one of the few that does.

But as a hard shell red light mask that also offers blue light, I can't put the iRestore mask head-to-head with the Shark mask, and it suffers for the comparison. The iRestore is the more basic option in that duel, despite costing $50 more than the Shark. It also leaves deep suction cup dents in my forehead that last for hours.

If the iRestore Illumina ever dropped from $399 to $299, I'd tell you to consider it. Don't get too excited if you're seeing $799 crossed out — it's always like that at Amazon, so it's not exactly an amazing deal.

Light therapy is a friendlier-sounding term for photobiomodulation, which describes how lightwaves communicate with your mitochondria to kickstart biological processes like collagen production, tissue repair, or general boosted cellular activity.

The specific wavelengths of red and near-infrared light used in an LED face mask can penetrate the skin barrier to stimulate the cells responsible for healing or slowing signs of aging.

Red light therapy masks are designed to reduce fine lines, dark circles, and crow's feet, improve skin texture, prevent wrinkles, boost collagen production, and reduce inflammation and redness.

Every red light mask in this list was tested for at least eight weeks, which is the average amount of time it takes to see visible results with daily usage. Many were used over a period of three months or more. Per each brand's instructions, the masks were only used on clean, dry skin before any other skincare products were applied.

Each reviewer's skin goals were obviously a main consideration, as well as the ease of incorporating the mask into their everyday skincare routine. Here's a breakdown of our testing process for red light masks.

Obviously, the biggest question is whether or not each red light therapy device actually made improvements to the skin. Each reviewer specifically considered skin goals like less frequent breakouts, faster healing of active breakouts, or fine line reduction.

Red light masks need to be worn consistently to work as intended, and you'll only be consistent if the mask is easy to wear every day. We considered several aspects of mask comfort, from the physical bendy or shell design to visibility while the LEDs are glowing near your eyes. Battery life also impacts day-to-day use — does the device need to be charged too often to be convenient?

Red light need to emit certain wavelengths to effectively penetrate the skin. We ensure that each device's red light bulbs emit the recommended 630 to 660 nm. We give extra points to masks that can switch to near-infrared between 810 and 850 nm or blue light between 415 and 445 nm. Extra settings like cooling or vibration therapy are also taken into account for the overall experience.

Mainstream full face red light masks range from $249 to $649.99. What we care about most is the bang for your buck. If a mask is toward the pricier end of the spectrum, are the settings and results premium enough to justify a higher cost? If a mask is at the cheaper end of the spectrum, is it a true affordable steal or only affordable because it's crappy? Could this one-time purchase become more cost-effective over time if you can spend less money on fancy serums or esthetician appointments?

Her ever-evolving experience in these categories helps her make thoughtful recommendations for how to spend your money during shopping holidays like Black Friday, which Leah has been covering for Mashable since 2017.

#181. US seeks new charges against former FBI chief James Comey

The Trump administration accuses the former FBI director of leaking classified information a decade ago. Comey denies wrongdoing, while his lawyers argue the case is legally invalid and comes too late.

The US Justice Department has launched a third attempt to prosecute former FBI Director James Comey, accusing him of illegally disclosing national defense information in 2016.

Court documents unsealed on Monday reveal allegations that Comey directed a subordinate to share classified information with a journalist.

The case marks another escalation in President Donald Trump's long-running conflict with the former FBI chief, who has denied wrongdoing.

The case centers on an October 2016 New York Times article about a secret US surveillance order involving internet company Yahoo.

The order reportedly required Yahoo to scan emails for messages potentially linked to a foreign terrorist organization. It had been issued by a secret intelligence court.

Prosecutors allege that Comey instructed a staff member to contact a New York Times journalist to correct inaccurate information published by news agency Reuters the previous day.

According to the Justice Department, this resulted in the illegal transfer of classified national defense information to an unauthorized person.

However, the allegations have already been investigated twice.

Federal authorities began examining the disclosure in 2017. Prosecutor John Durham recommended against bringing criminal charges in December 2018, according to Comey's lawyers.

A second investigation, launched in 2019 under the code name "Tropical Vortex," was also closed without charges in February 2020.

The Justice Department filed a legal document known as a "criminal information" in early October, shortly before the 10-year deadline for prosecuting the alleged offense.

Unlike an indictment, a criminal information does not require approval from a grand jury or judge.

Prosecutors say the filing allows them to extend the statute of limitations, which determines how long authorities have to bring criminal charges.

Comey's lawyers dispute that interpretation. They argue that federal law explicitly requires an indictment within 10 years and that the government's filing does not meet that requirement.

They have asked a judge to dismiss the case. A ruling is still pending.

Comey's attorney, Patrick Fitzgerald, also pointed to the two earlier investigations.

"Both times the Government determined that no charges should be brought against anyone," he said.

Responding to the latest allegations in a video posted on Substack, Comey condemned the prosecution.

"It would almost be funny if it weren't such an appalling abuse of the Department of Justice," he said.

"I'm still innocent. I'm still not afraid of these people, and I still believe in the independent federal judiciary."

The latest case is the third criminal proceeding brought against Comey since Trump returned to the White House in January 2025.

In September 2025, prosecutors charged him with making false statements to Congress and obstructing a congressional proceeding.

A federal judge dismissed the case after finding that the Trump-appointed prosecutor who brought the charges had been unlawfully appointed.

In April 2026, Comey was indicted again, this time over an Instagram photograph showing seashells arranged to form the numbers "86 47."

The Justice Department interpreted the numbers as a threat against Trump, arguing that "86" referred to killing someone and "47" identified Trump as the 47th US president.

Comey denied threatening Trump's life and has sought to have that case dismissed.

Comey was appointed FBI director in 2013 by Democratic President Barack Obama.

His relationship with Trump deteriorated shortly after the president took office in 2017.

Comey resisted Trump's request to pledge personal loyalty during a private dinner.

Trump fired him in May 2017 while the FBI was investigating Russian interference in the 2016 presidential election and possible connections to Trump's campaign.

The investigation was subsequently taken over by special counsel Robert Mueller. It established that Russia had interfered in the election but found insufficient evidence to prove criminal coordination between Moscow and Trump's campaign.

The two men have remained bitter political opponents. Trump has repeatedly accused Comey of treason, while Comey compared the president to a mafia boss in his 2018 memoir.

#176. Guardian Essential poll: One Nation and Labor’s primary vote on par as discontent with Albanese rises

Prime minister’s disapproval rating increases to 56%, the equal highest in Essential polls since he took the job in 2022

Follow our Australian politics live blog for latest updates

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

One Nation’s primary vote is back on par with Labor in the latest Guardian Essential poll, with Anthony Albanese’s public disapproval rising to its equal highest on record and more Australians than ever before believing the country is on the wrong track.

But Australians are still not convinced One Nation or the Coalition would do a better job than the current government, with Labor still the most trusted party on the economy and managing the country.

“This is a sobering poll for Labor, but not a ringing endorsement of One Nation either,” Peter Lewis, the executive director of Essential Media, said.

The Guardian Essential poll of 1,087 Australians, conducted last week, found Labor and One Nation neck and neck on 28% of the primary vote each. The Coalition is further back on 23%. Both conservative parties have improved their votes since last month, when One Nation was on 25% and the Coalition on 22%.

The poll took place early last week, after the alleged attack on the respected independent MP Helen Haines.

Labor lost a point from September’s 29%, coinciding with a further deterioration of Albanese’s personal approval ratings, and amid increasing scrutiny of the government’s handling of the economy, inflation and cost-of-living issues.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Disapproval of Albanese rose two points to 56%, the equal highest in Essential’s polling since he became prime minister in 2022. Albanese’s approval rating was 38%, also up one point, for a net approval of -18.

Albanese’s disapproval has been this high only once before, in August, when his approval was even lower at 35%, for a net approval of -21 that month.

The opposition leader, Angus Taylor, scored a 35% approval and 40% disapproval, for a net approval of -5 – a marked improvement since September, when his approval rating was just 31% and his disapproval 43%.

The poll also showed a new high number of Australians saying they felt the nation was going in the wrong direction, adding to the midterm malaise for Labor. In October, 61% of respondents said they thought the country was on the wrong track, with just 25% believing it was on the right track.

Essential’s “wrong track” number has been steadily increasing over Labor’s time in office, up from just 38% in May 2023. But it increased markedly between late last year, when it was 46% in October 2025, and early this year when it rose to 54% in January and has not dipped below 50% since.

Voters are also quick to blame the government for economic and cost-of-living issues. Asked what was most responsible for the rising cost of living from a list of factors, 32% of voters nominated government spending as the top cause. That was far above global events such as the war in the Middle East (which 24% nominated as the top cause) and supermarket price gouging (19%).

“The key markers are historic low levels for Labor midway through the term, but the electorate is clearly not settled on any viable alternative,” Lewis said.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“A key challenge is the perception that it’s local factors, rather than the global chaos emanating from the White House, which is to blame for cost-of-living pressure.”

The poll asked Australians which party they trusted on key issues, and what voters thought One Nation could do if it won government. When voters were asked which party was trusted to “ensure the economy works in the interests of everyday Australians”, Labor led on 32%, followed by One Nation on 29% and the Coalition far back on 18%.

Some 21% of voters said they didn’t see any difference.

On which party was trusted to “manage the country generally”, Labor again led on 31%, with One Nation (25%) and the Coalition (23%) further back.

Voters also gave their verdict on how One Nation would govern. While 43% of voters said they thought Pauline Hanson’s party would manage immigration better than current settings, 33% said they thought it would be worse. Only 20% of voters thought One Nation would handle relationships with trading partners such as the US and China better, with 39% believing it would do a worse job.

One Nation also received mixed verdicts on how it would manage energy policy (29% of voters said better, 36% said worse), cost of groceries (27% better, 37% worse), managing a crisis like a pandemic (23% better, 41% worse) and management of the economy (29% better, 39% worse).

#175. AI Agents Are Media’s New Favorite Audience

Every morning, Michelle Seo receives a briefing from Chattiana, her Muse artificial intelligence agent.

Chattiana’s briefing lists Seo’s agenda and to-do list, and plucks water-cooler topics from the headlines in her various newsletters along with links to the sources.

“I’m kind of challenging myself as an introvert to start more conversations,” Seo, a San Diego-based public relations consultant, tells WIRED. The newsletter, she says, gives her a sense of the daily news and options for filling an awkward silence on a Zoom call.

Seo is far from the first person to consume her news with an AI assist. In fact, the trend has become so common that some news outlets are starting to produce content that treats AI agents—not human readers—as the intended audience. This can look like anything from feeds optimized for AI to pieces structured to allow agents to create personalized articles.

“We believe that the media ecosystem is changing,” says Axios chief revenue officer Jacquelyn Cameron. “We see this with open web deteriorating. We see that search is shrinking. We see that hyperpersonalization is surging, that LLMs are becoming more important in the way that people are consuming” their news.

Major publishers have responded to that change in a variety of ways. Many, including WIRED’s parent company Condé Nast, have signed deals to allow AI platforms to surface their content. Outlets are grappling with “generative engine optimization,” or GEO, strategies to ensure articles appear in responses to AI prompts. Last year, AIOSEO, a SEO plug-in for WordPress sites, launched a feature that provides websites with LLM.txt files, which help AI discover and interpret sites’ content.

Others have taken a more defensive posture: The New York Times sued OpenAI and Microsoft in 2023 for copyright infringement, alleging that the companies illegally trained models on reporting. That case is still ongoing.

What Axios and a handful of others are trying, though, represents a more foundational shift in how the media operates—and who its stories are for.

“We have always said,” Cameron says, “that we want to be where the audience is.”

At the Digiday Publishing Summit in September, Axios announced the upcoming launch of Axios Direct: feeds designed for AI agents.

“We do believe that a significant portion of news consumption will move into AI systems, LLMs, and devices,” Cameron says. She describes the feeds as a more efficient way for agents to access Axios content, cutting back on narrative to prioritize structured data—more similar to a Bloomberg terminal than a series of articles.

Every’s founder Dan Shipper says the most common way Every’s content differs from a typical article is that its writers include prompts in pieces whenever possible, allowing readers to test concepts themselves. An article about a new model, for example, includes a series of prompts the reader can copy-paste into AI, demonstrating how the model answers questions and evaluates input.

Certain types of articles—such as guides—also include buttons that readers can press to “Read With Claude” or “Read With ChatGPT.” Clicking pulls up a prompt, instructing AI to “Start with a tight summary: one paragraph, bulleted. Then offer to go deeper.” Writers pack stories with details without worrying about losing readers’ attention, knowing agents will pick out the most relevant bits.

Two years ago, the outlet launched its first “extendable” article: a long-form feature accompanied by a database of reporting, from interview recordings to news articles to YouTube videos. Readers could query a chatbot, which would fetch additional details from the database.

“As a writer, you have to make choices about what goes into the story you’re telling,” Shipper says. “And there’s always a question about, are those the right choices? Or, are those the right choices for a particular reader?” With AI, journalists no longer choose.

“People are gonna do crazy, crazy stuff with this,” Shipper adds.

As outlets eye AI agents as a new audience, another question looms: Can publishers actually make money off of this?

Rasmus Kleis Nielsen, a professor of communication at the University of Copenhagen, says that while the market of publishing for agents is “emerging,” it remains immature and “extremely uneven.” Major questions remain, he says, about price points and how AI agents will interact with content in the future.

Cameron and Allison Murphy, Axios’ chief operating officer, are optimistic about AI as an audience proxy. According to the executives, Axios’s short-form, highly structured style has already made the publisher a favorite for AI to pull from, when responding to prompts. “Time is money for crawlers,” Murphy says. “They don’t want to have to go three quarters down the page to find the main point. So, that’s something that’s already benefiting us.”

But does AI crawling its content actually benefit Axios’ bottom line? Even if readers don’t actually visit the Axios homepage, the brand visibility in and of itself can be positive, Nielsen says. It’s not unlike publishers making content for social platforms like TikTok and Instagram that rarely bring viewers back to their websites.

Publications in general are grappling with how difficult it has become to monetize certain types of content when a chatbot can offer up answers on a silver platter. People only want to pay for information that other publications—and LLMs—don’t have. At Every, that means in-the-weeds guidance on AI and how to use it; at Axios Pro Deals, it’s speedy access to information that could move the market. An all-access Axios Pro Deals subscription costs $2,499 a year, while an annual Every subscription costs $288.

Shipper sees that $288 as a subscription for a human reader, along with their agents. “I may not want to read all of your stuff every month,” Shipper says. But if his subscription can include his agent, “I may want my agent to know it so that it can tell me, ‘Hey, this thing is important.’”

Murphy says that Axios’ starting point is one of acceptance: People are already using AI to absorb information, and that more people will be doing so in the near future.

“I also try to make it a little less existentially terrifying by thinking, this isn’t the first time this has happened in media,” Murphy says. People get their news via social media, via short-form video, via podcasts. “It isn’t really AI as the audience,” Murphy adds. “It’s AI as a tool that helps us reach our audience.”

#173. Astronaut busts out skateboard tricks in space

The International Space Station has a new skate spot.

NASA astronaut Jessica Meir posted a clip on X showing herself doing tricks on a fingerboard, a tiny Tech Deck-style skateboard, aboard the ISS. In the video, she rolls the mini board along the station’s blue equipment panels and metal rails, skating around Velcro patches and rolls of tape.

"Space skate!" Meir wrote, calling the clip a tribute to the skaters in her life back on Earth. She dedicated it to her brother, her husband, and their daughter, whom she called a "little ripper." Then she called out the GOAT himself and tagged skateboarding legend Tony Hawk to ask what trick he’d try in orbit.

Meir is now back on solid ground. She commanded NASA’s SpaceX Crew-12 mission, which spent 235 days on the ISS before splashing down in the Pacific off Southern California. The crew completed four spacewalks during the mission. It was Meir’s second long stay on the station, following 205 days in orbit from 2019 to 2020.

#172. Watch out, YouTube — Instagram goes from short-form to 90-minute videos

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

If you could have 1 million followers on any social media platform, which would you choose? There is a correct answer, and it’s YouTube, assuming you’d like to get paid for your work. But Instagram’s latest update could upend YouTube’s long-held position as the most lucrative platform for creators, if only Instagram plays its cards right.

Instagram announced on Monday that it’s testing a new type of video upload that will allow creators to share “Features,” videos up to 90 minutes long. After years in which every social platform (even LinkedIn!) aggressively pursued short-form video, this is a notable shift in strategy for Instagram.

Short-form, vertical video content has defined the internet of the 2020s, but the creators behind these clips don’t actually make much money directly from platforms like Instagram — the Meta-owned platform has some monetization tools like subscriptions, but it doesn’t typically pay creators on a per-view basis.

“You can get paid by the platform directly, you can get paid by a fan, and you can get paid by a brand,” Instagram head Adam Mosseri explained on the Colin and Samir Show, an interview show about the creator economy. “And right now, the way that it works on Instagram primarily is you primarily get paid by brands. Some people get paid by fans. We have still a small performance-based program, but … we spend more money than we make back by a long shot, and so it doesn’t really make sense to scale up because we’re just burning capital.”

YouTube’s payment model is more straightforward: There are ads on videos, and creators enrolled in YouTube’s Partner Program get 55% of the ad revenue YouTube earns from their videos. That adds up. The platform said last year that it paid more than $100 billion to creators, artists, and media companies in the previous four years.

Instagram and TikTok haven’t been able to replicate YouTube’s model with short-form content, which is inherently trickier to monetize than longer videos because there’s less room to place ads. Many creators see those accounts more like promotional arms for their YouTube channels than meaningful drivers of revenue.

Instagram is encouraging creators to upload longer videos, as it already did eight years ago with the now-defunct IGTV. It’s possible that its parent company, Meta, which makes its billions on advertising, will put ads in those long-form videos, too.

If Meta does add ads, could Instagram meaningfully compete with the YouTube Partner Program for the first time?

“It’s too early to share a long-term monetization model, but we’re exploring ways for creators to earn from brands, fans, and Instagram, including performance-based incentives,” a Meta spokesperson told TechCrunch. “Over time, we think there will be compounding benefits for creators to tell new stories, reach new audiences, and find new partners, which should lead to many monetization opportunities.”

It’s not a particularly promising answer, but it’s also not a no.

Mosseri explained in a video announcement why the platform is suddenly embracing long-form content after scrapping its old video product, IGTV.

“For a long time, I have been against doing long-form video on Instagram,” Mosseri said, noting that shorter content like Reels drives more shares and DMs among users. Instagram VP of Product Tessa Lyons, who has been advocating for this change, finally convinced Mosseri to give long form another shot.

“Reels is still going to be a core part of Instagram. But when you want to go deeper with the creators you love and the content that they’re creating, you should be able to do that on Instagram,” Lyons said in the video.

In other words, Instagram would rather keep fans on its own platform to enjoy both short- and long-form content from a creator than lose them to YouTube. Instagram is testing a feature called “Clips as Reels,” which lets creators make Reels out of their long-form content. The Reels link viewers to the longer video. Viewers can also save the longer video to send to the Instagram app on their TV.

Instagram is currently testing “Features” and “Clips as Reels” in Brazil and will now expand to the U.S. in a small test. The platform is also working on improved discovery features for Live video and a hub on a creator’s profile for episodic Reels, meaning a series of connected Reels similar to TikTok’s playlist feature.

“The thing that the team has been working on that we’re excited to share with you is trying to make it so that the short-form and the long-form video can be symbiotic,” Mosseri said. “We’re going to try to support these different types of formats, both in the mobile app, but also on the TV app, which I think is … going to take some time to get right. But I’m actually very excited about it, and we’re very committed to it long term.”

Prior to joining TechCrunch, she worked as a grassroots organizer, museum educator, and film festival coordinator. #171. Israeli election: A referendum on 'Bibi' Netanyahu?
