#170. Israeli elections have great consequences for Palestinians, but they don’t get to vote.

Election officials reported record turnout as Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s far-right coalition faced an opposition bloc led by a former military chief of staff, Gadi Eisenkot. A winner might not be immediately clear.

Aaron BoxermanDavid M. HalbfingerLeo Sands and Max Bearak

Polls have closed in an election that has seen Israelis vote in record numbers. In what is widely seen as the most consequential election in the country’s history, voters were deciding whether to keep Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and his far-right coalition in power or chart a new course.

By 8 p.m. local time (2 p.m. Eastern), two hours before polls were scheduled to close, 74.3 percent of eligible voters had cast ballots, the highest rate for that point in the day since 1988.

Turnout appeared to be particularly high among Arab citizens, who make up about a fifth of Israel’s population. By late afternoon, hours before the polls were set to close, turnout in some Arab-majority towns had exceeded the totals from the last election, in 2022.

Responding to the high turnout in those towns, Mr. Netanyahu accused Arab citizens of “trying to steal the elections,” repeatedly using brazen and overtly racial appeals to push his right-wing base to vote. An attempt by his Likud party on Tuesday to have voting hours extended was rejected by the Central Election Committee.

The election is Israel’s first since the Hamas-led attacks on Oct. 7, 2023, which was the deadliest day for Jews since the Holocaust and ignited three years of war that left Gaza largely in ruins and nearly all its population displaced.

Voters have a choice between two blocs offering starkly different visions. Mr. Netanyahu, the leader of the most far-right government in Israeli history, built a coalition of nationalists and ultra-Orthodox Jews, which faces an ideologically diverse group of Jewish opposition parties loosely led by Gadi Eisenkot, a former military chief of staff whose son was killed in the Gaza war. Parties representing Arab citizens of Israel also staunchly oppose Mr. Netanyahu’s government.

Both sides are framing the election as a make-or-break moment for the country. Mr. Netanyahu’s coalition offers the promise of a more religious Israel with an ever-tighter grip over the West Bank and Gaza. His opponents are casting the vote as the last chance to preserve democracy and pull Israel back from the brink of becoming a pariah state under international sanctions.

Voting will close at 10 p.m. (4 p.m. Eastern time), but there is unlikely to be an immediate determination of who will be the next prime minister. Opinion polls suggest that Mr. Netanyahu’s allies and rivals are roughly tied, and assembling a governing coalition in Israel’s parliamentary system could take weeks or months.

Opposition leaders led by Mr. Eisenkot have called for a reckoning over what they call Mr. Netanyahu’s failure to prevent the catastrophic Oct. 7 attacks. They also have pledged to repair Israel’s shattered image abroad, including by cracking down on Israeli extremist violence against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank.

Mr. Netanyahu has resisted the establishment of an independent commission to investigate the Oct. 7 attacks. He has vowed to move ahead with overhauling Israel’s judicial system, a move that critics say could undermine the country’s liberal democracy. And his allies say they hope to permanently entrench Israeli rule in the West Bank and Gaza despite international condemnation.

The 77-year-old leader has dominated Israeli politics for decades. In recent days, he repeatedly suggested that the country could face annihilation without him in charge.

Key issues: In a tumultuous campaign, several issues divided Israeli voters. Read more ›

West Bank and Gaza: As Israelis headed to the polls, Palestinians in the West Bank were under an Israeli military lockdown. Palestinians in the West Bank and Gaza cannot vote in Israeli elections, but they may have much to lose if Mr. Netanyahu’s coalition stays in power. Read more ›

Netanyahu sows doubt: Mr. Netanyahu used racially charged language to cast suspicion over high turnout among Arab citizens and to encourage his base to go to the polls. Election officials said Tuesday that there had been no indication of widespread irregularities.

Trump weighs in: President Trump has endorsed Mr. Netanyahu, praising him as an important partner in the war against Iran and saying anyone else would be the “wrong leader” for Israel. Analysts were skeptical that the U.S. president’s intervention would sway many voters.

Initial exit polls showed that against the odds, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party held a small lead over the party of Gadi Eisenkot, a former military chief, in Israel’s pivotal parliamentary election. But the anti-Netanyahu bloc, including two predominantly Arab parties, held a wide lead over the premier and his allies in exit polls published by Israel’s main television networks, meaning that no clear victor was yet in sight as the voting drew to a close Tuesday night.

The polls have now closed in Israel. People who are in line to vote will still be able to do so.

No party is expected to achieve a clear victory, which means the election will likely only be the first step in determining who will form the next government. Here’s how that might unfold.

The first projections of results in Israel’s election are about to be made public when the country’s major television networks publish their exit polls at 10 p.m. local time, when voting precincts close except to those already in line. Caveats abound: The exit polls have a spotty history of accuracy but give an initial sense of the relative strength of the parties. The closer fought the election, the riskier it is to place much stock in them. And this is just the beginning of what can be a drawn-out process to determine who will form Israel’s next government.

Polls have closed in Israel, and initial exit polls by Israeli broadcasters are expected to be released shortly afterward. But it will be hours before the definitive results are known, and it could be weeks until a new government is formed.

Early indicator: Exit polls in Israel can be useful to get a sense of each party’s relative strength, but they are not considered a reliable gauge of precise results — particularly in a tight race like this one.

Vote-counting: Votes are tallied in Israel by hand, with ballot slips tabulated at more than 12,000 polling places across the country. The counters are monitored by camera-toting election observers.

First results: After the hand count is completed, paper tallies are taken to regional hubs where the results are recorded electronically. The whole process takes hours, and the authorities begin to release the results once they are completed.

Possible delay: Israel is a parliamentary democracy, and no party is expected to win anywhere near an outright majority of the votes. Once the results are in, the process of forming a government will begin. That could take weeks or even months.

Next government: The party with the most votes and the most seats in the Parliament may not be the one to form a government. In 2021, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party won the most seats but was unable to assemble a majority coalition. Instead, after months of wrangling, an eclectic coalition of anti-Netanyahu forces cobbled together a single-seat majority in Parliament.

Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s governing coalition has the distinction of surviving for a full four-year term in office, something no government in Israel has achieved for more than 30 years. Since first taking office in 1996, he has tallied 19 years in power and become Israel’s longest-serving prime minister by far.

On Tuesday, he is seeking a seventh term, in Israel’s first parliamentary election since the deadly Hamas-led attack in 2023 that prompted its devastating war in Gaza.

The longevity of his most recent governing coalition followed three years during which a cycle of collapsed coalitions and five snap elections had left the country without a functional government or national budget.

In 2021, Israelis ousted Mr. Netanyahu from power and then returned him to office 18 months later. He had staged a previous comeback, in 2009, becoming prime minister a decade after his party was voted out of power.

In 2021, Mr. Netanyahu’s government collapsed after failing to pass a budget, setting off legislative elections. At the time, he had been indicted on charges of fraud, bribery and breach of trust, and the coalition he was leading was shaky. According to analysts, heading into an election was a gambit by Mr. Netanyahu to strengthen his grip on power before his criminal trial.

But the electorate that voted that March didn’t give him the seats he needed, ending a period of 12 straight years in office for him. It took months of wrangling for an eclectic coalition of anti-Netanyahu forces to cobble together a single-seat majority in the Parliament. Naftali Bennett, a right-wing former ally of Mr. Netanyahu’s, led the coalition as prime minister, hammering out a deal with Yair Lapid, a centrist and former broadcaster who was appointed foreign minister.

The eight-party coalition proved too unwieldy to endure. After two right-wing lawmakers defected and several on the left rebelled in critical votes, that government collapsed in June 2022.

After the resulting elections, held in November 2022, Mr. Netanyahu returned to power as right-wing voters moved to more extreme parties, diminishing the center left.

Mr. Netanyahu’s Likud party allied with far-right and ultra-Orthodox lawmakers to secure a clear majority in the Parliament, forming the most hard-line and religious conservative government in Israel’s history.

The coalition accounted for 48.4 percent of the vote but had a parliamentary majority because two anti-Netanyahu parties — one leftist, one Arab nationalist — fell just short of the share of votes needed to obtain seats.

It was a reminder of how tiny swings among voters can have a big effect under Israel’s parliamentary system.

Mr. Netanyahu has since overseen an acceleration of the country’s rightward shift, including an effort to overhaul the judiciary that prompted huge street protests. Questions about whether his government could have better pre-empted the Oct. 7 attack have also become hot-button issues.

In April, Mr. Lapid and Mr. Bennett said they would reconstitute their centrist alliance of 2021 for the 2026 election. Mr. Netanyahu will also face Gadi Eisenkot, a former military chief of staff who leads the largest opposition party.

When polls close in about half an hour, a frantic, low-tech counting process will begin. At each of roughly 12,000 polling stations, party representatives will begin counting ballot slips by hand, overseen by observers wearing body cameras. The paper tallies will then be transported to regional hubs, where the results will be recorded electronically. Only then, hours after polls close, will the authorities begin releasing results. The process is not expected to be completed before Thursday afternoon.

Days before Tuesday’s pivotal Israeli election, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu shocked his country by offering a bleak economic forecast that critics said could make Israel more like Iran, Russia and North Korea.

Mr. Netanyahu told an Israeli financial journalist on Friday that he was preparing for international sanctions against Israeli banks by drawing up plans for a “bypass economy” based on cryptocurrency. He also told Israelis to prepare for the end of billions of dollars of annual American assistance.

“It’s over,” he said. “It’s only a matter of time.”

The comments were a reflection of Israel’s deepening global isolation, with even once-staunch allies imposing or threatening economic sanctions and embargoes after years of war in Gaza and rampant violence by Jewish settlers against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank.

With settlers advancing into areas that were previously off limits, Britain last month decried what it called “ethnic cleansing” and announced trade sanctions on settlements that it said would also apply to entities that financed their construction. Though Israel’s banking system has not been directly targeted by sanctions, a dozen countries, including Canada, France and Spain, have said they support restrictions on trade with settlements, raising the prospect of further penalties that could affect Israeli financial institutions.

Opponents of Mr. Netanyahu’s government decried his pronouncements, which also drew a rebuke from the generally reserved central bank.

“Crypto currencies cannot serve as a route to bypass sanctions against Israel,” the Bank of Israel said in a statement. The bank added that the government would do better “to focus on preventing sanctions” and preserving Israel’s economic ties with its main trading partners.

Commentators said that Mr. Netanyahu’s outlook sounded apocalyptic, and some said it was part of a scare campaign to improve his election prospects.

Last year, amid growing global condemnation of the Israeli military’s conduct of the war against Hamas in Gaza, where it has been accused of war crimes, and the imposition of some arms embargoes, he warned that Israel would have to become a more self-sufficient “super-Sparta.” That reference to the ancient, insular Greek city-state that practiced militaristic self-reliance was, to critics, a frightening scenario for a small country whose economy is largely driven by a tech sector that is reliant on foreign investment.

Gadi Eisenkot, Mr. Netanyahu’s main rival in Tuesday’s election, said in a social media post that the prime minister’s “Bibi-coin vision,” a nod to cryptocurrency, would “dismantle the Israeli economy for generations.”

“No internet purchases, no high-tech investments, no national security,” he added.

Yair Lapid, the opposition leader who has served as prime minister and finance minister, wrote on social media that a vote for Mr. Netanyahu was a vote for “a ruined economy, sanctions that will lead to insane price hikes, and no military alliance with the United States.”

And a former governor of the Bank of Israel, Jacob Frenkel, told Israel’s Channel 12 on Sunday that moving away from the international financial system would turn Israel into “a leper.”

Mr. Netanyahu later walked back his remarks slightly, saying that he had heard the criticism and that cryptocurrency was only one option to deal with potential financial sanctions.

In a somewhat jarring contrast, Mr. Netanyahu’s campaign has argued that Israel’s economy overall is healthy despite years of war, helped by record export revenues, relatively low inflation and unemployment, and a strong shekel.

But many Israelis are not feeling it. The State Comptroller, a government watchdog, warned last year that the cost of living was “skyrocketing” in part because of structural challenges in Israel’s consumer market and low competition levels.

While foreign affairs and national security have dominated the political conversation ahead of the election, a recent survey by the Israel Democracy Institute, a nonpartisan research group, found that the economy was the top issue for many voters heading into Tuesday.

Both Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s bloc and the coalition of opposition parties are making a final push to urge Israelis to vote. Long waits at crowded polling places have been reported across the country, but the election committee has said anyone who is still in line at 10 p.m. will be able to vote.

The stakes of the Israeli elections are high for Palestinians in the West Bank, but they do not have the right to vote in them.

In interviews, some Palestinians voiced objections to not being allowed to vote, especially as they felt Israel’s occupation of the West Bank was becoming more permanent.

“The Israeli government has enormous influence over our lives,” said Montaser Amro, 32, a supermarket owner in Majd, a village near the southern city of Hebron. “It chooses when roadblocks open and close, it controls the borders, and it decides on how much of our economy functions.”

He added, “Why can’t we vote when settlers living hundreds of meters away can?”

Israel captured the West Bank in 1967 and reached an agreement with the Palestine Liberation Organization to establish the Palestinian Authority in the 1990s. The Palestinian Authority administers parts of the West Bank and has not held parliamentary or presidential elections for roughly 20 years.

Many Palestinians have long hoped the Palestinian Authority would transform into an independent Palestinian state. In recent years, those hopes have dwindled. The Israeli government promotes the expansion of settlements in the West Bank that are widely considered illegal under international law and rejects Palestinian self-determination there and in Gaza. Palestinian officials have also failed to unify their ranks and renew their leadership.

Alaa al-Khabass, 40, a resident of Hebron, said it was unjust that Palestinians could not cast ballots.

“Israel controls just about everything related to us, and I don’t see that changing any time soon,” he said. “We should be able to vote, but we live in painful reality where we lack rights and sovereignty.”

With just an hour still to go before Israel’s polls close, turnout has already surpassed the total turnout in the last several elections. Israel’s central elections committee said that 74.3 percent of eligible voters had cast a ballot by 8 p.m., the highest rate for that time since 1988. The polls are set to close at 10 p.m. local time, and Israelis are already tensely awaiting exit surveys which will finally give them a sense of who might soon be in charge of their country.

The election in Israel on Tuesday is unlikely to yield quick answers about who might lead the next government.

Voters don’t directly pick a prime minister in Israel’s proportional parliamentary system. They choose a party or slate, the first step in determining who may be selected to try to form a government. The process is not guaranteed to succeed and can lead to another election if it does not. Here are four things to watch for.

This is considered one of the most consequential elections in Israel’s history, and voters turned out in record numbers.

Recent polls had suggested that voter turnout could be at least as high or higher than in the last general election, in 2022. That year, slightly more than 70 percent of eligible voters — nearly 4.8 million out of nearly 6.8 million people — participated. That election followed four other votes in quick succession from 2019 to 2021 in which turnout hovered near 70 percent.

This year, there are 7.3 million eligible voters, about 540,000 more than in 2022.

Parties must clear a threshold of 3.25 percent of the total vote in order to get a seat in the Knesset, or Parliament.

But no party is expected to gain a majority of 61 parliamentary seats, according to the polls. That means there will likely be lots of post-election negotiations among those parties who pass the threshold.

Parties or lists that do not reach the threshold lose those ballots. As a result, parties and candidates lobby voters not to waste their votes on a list that seems unlikely to meet this criterion.

At least one party has also faced repeated attacks from Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party and the far-right Religious Zionism party led by Bezalel Smotrich, Israel’s finance minister.

The targeted party, People of Israel, is led by Ofer Winter, a former Israeli brigadier general with hawkish views on national security. Some polls show that much of Mr. Winter’s support comes from voters who would normally choose Likud. Mr. Winter has faced claims that a vote for his party will be wasted or may end up going to the leading challengers.

While Mr. Winter initially said that he would support Mr. Netanyahu, he has refused any deals to withdraw from the race and has since signaled the pledge may have been premature.

Israeli election officials have said there is no evidence of threats to the integrity of this election in terms of widespread fraud or vote counting irregularities. But experts are concerned about disinformation campaigns undermining the vote or thwarting turnout.

Critics fear that some moves by the governing right-wing coalition may cast doubt on the outcome even before the vote happens or may thwart turnout among some voters.

Absentee voting is mostly prohibited in Israel. But by some estimates, tens of thousands of Israelis living abroad were expected to fly in to vote, a factor that could potentially swing the outcome.

Likud had filed a lawsuit challenging an initiative to fly in Israeli voters from abroad — a move widely perceived as an effort to limit opposition voters from showing up. The party had said the nonpartisan program provided benefits that could sway voters in violation of Israeli law. But it withdrew the suit this month after the initiative’s organizers agreed to limit flights and not to defray travelers’ costs.

Likud and other right-wing parties also filed motions to limit participation of several Arab candidates in the election.

The Central Elections Committee can vote to bar candidates or lists on certain grounds, such as expressing support for armed conflict against Israel. But the nation’s Supreme Court gets the final say. This month, it rejected a challenge to the Joint List, a merger of three majority-Arab parties, and to Raam, a historically Islamist party led by Mansour Abbas, who is running with Yoav Segalovitz, a former deputy minister of national security who was previously in the centrist Zionist party There Is a Future.

In 2022, turnout in five of the largest Arab localities in Israel was lower, sometimes significantly, than the national figures. This year, officials in Arab communities worked to get voters to the polls in larger numbers and prepared for potential resistance.

Likud and the Religious Zionism party announced plans this month to deploy thousands of religious Jewish “observers” to “prevent fraud in voting and vote-counting in Arab communities.”

Critics contend that the presence of such observers, likely from far-right Zionist parties, was intended to intimidate Arab citizens of Israel, who make up 20 percent of the population and could be critical to the election’s outcome.

“The main issue we want to avoid is ‘fake news,’” said Abed Shahada, who leads voter turnout efforts for an organization representing mayors and other officials in Arab localities.

One major issue expected to drive voters is high crime in Arab communities. But if there are reports of or disinformation about criminal or violent episodes on Election Day that could keep people from the polls, Mr. Shahada said, teams are preparing by setting up a system to share verified information.

The Central Election Committee said it would not extend voting hours past the scheduled closing time. Earlier in the day, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party said it had asked the committee for an extension, even as Netanyahu called on his supporters to head to the polls. His chief opponent, Gadi Eisenkot, was opposed to extending the hours. Less than two hours remain before polls close.

Turnout data may help explain why Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu is worried about the pace of voting. With just over three hours until polls close, turnout in the five towns where his Likud party won its strongest support in 2022 was below the national rate by up to 10 percentage points.

Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu of Israel accused Arab citizens of “trying to steal the elections” on Tuesday, as he repeatedly used brazen and overtly racial appeals to push his right-wing base to vote.

“There are buses heading to the polls and long lines in Arab towns. Meanwhile, right-wing strongholds have yet to wake up!” he said on social media. “If we don’t go vote now, we will find ourselves with a government of Arab parties.”

Mr. Netanyahu has a long track record of appealing to his supporters by invoking the specter of the Arab vote. Arab citizens represent about 20 percent of Israel’s population, but have long complained that they are treated as second-class citizens and subject to discrimination.

In 2015, Mr. Netanyahu called on right-wing Israelis to vote because “Arabs were heading in droves to the polls.” His comments were widely condemned at home and abroad, including by the Obama administration. Mr. Netanyahu was accused of race-baiting and was forced to issue an apology for the remark.

On Tuesday, as initial data showed high turnout in Arab cities and towns, Mr. Netanyahu again used almost the same language to vilify Arab voters in several different statements over the course of the day.

“They say their voters are heading to the polls in droves. You too must head to the polls in droves,” Mr. Netanyahu said in a video statement, addressing right-wing voters.

Yousef Jabareen, who leads the Joint List alliance of Arab parties, accused Mr. Netanyahu of “inciting against Arab citizens and undermining the legitimacy of our vote.”

“I’m telling you: the Arab community knows how to punish you,” Mr. Jabareen said. “Today we’re sending you and your bloodstained government packing.”

Arab citizens have tended to vote at lower rates than their Jewish counterparts. But in the run-up to this election, surveys suggested that they would turn out in higher numbers in an effort to vote out Mr. Netanyahu and his far-right allies. In Rahat, a large Bedouin town in southern Israel, turnout just before 5 p.m. was already at 60 percent — matching the town’s final turnout in the previous election.

Mr. Netanyahu’s Likud party and some hard-right ministers in his government have also deployed incendiary messaging to appeal to supporters to vote.

The Likud party released images suggesting that there had been higher turnout in the Arab city of Rahat than Bat Yam, a Likud stronghold, which it called “a state of emergency.”

Another poster showed women in Muslim religious veils with the caption: “Right-wing voters, don’t stay home.”

Itamar Ben-Gvir, the far-right national security minister, joined in the prime minister in trying to stoke fears over high Arab turnout. In a video filmed near the entrance of the Arab town of Kufr Qassim in central Israel, he said that Arab voters were heading to the polls “at hitherto never-before-seen rates,” threatening Israel’s Jewish character.

The prime minister also pointed to the arrests of three people suspected of planning to bribe voters for Raam, an Arab Israeli party, on Tuesday. Dean Livne, the acting director of Israel’s central elections committee, told reporters that there were no reports of widespread irregularities.

Lia Lapidot and Johnatan Reiss contributed reporting.

Several people whose relatives were killed or taken hostage in the Hamas-led attack on Israel on Oct. 7, 2023, are running for office in Tuesday’s election, the first national vote since the assault, which shook the country’s foundations and ignited its longest war.

The October 2023 assault looms large over this week’s vote. There are sharp divisions over who was to blame for failing to prevent the worst military, intelligence and policy disaster in Israel’s history.

About 1,200 civilians and soldiers were killed that day, according to the Israeli authorities, and roughly 250 were taken hostage to Gaza. Many of the family members of hostages held by Hamas rose to prominence as they campaigned for their loved ones in television interviews and at nationwide protests.

Most of them called for Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu to take responsibility for the government’s failures of Oct. 7 and to prioritize negotiating the release of the hostages over continued fighting. Others supported Mr. Netanyahu’s right-wing government and urged it to keep up the military pressure on Hamas and Israel’s other enemies.

Parties from across Israel’s political spectrum have recruited candidates from what have become known as the Oct. 7 families. Here are some of the candidates with a chance to win a seat in Parliament:

Shira Shapiro is running with Yashar, the centrist party led by Mr. Netanyahu’s main political rival, Gadi Eisenkot. Ms. Shapiro’s son, Staff Sgt. Aner Shapiro, was on leave from the army on Oct. 7 when he attended the Nova music festival, one of the bloodiest targets of the Hamas-led attack. He took shelter in a crowded roadside bomb shelter and stationed himself by the entrance when gunmen attacked it. As the militants hurled grenades inside, Sergeant Shapiro repeatedly caught the grenades and threw them back out, until he was killed, according to surviving witnesses.

Zvi Mor, the father of a released hostage, Eitan Mor, is running as a candidate for the far-right party Religious Zionism, headed by Bezalel Smotrich. While Eitan was in captivity, Mr. Mor, an educator, founded a forum of right-wing parents of hostages and soldiers who opposed making deals with Hamas and pushed for Israel to defeat the group militarily.

Omri Ronen, a reserve soldier, is running with the left-wing Democrats, led by Yair Golan. His grandmother, Nira Ronen, was killed in her home in Kfar Aza, one of the border communities worst hit in the October 2023 attack, along with her Filipina caregiver, Angelyn Aguirre. Before Oct. 7, Mr. Ronen had been one of the leaders of a grass-roots group of reservists protesting the government’s plan to overhaul the judiciary. He said in a recent television interview that Mr. Netanyahu, whom he called “Mr. Oct. 7,” must go. “The word responsibility has meaning,” he said.

Rafael Ben Shitrit, a former mayor of Beit Shean in northern Israel, is in Yisrael Beitenu, a right-wing opposition party led by Avigdor Liberman. Mr. Ben Shitrit used to belong to Mr. Netanyahu’s conservative Likud party, but abandoned it after his son, Staff Sgt. Elroy Ben Shitrit, was killed in battle on Oct. 7 near the Gaza border. He has been an advocate for an independent state inquiry into what went wrong that day. Mr. Netanyahu has refused such an inquiry, and the opposition parties have pledged to establish one if they are elected.

Sharon Sharabi, also on Yisrael Beitenu’s list of candidates, is the brother of Eli Sharabi, a former hostage who was released from Gaza only to learn that his wife and two teenage daughters had been killed during the Oct. 7 attack. Another brother, Yossi Sharabi, was also taken hostage and killed after 100 days in captivity. Sharon Sharabi campaigned tirelessly for his brothers’ return. When Eli Sharabi was finally released, many Israelis were outraged over how emaciated he looked. “I was shocked,” Sharon said at the time. “I’d been told he would be thin, but I didn’t imagine how thin. It was extreme.”

Tali Gvili, running with Mr. Netanyahu’s Likud party, is the mother of Master Sgt. Ran Gvili, a member of a police counterterrorism unit who was shot and severely wounded in combat on Oct. 7 and then dragged into Gaza. His remains were recovered and returned to Israel in January 2026. The Gvili and Netanyahu families have become close, and the prime minister attended Sergeant Gvili’s state funeral, saying, “Our souls are bound with yours.”

Michal Hirsch Negri is running with Together, a joint slate led by right-wing politician Naftali Bennett and the centrist Yair Lapid. Ms. Hirsch Negri’s son Maj. Roee Negri was killed fighting in Kibbutz Be’eri near the Gaza border on Oct. 7.

Maj. Shahar Varon, a commander in the reserves, is another Together candidate. He fought that day in Kfar Aza, another border community, and later went to fight in Gaza. He was wounded in battle, then went back into Gaza to fight again. “There is life before October 7th, and there is life after,” he wrote recently, adding, “Our government disappeared that morning — but our people showed up instead.”

Israel’s Central Elections Committee just announced the latest voting data, which show Israelis are still turning out in large numbers. Voter turnout as of 6 p.m. local time is 66.7 percent, the highest for that hour of the day in decades, according to the committee, and nine percentage points higher than in 2022.

Sara Abu Sayum, 24, an Arab citizen of Israel who voted in the city of Lod.

For decades, Israel had virtually unanimous support among Republicans and Democrats in Congress, a reflection of a broader consensus in the United States in favor of strong backing for the Jewish state.

But that consensus began to fray several years ago and has recently threatened to unravel altogether as Democrats have shifted drastically and rapidly from unequivocal support for Israel, reflecting a sea change in Americans’ views.

Since the Hamas-led attack on Israel on Oct. 7, 2023, and the war in Gaza that followed, a growing number of Democrats have been under pressure from younger and progressive voters who are a crucial part of their base.

Lawmakers have begun rebuking Israel in ways that were once unimaginable. They have pointed to tens of thousands of Palestinian civilians killed in Gaza and focused much of their ire on Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu of Israel, whose actions and policies have left his country diplomatically isolated and who has become a political lightning rod in the United States. That has put at risk the bipartisan support for military aid that Israel has always counted on from American lawmakers.

A handful of far-right Republicans and libertarians in Congress have also questioned U.S. support for Israel, raising concern about Palestinian deaths and spending American money to defend a far-off ally. But the skepticism is far more widespread among Democrats.

The midterm elections have been shaped in part by Democratic voters’ mounting hostility toward Israel. Party leaders now view the issue as one of the major fault lines they will have to contend with next year if they win back control of Congress in November.

Over the past year, almost half of House Democrats voted to cut off aid to Israel. Democratic leaders who have long been staunch allies have said that the U.S.-Israeli relationship needs a fundamental reset, including a rethinking of the 10-year memorandum of understanding that commits billions of dollars of annual security aid to Israel, which will soon expire.

And Senator Chuck Schumer, Democrat of New York and the minority leader, who is the highest-ranking Jewish official in the nation, has condemned Mr. Netanyahu on the Senate floor and called for new leadership in Israel.

In recent weeks, top Democrats in the House and the Senate have proposed legislation that would impose sanctions on Israel for building settlements, making clear to Israeli companies and banks that they could be penalized for ties to such activity.

Those moves are in line with what lawmakers are hearing from voters.

A New York Times/Siena national poll conducted in May found that 74 percent of Democratic voters opposed providing additional economic and military support to Israel. A Pew Research Center poll in April found that eight in 10 Democrats and Democratic-leaning independents had an unfavorable view of Israel.

I’m in a suburb of Tel Aviv, visiting a civil-society-run election “war room,” where volunteers are working to safeguard the integrity of the vote.

Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party said in a statement that it had asked Israel’s Central Elections Committee to extend voting hours past the scheduled closing time of 10 p.m. The committee has the authority to do so, though it would be a highly unusual move.

Netanyahu has repeatedly urged his supporters to head to the polls, saying that right-wing strongholds are failing to turn out in sufficient numbers. The party of Gadi Eisenkot, Netanyahu’s leading opponent, said it strongly opposed an extension, arguing that Likud made the request in order to sow doubt about the election’s integrity.

In the run-up to Tuesday’s election Israel, perhaps the country’s most intensely scrutinized vote ever, the authorities and civil society groups mobilized to monitor the balloting, which many fear could be undermined by political interference and disinformation.

In an office building north of Tel Aviv, dozens of volunteer tech workers and lawyers planned to squeeze into a “war room” to field reports from thousands of volunteers watching polling sites for violence or obstruction. The effort is organized by antigovernment protest groups that say their goal is not to encourage voting for any political party but to ensure safe access to polls.

In Jerusalem, officials from the Central Election Committee are overseeing an extensive Election Day operation from Parliament. Committee employees are fanning out to more than 12,000 polling stations wearing body cameras to document irregularities. A desk operated jointly with Shin Bet, Israel’s domestic security agency, is scanning the web for disinformation to respond to it in real time.

“This election is the most threatened ever,” said Assaf Shapira, a researcher at the Israel Democracy Institute, a nonpartisan think tank. “The stakes are very high,” he said.

Some experts point to concerns, including the growing sophistication and prevalence of A.I.-generated content, a police force that critics say is increasingly loyal to the far-right minister overseeing it and a sitting Israeli government whose senior officials have repeatedly suggested the vote could be rigged.

The Central Election Committee, a partly political body chaired by a Supreme Court justice and whose administrative branch is composed of nonpartisan officials, is working to bolster confidence in the process and counter false claims of fraud, Dean Livne, the committee’s acting director general, said in an interview.

Tallies from each polling station will be made public within hours of the polls closing, and the counting of roughly 600,000 ballots cast remotely at places like hospitals, embassies, and military bases will be live streamed for nearly 24 hours, according to Mr. Livne.

“This election will be the most transparent ever,” he said.

Election observers agree that the risk of widespread fraud or manipulation of the vote count is exceedingly low. And as of Tuesday afternoon, election officials said there had been no indication of widespread irregularities. But some experts also say that government officials could be laying the groundwork to undermine confidence in the election so that they can then challenge an unfavorable result as rigged.

Throughout the campaign, members of Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s Likud party have questioned the impartiality and qualifications of election officials and suggested that antigovernment groups were plotting to obstruct the vote.

“To challenge the legitimacy of elections, you have to plant the seeds early on,” Yael Shomer, a political scientist at Tel Aviv University, said. “And I think we see them planting the seeds.”

Senior government officials have stopped short of explicitly threatening to reject the election result. Mr. Netanyahu recently said that he would stand to lose the election if too few of his supporters turned out to vote.

Observers warn of disinformation campaigns and attempts to intimidate some voters and deter them from casting their ballots on Election Day.

Such attempts, opposition figures and some experts worry, could involve the Israeli police, which is increasingly seen as serving the interests of the far-right national security minister, Itamar Ben-Gvir. Some fear that on Election Day, officers could look the other way at pro-government violence or attempts to intimidate voters outside polling stations in areas considered hostile to Mr. Netanyahu and Mr. Ben-Gvir.

Israel’s police commissioner, Danny Levy, has vowed to defend the integrity of the vote and said he would dismiss officers who express political views while on duty.

Concerns about voter intimidation are particularly high for Israel’s Arab citizens, who make up roughly 20 percent of the population and typically vote for parties opposed to Mr. Netanyahu’s. Turnout in Arab communities has historically been low, and they have been targeted with largely unsubstantiated allegations of widespread voter fraud by Mr. Netanyahu and his political allies.

Earlier this month, Bezalel Smotrich, Israel’s far-right finance minister, announced plans to deploy thousands of religious Jewish “observers” to “prevent fraud in voting and vote-counting in Arab communities.” Critics fear that could instigate chaos and discourage Arab citizens from going out to vote.

Officials in Arab localities are organizing teams to share verified information with their communities to combat disinformation intended to deter voting, including false reports of polling station closures or violence around voting sites, said Abed Shahada, who leads an effort to increase turnout for an organization representing Arab local officials.

“The threats are greater, but there is also a greater awareness of them,” he said. “We’re trying to have one confront the other.”

One noticeable trend is strong turnout in some of Israel’s largest Arab-majority towns. In Rahat, a Bedouin town in southern Israel, turnout stood at 60 percent just before 5 p.m. That was the town’s final turnout in the previous election once polls closed at 10 p.m. In Nazareth, it was roughly 46 percent, two percentage points below its final turnout in 2022.

I’m in Har Nof, an ultra-Orthodox neighborhood in Jerusalem, which is a stronghold of the Shas party that has long been in a tight political alliance with Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

Israelis are continuing to vote in large numbers, reflecting how many have said the election is a turning point for the country. More than 55 percent have voted as of 4 p.m. local time, the largest tally at that hour since the elections committee began tracking incremental turnout in the 1970s.

Shoshi Ashtbeker, 47, a school secretary from Tel Aviv, referring to Benjamin Netanyahu, whom she said she’d voted for all her life.

Arab voters could tip the scales as Israelis turned out in large numbers to vote on Tuesday in one of their country’s most consequential elections in decades.

Arab citizens, who make up about a fifth of Israel’s population, have historically voted at significantly lower rates than Jewish Israelis in national elections. But pollsters, researchers and activists expected Arab voters to turn out in force, driven to unseat a right-wing government that has enacted aggressive policies against them.

Arab Israelis are overwhelmingly Muslim, with Druse and Christian communities making up around 15 percent. Palestinians living in the Israeli-occupied West Bank are not Israeli citizens and cannot vote.

Recent polls suggest that neither Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s right-wing alliance nor the opposition bloc is likely to form a majority without a partnership with Arab parties. Such a stalemate would leave Arab leaders with huge influence on the results.

“The stakes are too high to sit this one out,” said Sireen Jabareen, 30, a director of a rights organization that helps Arab prisoners. She said she would vote for the first time after previously boycotting the political system, which she views as discriminatory toward Arabs. “I don’t want to be the kind of person who clings to rigid principles when reality changes,” she added.

Many others have also stayed home on Election Day in the past, alienated by political leaders, convinced their ballots hold no sway or unwilling to validate a system that they say treats them as second-class citizens.

Arab politicians and local leaders are warning of voter-suppression efforts, through either disinformation campaigns or the presence of armed right-wing activists near polling stations in Arab villages.

Last week, Mr. Netanyahu’s Likud party announced a joint effort with another right-wing party to send 2,000 activists to oversee “voting integrity” in Arab cities and towns. The move was widely seen as an attempt to suppress Arab voters.

In the 2015 election, Mr. Netanyahu sought to prod supporters by saying that Arab voters were “heading to the polls in huge quantities.” After critics accused him of race-baiting, he apologized for any offense caused by the remark.

But on Tuesday, he tried a similar tactic, pointing to apparently high turnout among Arab voters and suggesting that they were “trying to steal the elections.” (Election authorities said there had been no indication of widespread irregularities.) Mr. Netanyahu’s Likud party released a poster showing a crowd of women in hijab, urging right-wing Israelis “not to stay home.”

Yousef Makladeh, a leading pollster focusing on Arab society in Israel, said recent surveys indicated that 65 percent to 70 percent of Arab voters planned to vote on Tuesday. That would be an increase of 12 to 18 percentage points over turnout in the 2022 election.

For the very first time, activists say, efforts to turn out the vote feel like pushing on an open door.

Omar Abu Siyam, a social media personality from Mu’awiya, an Arab village near Haifa, Israel, said that when he told his nearly 90,000 Instagram followers that he had changed his mind and decided to vote, hundreds of commenters responded that they felt the same way.

The most critical issue for Arab voters by a wide margin, Mr. Makladeh said, is the surge in violent crime within their communities, a crisis many say the police have deliberately neglected under Itamar Ben-Gvir, the national security minister.

“Arabs are terrified of what four more years with Ben-Gvir in office will do to us,” said Dr. Thabet Abu Ras, a political geographer who is advising voter-turnout initiatives across Arab municipalities. He added that discriminatory housing policies, resulting in a record number of home demolitions in Arab communities, were also driving voters.

Higher Arab turnout could threaten smaller parties that are more likely to support Mr. Netanyahu’s coalition. By increasing the total number of votes cast, it would raise the threshold for smaller parties to win seats in the Knesset.

“This time,” Dr. Abu Ras said, “the Arab vote is worth gold.”

Israelis are voting in what many view as one of the most pivotal elections in the country's history.

It is the first parliamentary election since the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attacks and subsequent conflicts across the region - in Gaza, Iran, Lebanon, Syria and Yemen.

BBC Middle East correspondent Yolande Knell explains why it may take some time before a new government is formed after the polls close.

#168. Ukraine and Russia begin Oleshky ceasefire for aid mission

Around 2,000 people remain stranded in the city of Oleshky, facing severe shortages of food and medicine. The Russian-occupied town has been devastated by fighting and flooding since Moscow's invasion in 2022.

Russia and Ukraine began a localized ceasefire early Tuesday to allow humanitarian aid into the Russian-occupied town of Oleshky in southern Ukraine.

Both sides confirmed that the truce took effect at 3 a.m. local time (0100 GMT), following an agreement brokered by US first lady Melania Trump.

Russia's Defense Ministry said its forces had stopped shelling in the Kherson sector to prepare for the humanitarian operation.

Ukraine's human rights commissioner Dmytro Lubinets also confirmed that Kyiv was observing the agreement.

"As head of the Ukrainian negotiating team, I confirm that the Ukrainian side is honoring its commitments," he wrote on Facebook.

However, it could not be independently verified whether fighting had decreased.

Around 2,000 people, including up to 50 children, remain trapped in Oleshky, according to Ukrainian and US estimates. The town had about 24,000 residents before Russia's full-scale invasion in February 2022.

The United Nations says residents face severe shortages of food and medicine. Mines and drone attacks have made it difficult for them to leave.

Former residents told news agency AFP earlier this year that people remaining in the town were starving, sometimes hunting pigeons or surviving on food grown in their gardens.

"They've forgotten what bread is. You need flour for bread, and there's not even flour left," said Olga, who was in contact with two families still in Oleshky.

Oleshky lies across the Dnipro River from the Ukrainian-controlled city of Kherson.

Russian troops captured the town during the first days of the invasion in 2022. It suffered further destruction in June 2023 when the collapse of the Kakhovka dam caused widespread flooding.

The White House said last week that Melania Trump had helped secure the agreement after holding talks with civilian negotiators from Russia and Ukraine.

Her office said the first lady had also met United Nations officials who briefed her on conditions in Oleshky.

Announcing the agreement on Friday, Trump said civilian officials from both countries would visit residents to assess their needs.

Doctors, food, drinking water and medicine are expected to reach the town before evacuation corridors are opened.

"Residents who wish to evacuate will receive coordinated support and transportation to their preferred destination. I anticipate some will want to go to Russia, and some to Ukraine," Melania Trump said.

Ukraine has accused Russia of preventing residents from leaving, an allegation Moscow denies.

Neither side has provided details about who will monitor the ceasefire. Reports have also differed over its duration, with one account saying it could last through Saturday.

Russian missile attacks continued elsewhere in Ukraine as the localized ceasefire began.

Explosions shook Kyiv shortly after 3 a.m. local time Tuesday, according to witnesses, while air raid alerts sounded across several regions.

Kyiv Mayor Vitali Klitschko said the attacks had damaged the Southern Bridge and associated subway infrastructure. City authorities said traffic on the bridge had been suspended.

On Monday, a Russian missile attack on Kyiv killed at least one person and injured 10 others, including students at a dormitory. Further attacks caused casualties in Kharkiv and Pavlohrad.

"It is important that the world sees that Russia is showing nothing but a desire to kill and continue waging war," Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy wrote after the attacks.

The limited truce in Oleshky comes ahead of another round of US-mediated peace talks between Washington, Moscow and Kyiv, expected in November.

Negotiations have been effectively frozen since the United States and Israel launched joint strikes on Iran in February.

Previous attempts to establish ceasefires have repeatedly failed amid deep mistrust between Russia and Ukraine.

More than four and a half years after Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, hundreds of thousands of soldiers and civilians have been killed, while millions have been forced to flee their homes.

#167. Greens push for human override in aged care assessment after ombudsman’s scathing report into algorithm tool

Investigation recommends government changes legislation so staff assessing people for home support packages can correct the outcome

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Greens leader, David Shoebridge, has used a scathing report from the commonwealth ombudsman to press the government to use the last two sitting weeks of parliament to restore human override to its “unfair” aged care assessment tool.

The 104-page ombudsman report, published on Monday, recommends the government change legislation so that aged care workers assessing older Australians for home support packages can amend the outcome when the algorithm-driven assessment tool makes an incorrect decision on funding.

Known as the Integrated Assessment Tool (IAT), the ombudsman found it is leading to unfair outcomes, and has undermined the public’s confidence in aged care. Assessors meet older people, complete a questionnaire about their needs, and an algorithm then decides whether funding should be allocated, and at what level.

“People are tearing their hair out at what’s happening,” Shoebridge said.

“We’ve got aged care workers who are trying to help their clients … and the computer just says no. The ombudsman has told us now that this has been unfair from the moment it was conceived of. That it’s producing arbitrary and unfair outcomes for people who are at a stage of life where they desperately need help and support.”

State health officials were so alarmed by algorithmic changes to the IAT, introduced in November, that they kept a log of hundreds of cases where vulnerable older Australians were severely under-assessed, Guardian Australia previously revealed.

In a joint statement, the aged care minister, Sam Rae, and the health minister, Mark Butler, said the government will “consider the Ombudsman’s report carefully” and are acting on some of its recommendations. The government has commissioned an independent review of how the IAT assesses and prioritises people.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Of the 10 recommendations made, six were accepted and two, including instating human override for assessors and amending the aged care legislation, were noted.

“We have two more sitting weeks of parliament, where the Senate and the House of Reps can come to some arrangement and put in place a human override for aged care,” Shoebridge said.

Rae said that a new escalation pathway had already been introduced, allowing a wrong outcome to be escalated for review by a delegate, “adding a further layer of human oversight and nuance to the assessment system”.

“Our response aligns with the principles of the ombudsman’s report, allowing for human discretion to overturn assessment decisions that are not fit for purpose in a way that is fair and responsible for all older people.”

The commonwealth ombudsman, Iain Anderson, said: “I think that assessors being able to change decisions because they appear to be clinically inappropriate is really important, and so I am concerned that the escalation pathway will not necessarily do that”.

An aged care assessor with more than four decades of nursing experience, who spoke to Guardian Australia on condition of anonymity, said escalation pathways are not good enough.

“The delegate has not sat in front of that older person,” she said. “The delegate has not laid eyes on that person. I have.” She said while she escalates cases to a delegate in her workplace, who received special training to review cases, this adds time and administrative work.

She said she and her colleagues are experiencing severe anxiety and distress due to their work.

“I’m too scared to ‘accept’ on the algorithm’s decision, but I have no choice,” she said. “It feels like I’m telling the algorithm and the government, ‘great choice’ and sometimes I need to walk away from the computer because I can’t hit the button.

“It’s not Sam Rae or Mark Butler that then has to face the client. They’re not standing there after spending hours with clients, some of them in tears, and then telling them what the outcome is. I want the ministers to come to work with me and see what is happening.”

She said she has become “so anxious, and I get so agonised over every question that I ask my clients, that it puts me into a situation where I’m not finishing my work on time, I’m not reaching my KPIs, I’m working weeknights and weekends, and I am considering leaving this job.”

The shadow aged care minister, Anne Ruston, told the ABC’s Radio National on Tuesday that “the government needs to actually put proper human override back in,” describing the escalation pathway as “only a Band-Aid on a symptom”.

Independent senator David Pocock accused the government of “resisting attempts to fix a problem it created”.

“The Senate has offered a solution, the Ombudsman has now backed it, and the government needs to explain why it’s still standing in the way,” he said.

“The Ombudsman’s findings are extraordinary, including a finding that the department responsible for designing this system didn’t even understand the legal framework it had created.”
