#166. UK Politician Jess Asato Got Deepfaked. Now She’s Taking xAI to Court

Few people understand the current deepfake epidemic better than Jess Asato. The British Labour MP has spent years campaigning against the use of technology to abuse women and girls. Then, after calling out Elon Musk during the notorious Grok Deepfake Scandal of 2025, Asato found herself caught in the very crisis she had been trying to prevent.

Disturbing and degrading deepfake images of the MP circulated widely. One appeared to depict a sexual assault. When Asato reported them to X, the platform repeatedly dismissed her complaints. So she decided to take the fight somewhere else: the courts. She is now pursuing legal action against Musk’s xAI, arguing that it unlawfully used her image without her consent. The case could help establish a precedent for holding AI companies accountable when their products cause harm.

For Asato, this is personal, but it is hardly new territory. Before entering Parliament, she spent years working with charities supporting survivors of domestic abuse and vulnerable children. That experience has shaped her political career, informing her push for stronger protections for women and children online. Now, she is confronting more troubling questions: What happens when the technology enabling abuse becomes ubiquitous, the platforms hosting it refuse to intervene, and the people targeted are left to fight back on their own?

At stake for Asato is more than the misuse of a single image. It is whether consent still means anything when AI can manufacture almost anything, and whether the companies building these tools can be made to answer for what they do. Asato is betting that the law can catch up with the technology. And she is prepared to take on Musk to find out. (xAI did not immediately respond to WIRED’s request for comment.)

Also, before you read on, be aware that this interview includes references to sexual assault and child sex abuse.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

ROSIE SWASH: Jess, I just want to have a couple of warm-up questions before we get into it. Tell us a bit about your background, how did you end up becoming MP for Lowestoft?

JESS ASATO: Sure. So, I grew up in East Anglia in Norfolk and Suffolk, and in a not very political family. I got the politics bug in 1997 when Tony Blair was talking about education, education, education. Having experienced it, I felt that we could be doing a lot better in our education system. So I joined the Labour Party and got involved with my local groups.

Before Parliament, I worked for the largest children's charity in the UK for three years on their policy and public affairs, trying to change legislation on behalf of the most vulnerable children in the country. Six years prior to that I worked for a domestic abuse charity called SafeLives where we did huge amounts of work to try and improve the situation for survivors of domestic abuse, particularly through the Domestic Abuse Act.

Those twin issues of women and children have become my focus in Parliament, and I’ve been very clear that my reason for being here, my mission really, is to be a voice for women and children.

Well, that’s a good entry point for us to start talking about this year. So in early 2026, there was widespread reporting on Grok, xAI’s chatbot, being used to create sexualized images of children and women, and those images being circulated on X. How aware were you at this point of the phenomenon of deepfakes being used in this way?

Very aware, and actually had been working with survivors who had experienced deepfakes for quite a number of years. Ever since the advent of AI this has been happening to women, and there’s also been developing risks of children both being put into child sexual abuse material and also children using nudification tools on other children, thereby criminalizing themselves because these tools are so easy to access and often free.

So I was already very alive to the huge harms being created by AI deepfakes of both women and children before Grok ever happened. It was a known harm, which is why many of the tools from other companies had already taken steps to stop them from being used to create this kind of content.

There are a number of tools where, if you type in “nudify,” or “take the clothes off this person,” the tool will refuse to do that. So into that environment came Grok.

Was there a particularly egregious example or incident prior to Grok when you became aware of deepfakes that really stuck with you?

There is a brilliant campaigner who goes by the name of Jodie. I think it was probably listening to the experiences of Jodie, who was deepfaked, and it was such an affront to her that somebody would do this, and then she found out it was actually a friend. Such a betrayal of trust.

These things destroy not just the individual involved, but often families and everyone around them too.

For people who don’t know, who exactly was Jodie? Is this a constituent of yours or someone who came forward to you specifically?

No, no, no. She's a very well-known campaigner on this issue. That’s not her real name.

I know it sounds obvious, but what exactly is nudification?

It is where you ask an AI tool to take the image or video of a real person and use generative AI to make that person either nude, partially nude, or engaging in pornographic acts. This is something that, because of the photorealistic nature of AI images, it has become increasingly hard to see what is a fake image.

It can often look incredibly, incredibly real.

So what was it particularly about the Grok scandal earlier this year that made you want to raise this in the House of Commons?

It was the fact that we already knew that you could stop this from happening by designing the AI tool to reject the ability to do this.

This was completely avoidable. That’s why I spoke out, but also because I’d just spent a year and a half campaigning to get a ban on nudification apps, and here was an all-purpose AI tool becoming a nudification app.

It just felt to me as though we were making strides in the UK, and then the world’s richest man and his product decided to turn the clock back.

So what exactly did you stand up and say?

What I did was I talked about the need for the government to accelerate its ban on nudification apps, which it did. So the then secretary of state for science, innovation and technology, Liz Kendall, said that she was going to bring that ban into law through the Crime and Policing Act, which she did.

But I also said that it was very important that we went further than that, because, of course, Grok itself is not a sole-purpose nudification tool. It has a general use, and therefore we needed to bring all AI chatbots under the auspices of the Online Safety Act. That is also what eventually happened in the Crime and Policing Act, and we’re now waiting until that legislation comes into effect. There’s got to be a consultation, I understand, before it does.

So technically another Grok could happen in this period, but in the future there would be much stronger measures that we could take against tools like Grok creating nudification content.

So that’s a really positive outcome of your interjection in Parliament, but what else happened as a result of you standing up?

Well, one of the things that happened was that I posted on social media saying that Elon Musk should stop Grok from being able to do this.

And then, of course, I became a victim of the same trend. So Grok put this woman in a bikini. And most of the images were sort of pornified, I would say. They weren’t nude images, but they were me with inflated boobs in a bikini.

Then people tried to make it funny, I guess, by putting a swastika on me or hammer and sickles. Or making me pregnant with quintuplets and putting me in a kitchen. Because of course that’s the only use for women—birthing and making dinner. But the really disturbing one was a video of me being surrounded by a group of men, chloroformed over my mouth, and then I fall backwards onto a chair. Then my skirts are lifted up. So no nudity, but clearly the next frame is meant to be a sexual assault. So it was really distressing and disturbing.

That was the point at which a friend said, “You probably need to reach out to a lawyer.” Because each of these things we reported to X, and none of them contravened their terms and conditions. We just kept getting those sort of repeat emails coming back. “We’ve looked into this, and there’s nothing to see here.” So I did engage a lawyer, who sent a letter to X to ask for these to be removed, and they were.

But it was at that point that he said, “I think you might actually have a case. We think that xAI have broken the law.”

It’s impressive how you handled it, but it must have been really awful.

It was; it was awful. This is done to demean, to degrade, and to push women out of online spaces. From my perspective, I tend to use bad or difficult things that happen as a way of thinking about how we change this for the future.

My old boss was an amazing woman called Tessa Jowell. She used to say to me, “How do we change this negative energy into something positive?” “How do we work our way out of this into a place where we actually feel that we’ve learnt from it?”

So I guess she instilled in me that sense that when something bad happens you’ve got a choice. You can wallow in it, you can try and forget about it, or you can use it as a catalyst for change.

So I was quite trepidatious about the idea of taking on a legal action. Not least because of the person who is at the top of xAI, now SpaceXAI. [Eds. note: The company recently rebranded itself as SpaceXSI.] He’s the world’s richest man. But also because I didn’t want myself to necessarily be the story. Many, many women had things that were created of them through both Grok and other nudification tools that are much, much worse.

Having said that, I knew that if there was the opportunity to say that actually they’d broken the law, there was an important point of principle on behalf of all of the women and the children whose images were manipulated and misused by Grok, who would never, ever be able to bring a legal case. So I decided that I wanted to do this, because I wanted to be able to show that no one is bigger than the law.

Doesn’t matter how rich you are or how powerful you are or how big a reach you have in terms of your social media presence or who your friends might be at the top of various governments. If you break the law in our country, you need to feel the force of that. As an ex-lawyer and now as a legislator, it is important that we uphold our law where it has been broken.

So it’s also about giving control back, I think, to us as individuals, but also as a society. So many of these digital changes have been done without our consent or say, and I think there’s a feeling that there’s a sort of runaway train that we’re all on, whether we want to be or not, because we all need to use online spaces for our work, for connection.

And so my view is that we should be expecting companies to respect our law or at least make new laws to bring them more into line with what society really needs.

You said that you gave it some thought. In that moment of trying to decide how to respond, what are you weighing up? Who are you talking to? Are you going back and talking to your team or your family to work out how you should respond, knowing that it may invite further speculation and further attention?

My family mostly, my husband in particular. It’s one thing being a politician and arguing your case to change the law on behalf of women and children; it’s another having your own legal case.

So I was worried about the impact that that might have on my family, my children. I also know that this can bring danger to politicians. So I worried about the fact that I am obviously engaging and making the defendant somebody who has got this very wide reach on social media. However, I don’t like bullies. I very strongly believe that it’s important to fight on the field that you’ve got, and this was an opportunity I felt to make a difference.

So I weighed that up and thought that it was worth having that kind of attention and potential danger, because there is a bigger issue here that we need to get right.

When you say you don’t like bullies, are you talking about, let’s face it, men who used Grok in this way in the first place, or are you talking about Elon Musk?

I’m talking about the men who use this as a form of trying to push women out of the online space. Actually it was quite clear in quite a lot of the comments that they were saying, “Well, if you don’t like this, then get off the platform.”

Either that or, “If this is too hot for you, get out the kitchen,” which I always find quite funny because they put me in a kitchen. I think it’s really interesting when you look at X in terms of its demographics in the UK, it’s highly weighted toward men. I think some of that’s down to the fact that there is just so much misogyny and abuse on it. So I’m assuming, and correct me if I’m wrong, that the other side of the response to going public is that other women have come forward to you.

Absolutely. Actually, one of the heartening things has been people coming forward and saying, “Thank you, because this has now given me the license to actually accept that what happened to me wasn’t my fault. It’s not my shame.” So, in a way, what I’m hoping is that this has helped some victims to process what has happened to them and understand that this was not right.

I’ve also had emails from parents where they’ve said that this has happened to their daughter. It’s been done by boys at school. I’ve had teachers saying that boys have made pornographic AI imagery of them. And it’s something where we’ve just sort of let it happen and wash over us and accept that somehow this is now a part of the society we live in.

So yes quite a number of people have come forward. Obviously not all of their experiences have been through Grok, and I think one of the things is that often, when you see these films or images, you don’t know what platform they’ve been created on. I mean, the only reason why I knew it was Grok was because it was integrated with X, and you could see the commands that had been given by the users.

But much of the time, when this content is created, most victims don’t know by whom or on what platform. And so there’s really very little they can do about it. And I think it’s that sort of sense of helplessness that you have been violated, and that is the word that comes up so frequently from victims of this particular issue.

It’s not just that it’s nudified. Of course, much of my content wasn’t. It’s the lack of consent. And lots of people say, “Oh, but it’s fake. It’s just a laugh.” But what people don’t understand is that it is hyperrealistic, and it’s your face.

It’s a piece of your soul. It’s not just pixels on the internet. It is a person. And because obviously I’ve worked for a long time in violence against women and girls and spoken to many victims of sexual assault and to children who’ve been victims of child sexual abuse, it is the lack of consent that is so disturbing.

It can leave people with PTSD and quite deep trauma. And there have been a number of people I’ve met who have said that the world seems less safe to them because this has happened to them.

I think you’ve already touched on it, but what is the biggest myth around deepfakes that you would wish to dispel?

There is clear harm, even when it’s not done to demean or degrade. So even if it’s done sometimes by friends, the fact is that you haven’t consented to that. There is something about the human psyche which is that when you see yourself so realistically created in a different situation or scenario, something that’s not something you would have done, I think it’s quite discombobulating.

I think your brain can’t quite process it because your brain knows it’s not true, but it looks very true. And I think psychologically we still don’t really understand how this content is going to change the way we think about our world, how we know what is true and what isn’t true.

Now, of course, you can do fantastic things with generative AI. All I’m calling for is that we respect people’s consent, and that’s why one of the things I’ve been working on since this happened is to look at whether we could create a system of consent to be manipulated by AI.

Could we create a greater right over our likeness online so that there’s a new social norm, which is that no, you can’t copy and paste that picture into an AI chatbot and do whatever you want with it. That’s not yours. It’s not your data. It’s their data, and you’re breaching the law if you do that.

I think if we were to do that, we could have a wide range of applications. It wouldn’t just be in this space of the sexualization of women and children. But also with political disinformation. We are so close to a moment where somebody produces a hyperrealistic video of a politician saying something that they didn’t, and then that leads to real-world harm. And so we’ve got to find a new social norm that says unless for public figures it’s parody or satire—and absolutely you’ve got to protect that—but in all other instances your picture, your voice, and your moving image is your personal data, and it’s not for anyone else to use.

So that’s interesting because you’re touching on new social norms and the possibility of new laws to keep up with this technology. But your lawsuit is specifically under a data law. Talk to me about that versus a law around consensual imagery or deepfakes specifically.

So, my case is brought under existing law, the GDPR, the data protection law, and it contends that Grok misused my data without my consent. It didn’t have consent to process my data, the data being my face and my likeness. But also under privacy laws that I had a reasonable expectation of privacy and that particularly the video of me being prepared for sexual assault reaches a criminal threshold and that nobody should be expected to be on the receiving end of.

Let’s remember, this is actually a product that has been designed by software engineers and developers, and that actually products should not lead to an invasion of privacy and the harm that I then experienced as a result of that, including distress and a sense that I was being harassed by this particular creation of this particular video.

So, it rests on good solid legal grounds, and we await the defense from SpaceX AI.

I don’t know how much you can say, legally, but what part of the process are you in? What happens next? What’s the timeline for a resolution to this case?

So like with a lot of legal cases, it’s very, very hard to pin down a timeline. But my understanding is that we should be receiving the response and the defense from SpaceXAI in the near future. But we don’t have a deadline or a particular time that I can put on it.

After that, my understanding is that the case will then be looked at, and if there is a case to answer, then it will go to a court hearing. But once again, no idea when that might be. And I think there’s lots of legal things that I don’t understand that need to be worked out in the meantime as well between my lawyers and their lawyers.

Have you ever had any acknowledgment on X itself from Elon Musk or anyone at xAI about this case? Has he ever spoken publicly about it?

I don’t think so. They clearly want to challenge this case, and so the best place to do that, I think, is in the courts and to leave it to see whether the merits of the case come through.

You were in New York recently to address the city council about your case. You didn’t get to speak to anyone directly at the company, is that right?

No. I mean, despite being subpoenaed, they didn’t turn up. Four of the other frontier AI companies did attend under subpoena. SpaceXAI didn’t, but they eventually did get their hearing with SpaceX in the city council. So, they have now had their opportunity to put questions and indeed did raise my case with them, I understand.

Was it a little enraging to attend a hearing like that where a company that you’re suing doesn't bother turning up?

I think it’s quite rude to the elected members of the New York City Council when you are a business that operates in New York. Indeed, I was told that they were just a few subway stops from City Hall. So I had traveled quite a long way from England to be there. And they couldn’t be bothered to turn up, which yeah, it felt arrogant and a bit of a snub, but they’ve now got there, so that’s a good thing.

Since you made the decision to take this to the courts, there have been a number of proposals in Parliament, notably there was a proposal for a kill switch bill at one point. Now I believe it’s dead or it’s not going very well. Is that right?

So, yes, there have been proposals put forward for a kill switch. And my understanding is the government rejected that. There continues to be strong calls for regulation of particularly superintelligent AI. We’ve heard various theories, whether it’s 2 percent, 10 percent, 20 percent risk of human extinction from this sort of race to superintelligence, a recursive AI, and we wouldn’t allow those margins of risk in any other sphere.

We would not allow it in bridge building or airplane making. They didn’t even allow it when they were testing the nuclear bomb and the Trinity test. It feels to me as though so much of this decision-taking is outside of democratic structures. It’s not with the public. It is being done by some incredibly, exceptionally wealthy companies and organizations who say they have the interests of the planet at heart.

But each time, actually, they’re asked about whether that is the future of humanity or whether they think that these risks could kill humanity, there’s a sort of shrugging of the shoulders and, “We’ll just keep going because we want to make money,” I guess.

It has been reported in the British press that there are members of Parliament, some of whom are working on committees to do with technology, who still don’t really understand what AI is. And clearly you’re not one of them because you’re aware of the dangers of this issue. But do you feel working across parties that you’re still having to explain technology and its dangers and possibilities to people who are making laws, or should be making laws, to protect the public from AI?

Well, look, I don’t think that’s only the case about AI. We are humans after all, and we have to deal with every single subject that exists under the sun. We have to get our heads around things that we’ve never heard of before.

That’s true. But you have to then make laws and decisions. So, I don’t want to be unfair to my colleagues.

You may be a specialist in housing policy and you then need to get your head around AI. But I would say that this is the most important issue in front of anybody in parliament or politics at the moment, and therefore, everyone needs to get their head around AI.

My background is in social policy for women and children. But because we did quite a lot of work on the Online Safety Act when I was working for a children’s charity, we campaigned to get the age verification ban for children in pornography into the OSA. And indeed, created quite a strong piece of legislation in respect of that.

We had to get our heads around online safety on the online space. The problem was, of course, that act didn’t include AI chatbots. There were a number of amendments that tried to get it in and we would’ve been in a much better place had we actually succeeded.

But you know, I’m still not a tech expert even though I had quite a while to try to understand these things. But my view is that this is definitely the new front line of where harms are occurring. We can see that hugely in the increase in AI-generated child sexual abuse material, which is absolutely exploding.

In the end it comes down to the people who create the products. And that’s why we should be seeing digital product regulation in the same way as we would any other product regulation.

I’m very, very hopeful that the government is going to look at it really, really closely because there are always design choices that you can make and that can help to prevent these harms, these harms that are completely knowable. We can predict these harms, and so it should be easy to design them out from the start.

Companies can put in more safeguards. Why don’t they?

Profit. But also, let’s be honest, these are global tools and companies. So unless you have international agreement it is a bit like playing whack-a-mole, isn’t it?

The person who made that horrible video of me being chloroformed lives in the US, so the police aren’t gonna follow it up because they’re out of the country.

I don’t know who they are, but the police did find who they were because it’s a criminal offense what they did. They just weren’t able to bring the charge.

We’ve spoken in the last 40 minutes about the full spectrum of human awfulness. I don’t think there’s any area of it that we haven’t …

I’m really struck by how cheerful you are despite this. How do you remain so upbeat and hopeful while dealing with all of this?

Because you’ve always got to have hope for the future. I do have a really strong conviction that with the right group of people pushing toward a goal, you can achieve that. You don’t have to accept that things will always be like this. When people come together, when communities come together, you can act for change.

We’re just about to introduce our ban on strangulation in pornography, which is another thing that I’ve been campaigning for and introduced an amendment last year, which the government accepted.

There are always things that we can do to change. You just need to keep going. And also I would say that when you work with these terribly dark areas, yes, it can be quite vicariously traumatizing. But actually the people who work within the sectors are some of the most hopeful people, partly because if you lose hope, then it’s really bad. And we can’t because we’re talking about protecting vulnerable children, and we are fighting for women and our rights to be free of this harm.

#165. Microsoft offers buyouts to 60,000 employees amid AI investment

Microsoft is looking to shed a massive number of workers.

In a Monday blog post, the Windows and Xbox maker announced a voluntary buyout plan for its employees in Canada, India, the United Kingdom, and the United States. While the blog post doesn't provide a specific number, reporting from CBS News and other outlets indicates that about 60,000 employees (roughly a quarter of Microsoft's global workforce) will receive the offer. That doesn't mean all 60,000 will accept the offer, but it is a surefire sign that Microsoft feels the need to greatly reduce its human workforce.

"The Voluntary Exit Offer is new for us. We’ll learn from it," Chief People Officer Amy Coleman wrote in the blog post. "At the same time, we’ll keep helping people focus on the work that matters most, build new skills and, where it makes sense, find new opportunities across Microsoft."

As CBS News noted, this comes at the same time Microsoft is expected to spend $175 billion on AI infrastructure in 2026 alone, signaling the company is not exactly lacking in funds at the moment. Whether or not that AI infrastructure will adequately replace human labor remains to be seen.

It hasn't been a good couple of years for Microsoft in terms of generating positive headlines. The company has laid off thousands of workers across divisions, including multiple rounds of cuts at Xbox. That coincides with increasing AI creep in the Windows operating system and new, fairly strict limits on Xbox game streaming, to say nothing of Microsoft's prominent place as a BDS boycott target due to its partnership with the Israeli military.

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

A 5th-generation craftsman in Nishi-Izu shows how katsuobushi is still made with a traditional wood-fired smoke-drying method dating back to the Edo period. The process includes filleting and boiling fresh bonito, repeated smoking, repairs with bonito paste, koji fermentation, and months of sun-drying until the fish becomes hard and glossy.

#163. Databricks co-founder shares his secrets for winning big enterprise customers

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

Startups have never been under more pressure to grow quickly. To do that, many of them must land large enterprises, the deep pockets of the IT world, as customers. But as any company that sells to enterprises will tell you, it’s not easy to win such contracts.

One critical mistake most younger startups make is believing that their tech will practically sell itself, says Arsalan Tavakoli-Shiraji, co-founder and senior vice president of field engineering at Databricks, which helps businesses store and analyze huge amounts of data.

Yes. The tech must work well. But that’s not enough, he warns. Databricks today is an AI and big data storage giant, valued at $190 billion, with more than 70% of the Fortune 500 as customers. But it was once a tiny startup, and Tavakoli-Shiraji was responsible for helping bring in its first big deals.

“Everybody when they’re selling to enterprises at startups … we think it’s all about the tech and having the best tech,” Tavakoli-Shiraji told attendees at TechCrunch Disrupt last week. But he learned the hard way that “people and processes matter the most.”

He remembers securing a meeting with a prospect in the early days, only to discover that it was the same person who signed a mega-deal with his major competitor. “He has no interest in listening to me because he bet his career on that competitor,” Tavakoli-Shiraji said. He also remembers early deals fizzling because the startup wasn’t prepared for legal, contractual, or security questions.

He offered his top tips for startups to win big customers:

When Databricks was young, it tried and failed to score a meeting with UBS, the large Swiss bank. Then it did “a partnership with Microsoft,” becoming an Azure cloud partner. That meant sales agreements would be covered under Microsoft’s terms.

But it also meant that Microsoft’s sales folks actually booked the meetings and advocated for the startup. “All of a sudden, that meeting wasn’t hard to get,” the founder recalled. “It was a huge boon to us that we were able to partner with somebody who had that access and distribution.”

With Microsoft’s sales team helping, “we started getting pulled everywhere.” That created new problems, like prospects wanting features for countries Databricks hadn’t expanded to, but they were good problems to have.

Today, Databricks is the big company that works with startups, selling their products under Databricks’ own terms and cybersecurity setup. Examples include Hightouch, which provides AI-powered marketing and customer-data tools (Databricks has also invested in it); Addepar, which offers data tools for investment professionals; and Tecton, a machine learning startup that Databricks later acquired.

Tavakoli-Shiraji groups enterprises’ main concerns and objections into four categories he calls the four Cs: context, choice, cost, and control. Context means an AI that can be trained “like bringing on a new employee,” he says, learning the company’s terminology, processes, priorities, and so on.

Choice is the freedom to switch AI models rather than be tied to a single provider. Cost is about controlling how much the AI product will cost to run. Control involves the safeguards that govern the AI’s behavior and protecting sensitive information.

Tavakoli-Shiraji jokingly asks CIOs where they stand with AI on a scale of one to five. A one means employees merely have access to ChatGPT, and a five means the company has adopted new AI capabilities that have changed its business processes.

“And everybody loves picking, ‘I’m three leaning towards four.’ But 99% of the ones I talk to are in bucket one and two.” Enterprises still haven’t figured out how to deploy AI agents in ways that create value beyond allowing engineering teams to write code. “It’s going to take many, many years before people can actually properly deploy AI in a very meaningful way in the enterprise.”

One big opportunity for startups to bring that value is to modernize expensive legacy systems. “If you go to any bank today, they still have [an IBM] AS/400 and mainframes,” he described. “Nobody wanted to touch it because nobody knew what that code did.”

But today, AI agents “can go in and understand and actually decompose what that code does.” That, he says, opens the door to products that do everything from adding modern interfaces for those ancient systems to replacing them altogether.

Tavakoli-Shiraji also shared his predictions for the future of enterprise AI adoption, along with an anecdote of what happened when the company was young and he pretended to be a lawyer to land a big enterprise contract. See the full interview below.

#162. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#158. MP calls for rethink of pre-poll voting amid heightened security concerns in wake of Haines incident

Liberal-National MP Garth Hamilton says he has ‘witnessed three interactions between cars and people at pre-poll, two accidental’

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

A Coalition MP has called for a rethink of pre-poll voting at federal elections, including potentially shortening the amount of time booths are open, amid concerns of threats to voters and volunteers.

Security risks to politicians and their staff have been front and centre this week, as parliament returned after the alleged attack on Helen Haines, which left the independent MP in a serious but stable condition after both her legs were amputated.

The LNP member for the Queensland electorate of Groom, Garth Hamilton, said the risk of having members of the public and volunteers lined up at booths near car parks or roads needed to be assessed.

“I’ve had three elections and I’ve witnessed three interactions between cars and people at pre-poll, two accidental, one wasn’t,” Hamilton said on Tuesday.

“The threat is already there, the potential is already there in a heightened environment.”

Pre-polling booths usually open one to two weeks before an election. The Toowoomba-based politician said the length of the pre-polling period, the allocation of resources at centres and the role of postal voting were all measures that could be considered to lower the risk to voters and staff.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

“What we obviously want is not in any way to restrict anybody’s access to democracy, but we also want to make sure that it’s done in a safe way,” Hamilton said. “There’s a reasonable conversation to be had on the shape of pre-poll in Australia, if these threats are increasing, and that’s what we’re hearing from the AFP.”

Australians are increasingly choosing to vote early ahead of polling day. More than 9m votes, or 56%, were cast at early voting centres, according to the Australian Electoral Commission’s 2025 election report.

It is understood Jerome Laxale, a Labor MP who chairs the parliamentary inquiry into the 2025 election, raised safety and his committee’s interim report on the issue at a caucus meeting on Tuesday.

The report recommends the electoral commission reviews the resourcing requirements and duration of pre-poll voting to ensure adequate arrangements are in place, noting the rising number of people opting to cast their ballot early.

The AEC advised the committee that shortening the pre-poll period would not decrease early voting, but would instead “compress the growing demand into a shorter period and increase operational risks, such as queues and shortages of appropriate venues”.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

Laxale, who has represented the NSW seat of Bennelong since 2022, said it was incumbent on the parliament to provide a safe and welcoming atmosphere at a polling booth.

“Democracy doesn’t collapse, it just crumbles,” he said.

“If people go, ‘you know what? I don’t want to deal with that. I’m not going to vote,’ then that has a snowball effect, which will undermine and erode our democratic process. Voting has to be safe and secure.”

Submissions to the inquiry detailed neo-Nazis turning up to booths with speakers, volunteers being bullied and harassed and voters leaving polling centres in tears.

The independent MP Monique Ryan said candidates, volunteers, and campaign workers facing threats of violence should be given clear guidance for how they might engage with federal and state police.

“We need to go back to democracy sausages and bake sales, not voters feeling intimidated and harassed as they approach polling booths,” she said.

#157. UK Home Office May Face Investigation Over Use of OpenAI Tech

A UK government watchdog is considering an investigation into the Home Office’s use of generative AI tools to process asylum applications, WIRED has learned. The potential probe follows lawmakers and researchers publicly questioning the tools’ accuracy and the government’s secrecy around their use.

The Independent Chief Inspector of Borders and Immigration (ICIBI), which monitors the Home Office’s management of borders, immigration, and customs, has asked digital rights nonprofit Open Rights Group (ORG) to share more information on the tools as it draws up plans for the topics it will inspect next year, campaigner Sara Alsherif tells WIRED. ORG’s findings come from a six-month investigation into the tools, which involved 11 parliamentary questions, two ministerial letters, and 10 public records requests that attempt to pry details from the Home Office, which has withheld details on the tools.

The ICIBI tells WIRED it cannot confirm what topics are under active consideration before it publishes its annual inspection priorities, but it says it is aware of concerns about the use of AI tools and pointed to its findings last year that one of the tool’s “effectiveness and reliability were varied.”

“Asylum decisions are made by trained caseworkers, with AI tools used only to assist in navigating interview records and policy information. They cannot be used in isolation and do not make decisions on claims,” a Home Office spokesperson tells WIRED in an email. “We regularly monitor the performance of these tools alongside our established quality-assurance processes, and have already committed to publishing updated evaluations on their roll out.”

The Home Office uses the Asylum Case Summarisation (ACS) and Asylum Policy Search (APS) tools, built on OpenAI’s 2023 GPT-4 large language model and Microsoft Foundry, to summarize transcripts of interviews with asylum applicants and information about their countries of origin, according to documents obtained by ORG. The immigration and law enforcement authority started piloting these tools in late 2024 as the Labour government tried to clear a backlog of around 100,000 asylum cases, while staying on top of the tens of thousands of new applications the UK receives each year. While the department has made significant progress, halving the number of people waiting on an initial decision in the year through June, the number of appeals against decisions has ballooned.

Researchers are concerned about the accuracy of the AI tools–especially to decide something as high-stakes as whether to send someone back to a country where they fear persecution. ORG found that the percentage of summaries generated by the ACS deemed not fit for use—often because they contain factual errors or misrepresentations—is significantly higher than the published 9 percent error rate calculated in the pilot evaluation. There is no quality control for the summaries produced by the APS, it also found.

Asylum decision-makers use the tools to save time when reviewing asylum cases; when used together, the pilot showed the tools could save around an hour per case, according to government estimates. Caseworkers use the ACS to speed up their review of transcripts of interviews where asylum seekers explain how they were persecuted in their country and why they are afraid to return. They use the APS as a search assistant to find and summarize country policy information, which informs them whether the applicant faces a genuine risk of harm in their home country. In July, a UK judge accused the Home Office of citing an “AI hallucinated” document to refuse a Moroccan woman and her child’s asylum claim on the basis that her home country would be safe for her to return to.

“We’re dealing with people’s lives. We’re talking about people that have faced some of the worst circumstances, and we’re meant to be making decisions based on facts and on understanding harm,” Labour backbench MP Bell Ribeiro-Addy tells WIRED. “I just don’t think it’s appropriate that machines are doing that.”

A Home Office spokesperson says, “Use of Artificial Intelligence is just one of the tools at our disposal to protect our borders, and does not replace or automate the role of human decision-makers.”

Ribeiro-Addy, Alsherif, and the Public Law Project, which has separately been investigating the tools, also worry that well-documented “cultural bias” in GPT models could lead to language barriers and the loss of cultural nuances in the summaries, which could in turn result in discriminatory decisions. An OpenAI spokesperson highlighted to WIRED the company’s ongoing work to improve its models’ accuracy and pointed out that the company will be retiring GPT-4 in January.

In the absence of regulation, the UK’s “AI Playbook” mandates that departments must be transparent about the algorithmic tools they use in decision-making processes and make information on them available to the public, including listing those tools on a public repository. The Home Office’s case summarization and policy search tools are not on that list.

Asylum seekers are also not told when these AI tools are used in their case. “Migrant organizations, legal practitioners, not many of them know about the actual use of this tool,” says Alsherif, who leads ORG’s migrant digital justice program. “So we need to let them know about this, let them know how it works, so they can challenge it.” Not being able to contest an incorrect AI summary of their personal information may breach UK data rights laws, Cloisters lawyers wrote in an independent legal opinion commissioned by ORG. The Home Office states that parts of the decision-making process may involve automation in its privacy notice.

Both groups have sent their findings to UK data regulator, the Information Commission, which is currently investigating the Home Office’s refusal to tell ORG how staff use and were trained to use two AI tools.

Public Law Project senior research fellow Caroline Selman also notes that the ACS and APS tools are just two of the 11 high-risk AI systems that her team has found being used across government but absent from the public repository. She’s concerned there may be more deployed across government that even researchers, let alone the individuals they are being used on, are unaware of.

“Other tools are being used but not being publicized at all,” says Selman, “and you can’t even begin to ask questions about things if you don’t know that they’re being used.”

#156. Brazil elections: Merz, world leaders congratulate Lula on his win

Brazilian President Lula secured a surprise win over far-right challenger Flavio Bolsonaro, whose party recognizes Lula's election victory.

It was a tight runoff, with Lula winning 51.7% of the vote against Bolsonaro’s 48.2%, official results showed

In his victory speech, Lula urged more regulation of big tech platforms after allegations that Meta interfered in the race

Bolsonaro refused to concede, but his party recognizes Lula's victory

Trading was suspended on the stock market for half an hour on Monday morning as shares and Brazil's real currency tumbled

Several Latin American leaders congratulated Lula on win

Germany's Merz said he looks forward to 'deepening strategic ties' with Brazil

US President Trump said the vote 'looked terrible,' but added, 'I like Lula'

Keep reading for developments from the second round of Brazil's presidential election on Sunday, October 25, with continued coverage into Monday, October 26.

Tired of missing our real-time updates? Click here to add us as a Preferred Source on Google. Then tap the "Star" or "Preferred" to keep DW News at the top of your feed.

Thank you or obrigado for following our live news blog coverage of the Brazilian presidential election runoff.

We're sure to have more coverage of Brazilian politics, Lula and Flavio Bolsonaro in the coming days and weeks.

Brazil's Liberal Party has acknowledged President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's election victory, breaking with its own candidate Flavio Bolsonaro, who has refused to concede defeat.

"We recognize the presidential election results," party leader Valdemar Costa Neto said in a statement on Monday. "But we believe the issues raised need to be properly clarified."

The statement marks a clear break with Bolsonaro's position. The senator has vowed to seek the annulment of Sunday's runoff result, claiming that a two-hour extension of voting favored Lula.

The extension was ordered due to transport problems in several cities that made it difficult for voters to reach polling stations. Bolsonaro's own campaign had also requested more voting time in some regions because of heavy rainfall.

According to electoral officials, the extension did not change the outcome of the election.

Bolsonaro has yet to publicly respond to his party's statement.

Defeated Brazilian presidential candidate Flavio Bolsonaro says he has been ordered to surrender his passports and is banned from leaving the country.

The right-wing senator said federal police approached him after he visited his imprisoned father, former President Jair Bolsonaro.

"I was ordered right on the street to hand over my passports, and I'm also prohibited from leaving the country," Bolsonaro said in a video posted on social media.

He called the move "a clear attempt to intimidate me" and claimed the order came from Supreme Court Justice Alexandre de Moraes. Neither the court nor federal police immediately confirmed the reported ruling.

Bolsonaro said the decision was based on comments by his brother Eduardo, who had suggested he should leave Brazil if he lost the election. He denied having any plans to flee the country and said he would comply with the order.

Ask, and you shall receive — sometimes anyway! Now a senior US government representative has commented on Brazil's vote.

Leaving Washington for a midterm election campaign rally, President Donald Trump told reporters that it "looked terrible" to him.

"I didn't take sides but it looked like another rigged election frankly... it looked so corrupt, so terrible," he said.

Asked by a reporter if he wanted to "do something about the election," Trump seemed to suggest he did not, albeit repeating his criticism of the mechanics of the vote.

"Well, I'm not running Brazil. I'm running the USA. I just thought that the election was, uh — I like Lula, I get along with him very well — I just think it had a very bad look, the election. The way it was tabulated, you know? The extension given was very strange."

As of 3:20 p.m. in Washington D.C. (1920 GMT), the upper echelons of US government were yet to respond to the election in Brazil on Sunday.

The only October 26 State Department press releases concerned Austria's national day and Secretary of State Marco Rubio's planned trip to Georgia later in the week for a G20 Foreign Ministers' Meeting.

Rubio had not opined on his social media channels and US President Donald Trump's Truth Social account — which only tends to pick up on stories with a tape delay unless they involve Trump directly — had remained inactive since Sunday night.

Lula and his Workers' Party often had strained relations with US governments, especially Republican ones, but this has been particularly pronounced in Trump's case.

Just joining us and wondering how Brazil's runoff vote had a considerably clearer margin of victory than predicted and yet still ended in controversy and the losing candidate refusing to concede defeat?

Challenger Flavio Bolsonaro's objections were focused around the evening extension of voting past the planned closing time of 5 p.m. across the country.

The decisions by a Lula-appointed Supreme Court justice on Sunday followed a complaint over allegations of pro-Bolsonaro mayors being behind public transport irregularities that may have hampered people's plans to vote.

On election night, Bolsonaro then told journalists it was "impossible to recognize the election results, given the circumstances under which things unfolded."

For now it's unclear whether, as the dust settles, Bolsonaro will continue to resist the outcome and try to encourage protests in the way his now-jailed father Jair Bolsonaro did four years ago.

But two factors in particular seem to speak against the prospects of a potential Bolsonaro challenge.

Firstly, his fairly sizeable margin of defeat — losing by slightly more than 4 million votes rather than just a few thousand — makes it much more unlikely that a two-hour extension at polling stations could have even the theoretical potential of swaying the result.

And beyond that, his camp appealed for and successfully secured permission to keep polling stations open in some of Bolsonaro's southern strongholds, after torrential rains interfered with would-be voters' travel plans.

Nigerian President Bola Ahmed Tinubu was one of several African leaders to congratulate Lula on Monday, saying that his renewed mandate offered an opportunity to strengthen bilateral ties.

"As two leading nations of the Global South, we can promote economic integration and advance our shared interests in a rapidly changing global order," Tinubu said online, saying he looked forward to expanding cooperation in a raft of areas.

Tanzanian President Samia Suluhu also extended her "sincere congratulations" to Lula.

"Tanzania values its longstanding friendship with Brazil, and looks forward to continue building on this foundation to further strengthen bilateral relations and deepen cooperation, for the mutual benefit of our two nations," Suluhu said.

Seychelles President Patrick Herminie called the runoff election result "a powerful affirmation of the trust the Brazilian people have placed in President Lula's leadership," in comments published by his office online.

He said Lula's win was "a testament to his lifelong commitment to the dignity and well-being of ordinary men and women," saying the Seychelles shared Lula's values like "social justice, equality of opportunity, the protection of workers and the most vulnerable, and a model of development that places people at its center."

Herminie expressed a desire to deepen bilateral cooperation, particularly in tackling climate change, protecting the oceans and advancing the interests of the Global South.

Russian President Vladimir Putin offered congratulations to Lula in a telegram published by the Kremlin on Monday.

"It is difficult to overstate your personal contribution to strengthening the strategic partnership between Brazil and Russia," the telegram stated.

Putin, a leader of a fellow BRICS alliance member, is one of few world leaders to have worked with Lula across all of his presidential terms, starting in 2003.

Putin also congratulated Lula on his upcoming 81st birthday, which falls on Tuesday.

The Russian president's spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters in Moscow earlier in the day that the Kremlin welcomed Lula's re-election.

Brazilian President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva has won a historic fourth term after defeating far-right rival Flavio Bolsonaro in a runoff election.

It's a historic political comeback for Lula who trailed in the first round.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Trading on the Sao Paulo stock exchange, the largest in Latin America, was suspended for 30 minutes on Monday after plummeting nearly 17% in the wake of the re-election of left-wing Brazilian President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva, who defeated conservative Flavio Bolsonaro.

The sharp drop in the benchmark Ibovespa index triggered a 30-minute "circuit breaker" mechanism provided for "periods of sharp market decline," according to a stock exchange statement.

Meanwhile, the US dollar surged 12% against Brazil's currency, one of the real's biggest ‌daily moves on record.

Prices of raw sugar, one of Brazil's most important exports, slid nearly 5% on the collapsing exchange rate.

Prediction markets had indicated a Bolsonaro win but the right-wing senator's refusal to concede defeat and ​his pledge to challenge the election result have added to investor uncertainty.

"Important for local financial markets will be how quickly Bolsonaro accepts the result, or at least allows the constitution to run its course," Chris Turner, a strategist at ING in London, told the Reuters news agency as attention turned to Lula's choice of finance minister — a clue as to his fiscal approach.

"The key signal for markets will ⁠be the ​next Lula administration's commitment to fiscal discipline, starting with the appointment of a new finance minister in coming weeks," RBC BlueBay Asset Management's Graham ​Stock told Reuters.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz has joined the chorus of world leaders  congratulating Brazilian President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva on his re-election.

"I look forward to further deepening the strategic partnership between Germany and Brazil together," Merz said in a message posted on X.

Germany and Brazil held government consultations in Hannover in April of this year, at which Merz and Lula agreed to cooperate more closely on defense and raw materials, among other things.

Brazilian markets are bracing for heavy selling on Monday, with futures of Brazil's benchmark stock index ‌Bovespa ⁠falling ⁠nearly 15% in early Monday ​trading

On global markets, the iShares MSCI Brazil ETF, which tracks a basket of major Brazilian stocks, plunged as much as 26% in premarket trading as investors unwind bets on a right-wing victory.

Brazilian Real November futures also fell 10% in Asian trading, while US-listed shares of Brazilian stocks dropped as well, with energy giant Petrobras plunging more than 20% and banking group Itau Unibanco cratering almost 30%.

Investors had seen election loser Flavio Bolsonaro as the candidate most likely to boost markets with fiscal austerity and deregulation, while Lula has pursued policies of redistribution and state-led development, including costly welfare programs.

The Kremlin said on Monday it welcomed Lula's victory and praised what it said were his efforts to cultivate Brazil-Russia ties.

"We welcome Mr. Lula's victory. He does a lot, not only for the development of bilateral relations but also for advancing processes within the framework of BRICS," Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told reporters.

He said Russia hoped for more of the same.

Lula himself has possibly put Brazil in the Kremlin's good books by not joining Western sanctions on Russia over Moscow's invasion of Ukraine and calling for a negotiated peace.

However, he has called on all nations to adhere to the principles of sovereignty and territorial integrity enshrined in the UN Charter, thus implicitly condemning Russia's aggression.

Brazil and Russia are both members of BRICS, an international cooperation group founded in 2009 along with India and China, with South Africa joining a year later, and taking all those countries' first initials to make up its name.

It has since then been expanded to include 10 member nations in all, with a number of "partner countries" also invited in 2024.

Messages of congratulations continue to rain in from around the world following Lula's unexpected win.

China's response was measured, with Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun saying that "China has noted that Brazil's Superior Electoral Court has announced President Lula has won the second round of the Brazilian general election, and expresses its congratulations."

Guo added that Beijing was "willing to work with Brazil to continue advancing the building of a China-Brazil community with a shared future."

French President Emmanuel Macron, after expressing his congratulations, said Brazil and France should work to "strengthen our strategic partnership."

"France and Brazil have much to build together," Macron said X, adding that the two countries should nurture their friendship "in the service of a fairer world."

Spain's prime minister, Pedro Sanchez, was more effusive, casting Lula's victory as evidence that the world is experiencing a backlash to its seeming rightwards political trajectory at present.

"A great victory for Lula! What has happened in Brazil will happen in Spain. The global progressive comeback has just begun," Sanchez, who has called snap elections in November, posted on X.

#151. Cohere says Alibaba’s Qwen model tried to send a file to Beijing

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

Canadian AI company Cohere said on Monday that it found one of Alibaba’s newest AI models, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, trying to upload a file to a server in Beijing during a routine cybersecurity test, even though the model had said in its own notes that it wouldn’t use any tools.

The incident initially raised fears that the model had undermined a fundamental promise to businesses — that self-hosting an open-weight model keeps data in-house. But those early fears of corporate espionage were short-lived. Researchers quickly dismissed the threat, saying it was likely a by-product of messy training environments that cause models to unexpectedly act outside their instructions rather than a deliberate attempt to secretly send sensitive information to China.

The incident began on Friday during an evaluation in which the Qwen model was asked to write a rule for catching malware downloads. According to a blog post from Cohere, the model’s written reasoning explicitly stated it would not use any external tools. Yet in the very next step, it attempted to send a test string (a short piece of placeholder text) to an external server before being blocked by a security sandbox.

Cohere said it could not tell whether the behavior was deliberate or a leftover from its training.

Asked about the debate over the model’s intent, Cohere co-founder Ivan Zhang told TechCrunch that Cohere has seen its own models pick up strange habits in training simulators.

“It’s really hard to assign intention or motivation. We’ve read our own model traces, we’ve seen reward hacks, and sometimes these models get really creative when it comes to trying to break out their sandboxes,” Zhang said. (Reward hacks are shortcuts a model finds to earn credit without doing a task as intended.)

To teach an AI model to act as an agent that takes actions on its own, companies like Alibaba train it in thousands of practice setups, sort of like flight simulators for software. The model is rewarded every time it finishes a task in a simulator, and it picks up habits along the way. If these practice setups routinely stored files on Alibaba’s cloud servers, models could learn to save files there by default.

Critics also argued that the incident could have been caused by the way Cohere investigated the behavior, steering the model toward it instead of measuring how often Qwen does it on its own.

To find what triggered the upload attempt, Cohere supplied the beginning of a network command (an instruction telling one computer to connect to another) and let the model finish it. Because it processed thousands of routine network commands during its training, the model acted like a highly advanced autocomplete — often filling in web addresses, some of them for Alibaba’s servers.

Cohere said the original upload attempt happened without any such help, but that the behavior is rare.

After developers asked for more evidence, Cohere released the raw traces from its tests on Monday evening on the AI developer platform Hugging Face, along with a script so others could try to reproduce the behavior.

One initial skeptic, after going through the traces, rated the evidence as strong that Qwen can invent suspicious web addresses, but found no evidence that Alibaba planted a backdoor or that Qwen was deliberately hiding its intent.

The incident lands as governments are deciding how far to push back on Chinese AI. Last week, the Department of Homeland Security ordered federal civilian agencies to remove the Chinese AI developer DeepSeek’s products from their systems.

In its blog post, Cohere called on governments to clarify whether their restrictions on Chinese AI also apply to models running on government computers.

“I’m not asking for full regulation of these open-weights models,” Zhang told TechCrunch, adding that companies should treat open models like any program downloaded from the internet.

Cohere, which sells its own AI models to governments, stands to benefit if officials grow warier of free Chinese alternatives.

#149. Australian politics live: inflation expected to rise to 4.2%; eSafety head’s warning about women in senior roles

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Australia’s eSafety commissioner, Julie Inman Grant, said she and her family had been subjected to an onslaught of online abuse and threats in the 10 years she has been in the role.

In her final appearance before Senate estimates last night, Inman Grant it was a “sensitive and pivotal moment” for the parliament in the wake of the alleged attack on Helen Haines, and the environment was “dangerously febrile for women in senior government roles.”

double quotation markWhen public officials are used for political point scoring, a target goes on our backs and we are subjected to an onslaught of online abuse and threats.

As many of you know, this has been the normal state of affairs for me and my family for years, yet somehow this is meant to be accepted as part of public service.

Inman Grant did not name any people who have targeted her personally, but her work in the role has seen her labelled as a “censorship commissar” by the world’s richest person, Elon Musk. US Republican congressman Jim Jordan labelled Inman Grant as a “zealot for global takedowns”.

Inman Grant said more needs to be done for the next generation of leaders to feel safe in online spaces or public-facing roles.

double quotation markSocial media is no longer the once envisioned digital town square – it has become more like a gladiatorial arena.

Meanwhile, platforms continue to roll back abuse and violent speech policies that let some of the most egregious offenders out of digital jail.

The Greens leader, David Shoebridge, will address the National Press Club in Canberra today, promising to help Australians struggling with cost-of-living and answering questions about allegations against senator Jordon Steele-John.

He will propose lifting the tax-free threshold from $18,200 to $26,000, meaning people earning a wage won’t pay tax on their first $26,000. He says that will save the average working Australian more than $1,000.

The Greens are also proposing a $1,000 annual boost for people on income support, including pensioners, carers, students, and job seekers.

double quotation markNo matter who you are, whether you’re working, studying, looking for work or on a pension, you’ll get more than $1,000.

Costings for the Greens from the Parliamentary Budget Office show the plan would cost about $22bn a year, with Shoebridge proposing to pay for it through higher taxes on gas exports and a market-based tax on resources companies.

Steele-John is on leave from parliament amid an investigation into allegations about his conduct towards women. He has denied the allegations.

Official data released at 11:30am are expected to show inflation accelerated to 4.2% in the year to September, from 4% in the month before, as rising fuel costs amid a worsening Middle East conflict compound cost of living pressures.

Look out for the Reserve Bank’s preferred gauge of underlying inflation, which removes the most volatile prices. That measure is predicted to hold at 3.6% in the year to the September quarter, economists say, but anything higher will virtually guarantee a fifth interest rate hike next Tuesday.

The RBA may feel the need to hike regardless, as recent comments from top central bank officials suggest they are more worried about high inflation becoming entrenched in the system than the rising rate of unemployment.

Financial markets are pricing in about a 65% chance of a Melbourne Cup day rate rise.

The latest inflation report also lands in the middle of a parliamentary sitting week, and the opposition will be quick to use the figures to attack Labor for its management of the economy.

Rising unemployment and accelerating inflation is not a situation anyone is comfortable with. But a more benign set of numbers today will take the heat off mortgaged households, the Reserve Bank and the Albanese government.
