#139. Xi Jinping’s corruption crackdown in the spotlight as China prepares for meeting of top leaders

The fifth plenum comes against a backdrop of years of purges that have had some analysts wondering if the issue is corruption or a lack of loyalty

When Xi Jinping came to power in 2012, he pledged to stamp out corruption all through the ranks of the Communist party. More than a decade later, the pace of investigations has not slowed. Last year, China’s disciplinary authorities opened investigations into more than 1 million people, including more than 100 officials at or above ministerial or provincial level.

As Chinese Communist party (CCP) officials kick off an important meeting this week, discipline – and party loyalty – is high on the agenda. For those who try to read the tea leaves of elite Chinese politics, concern about the apparent lack of discipline is dominating CCP discussions.

The “fifth plenum” began on Monday and is set to focus on “major issues on advancing the party’s full and rigorous self-governance”, according to a Chinese state media. In party-speak, “governance” is a byword for discipline, loyalty and anti-corruption.

As Xi continues to crack down on corruption, the fact that investigations nearly always result in a conviction begs the question over whether targets are truly guilty of corruption, or are they just political rivals to the people in charge.

Kerry Brown, director of the Lau China Institute at King’s College London, explained that in Xi’s worldview, which is obsessed with discipline and loyalty, there may not be much difference between the two. “If you’re corrupt, you’re not politically loyal. If you’re politically loyal, you’re not corrupt,” Brown said.

He added that in the CCP system, “corruption is not just material things”, but also “spiritual corruption”, which includes political disloyalty.

Some analysts argue that the reason that the anti-graft drive is so enduring is because corruption is a feature rather than a bug of an authoritarian, top-down political system with a sprawling bureaucracy that governs 1.4 billion people.

Neil Thomas, a senior fellow at the Asia Society thinktank, said: “Xi has made the party less corrupt and strengthened his control, but he knows that tougher punishment cannot fully resolve the problem of officials putting their own interests ahead of the party’s.

“Xi’s answer is to keep tightening discipline, making anti-corruption a permanent method of governing with no obvious point at which he can declare the job done,” Thomas said.

The job may be never ending, but the plenum is an opportunity to refocus the party’s energy on discipline. The fifth plenum is the fifth of seven gatherings that normally happen in every five-year cycle between CCP congresses. The plenums bring together more than 300 members of the CCP’s Central Committee, the most senior officials in the party hierarchy.

The meeting is particularly significant because it is likely to be the last substantive meeting before next year’s 21st Party Congress, where a new Central Committee will be chosen, and where Xi is expected to be anointed for a fourth term as the head of the CCP.

Eliminating anyone who poses a risk to his authority or to the party’s credibility is a key priority for Xi. And potential threats are everywhere. Nearly 15 years after Xi took office, according to CCP proclamations and a host of recent investigations, the problem is still not stamped out. “It is a deeply ingrained part of [Xi’s] worldview,” said Brown. “Everyone else is corrupt”.

In the past, Xi has warned that the party could “lose its ruling status and inevitably be eliminated by history” if it could not get its house in order. “This is by no means an exaggeration,” he said.

The current focus on party governance comes amid a host of high-profile investigations against senior party and military leaders.

In January, Zhang Youxia, China’s top general and a man second only to Xi in the military hierarchy, was placed under investigation; he was expelled from party last month, having already been removed from the military’s top ruling body, the Central Military Commission (CMC)

A host of other purges means that the CMC, which normally has seven members, has been reduced to just two: Xi and Zhang Shengmin, the CMC’s head of discipline.

Other recent scalps include Ma Xingrui, the former party secretary for Xinjiang, and Huang Qifan, the former mayor of Chongqing.

Huang had long been known as the ultimate survivor in Chinese politics because of his connections to the scandals in the early 2010s in which Xi’s political rival, Bo Xilai, the former Party boss for Chongqing, was embroiled in the death of the British businessman Neil Heywood. Bo was ultimately convicted of bribery and embezzlement, for which he is serving a life sentence, but Huang survived. No longer.

Some China analysts have asked whether the recent purges, which have reached the highest echelons of the Party and military ranks, indicate instability in the regime. But Xi likely sees it differently. Corruption investigations demonstrate who is in charge. Plus, Brown added, they are “the most easy and natural tool by which to enforce unity and discipline”.

#138. Quince’s Merino Wool Tee Doesn’t Cost a Fortune

I've been singing the praises of merino wool in these pages for over five years. Most people have come around to the fact that merino wool is a super fiber; it's both sustainable and quite remarkable in its capabilities. Merino wool somehow manages to be comfortable in 95-degree heat, and it'll insulate you against below-freezing temperatures. Unlike synthetic fibers derived from petroleum, merino wool resists odors and is naturally breathable. What's not to love? The price.

Merino wool isn't cheap. That's why I love these merino T-shirts from Quince: They're just $42.

The Quince All-Season T-shirt is 100 percent extra-fine merino wool with a 165-gsm weave, which is perfect even on the warmest summer days where I live. (Admittedly, I think the hottest it got this summer was 92 Fahrenheit). I like the relaxed fit here as well. It's neither skintight technical nor overly loose and baggy. It fits like a T-shirt is supposed to. That's what Quince does well in a number of categories: offer a solid, no-nonsense, competitively priced merino wool T-shirt that fits well and feels great. As an added bonus, Quince offers free shipping and free returns for 365 days. I haven't actually tested either—I haven't needed to, my Quince T-shirt is still going strong after two years of use. But there are plenty of customer reviews on Reddit suggesting the company does honor that offer.

Not sold on the idea of a wool T-shirt? I understand. When I started testing merino, I too had visions of lumberjacks in plaid and itchy sweaters someone's grandma had knitted. None of that is merino wool. Merino sheep have thinner, softer wool, which has evolved to keep them comfortable across a wide range of temperatures and is comfortable to wear next to your skin.

One wool myth that's not just a myth is care. Merino isn't cotton. Quince's care instructions for the All Season T-shirt say to wash it in cold water and lay flat to dry. The latter is important, as hanging merino wool to dry will stretch it out (because of the water weight). Can you throw it in the dryer? Yes. I have, for testing purposes only. It will shrink, and it will wear it out much, much faster, but you can do it.

#137. Police in northern Germany search for suspects after car ramming overnight injures several

A person intentionally drove into a group of people in a northern German town, police have said. Three people have been detained in what police said was likely a gang confrontation.

A BMW was driven ​deliberately ‌into a group of people, leaving ‌four to five of ‌them ​with some serious but not life-threatening injuries in Neustadt am Rübenberge late on Monday night, police said on Tuesday.

A police patrol making routine traffic checks in town near Hannover in the state of Lower Saxony, intervened, shooting at the car to stop it around 11:30 p.m. local time (2230 UTC/GMT)

"The officers witnessed the incident as it happened and opened fire on the vehicle after it had been driven into the group of people," the spokesperson said.

Several suspects had fled the scene of the ramming on foot, police had said.

"The sequence of events and the exact roles of those involved are still under investigation," the spokesperson said, adding that forensic analysis of the cars and ramming scene was ongoing.

A reporter for the German DPA news agency described several damaged cars, with flat tires, a smashed rear window and bullet holes.

#131. Jay-Z to perform at Super Bowl half-time show

Rapper Jay-Z will perform at the Super Bowl half-time show in February, the NFL has announced.

Jay-Z's company Roc Nation has produced the half-time show since 2019, and he plays a key role in selecting each year's performer but he has not performed before at the NFL championship game.

"Anticipation as to who Jay-Z might have guest star at halftime for the big game will now kick off," the NFL said in a statement. "Of course, his wife, BeyoncÃ©, will be atop the list."

Next year's Super Bowl LXI will take place on 14 February near Los Angeles.

The hip-hop star will follow in the footsteps of other recent half-time performers including Bad Bunny, Kendrick Lamar, Usher, Rihanna and The Weeknd.

The musical performance draws global attention while the sport is watched by tens of millions of people in the US.

Kendrick Lamar's 2025 performance was the most watched Super Bowl half-time show in history and won the Emmy award for outstanding music direction.

The announcement was made after Jay-Z, whose real name is Shawn Carter, performed two shows last weekend at the Los Angeles stadium where the Super Bowl will be played.

The LA shows were part of a tour to celebrate the 30th anniversary of his debut album Reasonable Doubt.

"From 'Reasonable Doubt' to standing on the Super Bowl halftime stage, this has been a journey," said Jay-Z.

"Thirty years of music, work, and culture on the world's biggest stage. This run continues to be a dream come true."

NFL commissioner Roger Goodell said he "couldn't be more excited for fans around the world to experience the artistry, creativity and cultural impact he will bring".

"When we first reached an agreement with Roc Nation to produce the show, I had just one request: that Jay-Z take the stage himself one day, whenever the moment felt right to him," Goodell said.

The star's accolades include being the first rapper inducted into the Songwriters Hall of Fame, 25 Grammy awards and being inducted into the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame in 2021.

#130. ‘Zero-energy companionship’: bizarre hobby takes seed among China’s stressed young adults

Faced with a stagnant jobs market, many are spurning children or pets in favour of ‘raising bricks’

Young adults in China looking for a cheaper, low-maintenance alternative to having children or pets have found an answer: raising bricks.

Bricks may not be as cuddly as cats or as life-affirming as human offspring, but they also cost next to nothing. “No cats, no dating, I started raising a brick,” wrote one brick parent on social media.

Raising “brick babies” is easier than caring for house plants, involving spraying them with water a few times a day then waiting for moss spores already present in the atmosphere to attach themselves and grow. One Chinese media article described the bricks as being “practically immortal”. “If you forget to water them, the moss goes dormant. If you remember to water them again, the greenery returns.”

A hashtag related to the trend has attracted more than 5m views on the Chinese social media platform Weibo.

Chinese commentators have linked the trend to young people looking for low-effort routes to finding meaning in life. One brick “parent” told Chinese media the hobby created “a sense of accomplishment no less than completing a long-term project”.

One user on Xiaohongshu, an Instagram-like platform, also known as Rednote, posted a picture of their rusty-coloured brick, saying “no output required, no KPIs to hit, no need to beg it to bloom [and] no comparing its growth with others”.

Another user wrote: “My friends asked if I’d lost my mind. I said no, I’m just starting to raise a brick.

“A brick gives me a sense of certainty that feels way more grounded than a lot of relationships. Psychologists call this ‘zero-energy emotional companionship.’ It fulfils your need for company without demanding any obligation to give love in return.”

Young people in China are increasingly shunning the traditional expectations of society, such as having children and working long hours in strenuous jobs, assuming they can find one in the first place. Nearly one in five 16- to 24-year-olds, excluding students, are unemployed, according to official statistics. Some estimates put the unemployment rate much higher.

China’s birthrate is in decline and fell to a record low last year. Faced with a sluggish job market after years of intensely competitive studying, many young people are choosing to shun the rat race and opt instead for “lying flat” – pursuing a low-effort, low-stress lifestyle; one well-suited to raising bricks.

The trend has become so popular that Chinese media have started to warn about the health risks.

Cultivating moss could lead to the spread of mould, with some “brick parents” reportedly complaining of itchy eyes and runny noses. Xuedong Li, a retired professor of moss from Beijing’s Capital Normal University, urged people to keep their bricks in an enclosed environment. “It is best to wear disposable gloves and a mask during handling to reduce spore inhalation and skin contact,” Li told Chinese media.

→ Baca artikel asli

#129. Early Black Friday Deals 2026

While the Friday after Thanksgiving has traditionally been the busiest shopping day of the year, Black Friday deals have gradually spread well beyond the day. First came Cyber Monday, then Cyber Week. Now, early Black Friday deals kick off before November has even started. The tricky part for early birds is knowing what’s a genuine deal and what’s likely to go even lower when Black Friday arrives.

The Reviews team at WIRED tracks prices on our favorite gadgets year-round, and we only highlight deals on devices we have tested that we’d be comfortable recommending to friends and family. Some of these picks may be cheaper for Black Friday, but these are the discounts that have caught our eye so far.

The best trackers for anyone with an iPhone; you can use them to keep tabs on your keys, luggage, bike, or whatever else you worry might go missing. Before you pull the trigger, they will likely drop by at least another $30 for Black Friday.

They are comfy, boast top-notch active noise cancellation, and can even translate some languages in real-time. That said, they are at this price often enough that I hesitate to call it a great deal, and they will likely drop by another $20 for Black Friday.

I immediately like it more than all my old faceless chargers. The charging pad supports Qi2/MagSafe and tilts up to 70 degrees to reveal a second pad to charge AirPods or other wireless earbuds. #128. Two men face UK national security charges following an incident near RAF Fairford, with police suspecting Iranian state involvement

British prosecutors have charged two men over an incident near RAF Fairford in September. Police suspect Iranian involvement in the case at a base used for US strikes on Iran.

British prosecutors said Tuesday that two men had been charged under the National Security Act in connection with a September incident near RAF Fairford, an airbase used by US forces to launch strikes on Iran.

US bombers were withdrawn from Fairford on security grounds shortly after the arrests immediately after the incident.

The Crown Prosecution Service charged Mohmed A., 24, of London, with engaging in conduct likely to materially assist a foreign intelligence service. Counterterrorism police chief Laurence Taylor said investigators believed the country involved was Iran.

Nouman E., 24, also of London, was charged with entering a prohibited place for a purpose prejudicial to the UK on August 22. Both men were due to appear in court later Tuesday.

Nouman E. was initially arrested near the Gloucestershire base on September 27 and rearrested on October 20.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A separate suspect, a 25-year-old British-Iranian dual national also arrested on October 20, was released without charge over the suspected intelligence offense. He remains on bail following an October 1 arrest on suspicion of preparing terrorist acts. Five other men also remain on police bail.

Armed police responded to reports of three suspicious vehicles near the base on September 27 and arrested five men. Scores of residents were evacuated as police and military bomb-disposal experts searched the area for explosives.

Police have said they consider Iranian state involvement "highly likely."

"We have seen a range of incidents in recent months that we believe have been directed by the Iranian state," said Laurence Taylor, head of UK counterterrorism policing.

He said police were working with MI5, Britain's domestic intelligence service, to counter the threat and urged the public to report concerns.

Iran has denied involvement, although it has previously said it considers bases used to attack its territory legitimate targets.

#125. Nvidia backs Indian startup helping companies run AI models with fewer GPUs

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW.

As the AI boom strains computing capacity, Nvidia has backed Indian startup Simplismart in a $14 million funding round, betting on software that helps companies run AI models more efficiently and, in some cases, with fewer GPUs.

Nvidia participated in the all-primary Series B round alongside Accel, Shastra VC, and Micromax, with Dallas Venture Capital leading the investment. The deal values the four-year-old startup at about $100 million, including the new funding.

Companies are nowadays spending heavily on GPUs to power AI applications, but running those models reliably and economically at scale remains a challenge. Different workloads demand distinct trade-offs between speed, cost, and performance. Deploying and operating AI models to meet business requirements often also requires complex adjustments. Simplismart says its software automates much of that work, tailoring how models run on existing computing infrastructure instead of requiring customers to manage those configurations themselves.

“Nobody actually needs the fastest or the cheapest inference,” Amritanshu Jain (pictured above, left), co-founder and CEO of Simplismart, said in an interview. He added that companies instead need to run AI models at speeds and costs that make sense for their specific applications.

Simplismart’s software sits between AI models and the computing infrastructure they run on and adjusts how models are deployed and managed to meet customers’ requirements. It says it can optimize factors such as how quickly a model responds, how many requests it handles, and how much computing power it consumes, helping companies get more out of their existing GPU stack.

The Bengaluru-based startup offers companies a way to deploy and run AI models through its own platform or within their existing cloud and on-premises infrastructure. It also provides tools to fine-tune models, automatically scale computing resources as demand changes, and monitor performance. Additionally, Simplismart allows cloud providers to build and operate their own AI inference services on GPU infrastructure using its software.

Simplismart says it helped AI video-generation platform InVideo cut its GPU serving costs by 56% using its software. The startup also works with AI video companies HeyGen and Higgsfield, as well as enterprise AI startup Ema, among others. Moreover, Indian cloud provider Yotta uses Simplismart’s technology to power the serverless AI inference service within its Shakti Studio platform.

Simplismart’s annualized revenue run rate has grown 14-fold over the past six months to an eight-figure sum, Jain told TechCrunch. The figure is based on recent customer usage rather than contracted recurring revenue and excludes revenue passed through to infrastructure providers, the company said. He did not disclose the exact figure.

More than half of Simplismart’s revenue comes from the U.S., despite the startup having more customers in India, Jain said.

Alongside its investment, Nvidia has been working with Simplismart on technical integrations, including around its Dynamo inference software, Jain told TechCrunch. The collaboration involves Simplismart’s engineers and Nvidia’s solutions architects, while Nvidia has also helped introduce the startup to potential customers and partners, Jain stated.

Interestingly, the investment presents an apparent paradox as Nvidia, the dominant supplier of AI chips, is backing software that can help companies run some workloads with fewer GPUs. Jain, however, argued that making AI inference more efficient could ultimately benefit Nvidia by making its hardware more attractive to customers and encouraging wider adoption of AI applications.

Simplismart is serving customers in an increasingly crowded market for AI inference software, competing with offerings from Nvidia itself, as well as major cloud providers and hyperscalers including Amazon Web Services, Google Cloud, and Microsoft Azure. A growing number of startups are also developing tools to make AI models faster and cheaper to run as there is growing demand in this segment, driven by rising demand for AI applications and the need to make better use of expensive GPU resources.

“Where we tend to win is where people have to be more stringent on their SLAs [service-level agreements] and TCO [total cost of ownership],” Jain said, referring to demanding performance requirements and tight cost constraints. Unlike rivals that offer preset options for faster or cheaper inference, Simplismart aims to find a balance tailored to each customer’s needs, he said.

Founded in 2022 by former Oracle engineer Jain and former Google engineer Devansh Ghatak (pictured above, right), Simplismart grew out of the pair’s experience building and deploying machine-learning systems. The two met while studying at BITS Pilani, and the startup now employs 58 people, Jain said.

Simplismart plans to use the new funding to expand its presence in the U.S., grow its team, and invest further in developing its inference software. Jain told TechCrunch that the U.S. remains a key market for the startup as it looks to build on its existing customer base there.

The funding announcement follows a report by Indian English daily The Economic Times that Simplismart was in talks to raise $40 million to $50 million at a valuation of $300 million to $400 million. Asked about the report, Jain declined to comment, saying the startup was not actively fundraising. He added that the Series B was closed and would not be extended.

#123. How to watch Verity: When and where the latest Colleen Hoover adaptation is streaming

The latest Colleen Hoover adaptation has arrived with a bang. After grossing $162 million in theaters, psychological thriller Verity is making its way into living rooms just in time for Halloween.

Adapted from Colleen Hoover's extremely popular 2018 novel of the same name, Verity stars Dakota Johnson and Anne Hathaway in what Mashable's entertainment editor calls her "wildest performance of 2026."

Critic reviews are mostly harsh, with the film only holding a 31% on Rotten Tomatoes, but audiences are loving the adaptation, leading to its box office success. Whether you're a Colleen Hoover fan waiting to compare the book with the film or you're just seeking a fun psycho-sexual thriller to watch this season, Verity is now available to watch at home. Here's everything you need to know.

Verity follows writer Lowen Ashleigh (Johnson) after she's hired as a ghostwriter by best-selling author Verity Crawford (Hathaway) to complete her book series. While staying at the Crawford home, Lowen discovers a disturbing manuscript revealing some of Verity's darkest secrets.

As of Oct. 27, you can officially watch Verity at home one of two ways: rent it or buy it. A streaming option is still to come at an unknown date. See the details below.

Verity will make its at-home debut on digital retail sites like Prime Video and Fandango at Home on Oct. 27, 2026. You can buy it forever at any of the retailers below or rent it for 30 days. If you choose the rental option, just note that once you start watching, you'll only have 48 hours to finish.

Prime Video — buy for $24.99, rent for $19.99

Apple TV — buy for $24.99, rent for $19.99

Fandango at Home — buy for $24.99, rent for $19.99

While no official streaming date has been announced yet, we know that as an Amazon MGM Studios film, Verity will likely make its streaming debut on Amazon's niche streaming service MGM+.

Previous Amazon MGM Studios films like Is God Is and Project Hail Mary graced MGM+ first before finally expanding to Prime Video. It is likely that as a box office success, Verity will follow the same formula.

The good news is that you likely won't have to wait too long. We'll update this story with new streaming information once Amazon MGM Studios makes the announcement.

If you're new to MGM+, then you can score a seven-day trial and watch Verity for free once it's released. If not, there are a couple of ways to save on a subscription.

On any given day, the annual MGM+ subscription plan will save you about 35% or nearly $34. It costs $61.99 for a full year of streaming, which breaks down to $5.17 per month instead of the usual $7.99 per month. You'll have to pay up front, but the savings are ultimately worth it.

The current deal gets you two months of MGM+ for just $1.99 per month, but it's set to expire on Oct. 28. There's a good chance another discount will pop up around Black Friday, so stay tuned. #122. Ex-FBI chief James Comey faces federal charges for third time

Watch: "I'm still innocent" - James Comey responds to new charges

Federal prosecutors have taken steps to charge ex-Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) Director James Comey with leaking national defence information, according to a court filing.

It marks the third time the justice department has moved to bring criminal charges against Comey, a longtime critic of President Donald Trump.

Few details are provided about the two counts in the three-page filing, but Comey's lawyers said in separate court papers that the case stemmed from an alleged disclosure of classified information to the New York Times in October 2016, while he was head of the FBI.

Comey denied any wrongdoing in a video posted on Substack on Monday, calling the charges an "appalling abuse".

"It would almost be funny if it weren't such an appalling abuse of the department of justice, one that we cannot become numb to," he said in the clip.

"Nothing's changed with me - I'm still innocent, I'm still not afraid of these people and I still believe in the independent federal judiciary."

Comey was charged in Washington DC through a criminal information, which is a formal written charging document filed directly by a prosecutor.

This is different to a criminal indictment, which must be approved by a grand jury, a group of citizens sworn in to review if evidence is strong enough to prosecute.

Comey's lawyer, Patrick Fitzgerald, said in a statement that two different teams of prosecutors had investigated the alleged disclosure to the New York Times a decade ago during the first Trump administration.

"Both times the Government determined that no charges should be brought against anyone," he said.

The attorney said the court filing "purports to charge Mr Comey with crimes that he did not commit".

His legal team has filed a motion to dismiss the charges.

Former federal prosecutor Glenn Kirschner told the BBC that the case may have exceeded the 10-year statute of limitations, giving the motion to dismiss the charges "a very good chance of being granted".

Kirschner said it seemed like the charges against Comey were a "revenge-fuelled attempt by Donald Trump to have his Department of Justice find something that might stick".

Jessica Levinson, law professor at Loyola University in Los Angeles, said that because the charges were still under seal, the details about what federal prosecutors are alleging were slim.

"We do have some examples of the Trump administration and the department of justice going after a variety of people who are his political enemies; we have very different outcomes in some of these cases, though," she told the BBC World Service's Newsday programme.

While other cases involving Comey have not moved forward, cases brought against other Trump adversaries have moved forward, Levinson noted.

Comey was fired by Trump during his first term, after the former FBI director opened an investigation into claims of Russian meddling in the US 2016 presidential election.

Since then, Trump has repeatedly called for his prosecution, and these charges are the administration's third attempt to do so.

Comey was first indicted by a federal grand jury in September 2025 on charges that he had lied to Congress during testimony and obstructed a congressional proceeding.

He pleaded not guilty the following month before the case was dismissed in November.

In April 2026, Comey surrendered to authorities to face charges alleging that an image he briefly shared on social media posed a threat to Trump's life.

The case stemmed from a 2025 Instagram post by Comey, which contained a photo of seashells on a beach arranged to read "86 47".

"Eighty-six" is a slang term for "get rid of", and prosecutors allege it encourages violence against Trump, the 47th president.

Comey denies any wrongdoing, saying he did not know what the numbers meant, and accusing the prosecution of political motivation. That case is ongoing.

The latest charges against Comey were made public the same day a federal judge ruled the justice department illegally fired his daughter, Maurene Comey.

Maurene Comey, who lost her job as a federal prosecutor in New York in July 2025, sued the government, arguing she was unlawfully fired because of her father and because of her perceived political affiliations.

The judge ruled on Monday that the Trump administration must reinstate her and that she must be given back pay.
